Fixes: changed the interferer's PCI, tilt 2° → 4°, HO retune, ANR clean-up, capped PRB use per UE to protect others, scheduler tuning and 256QAM enabled, backhaul checked. Takeaway: good RSRP + bad SINR/CQI + high PRB = interference plus load, not coverage.
Level 3 — definitions, NSA/VoLTE/latency specifics, answer structure
Drop-rate formulas — watch the definitions. Drop rate = abnormal releases ÷ successful setups (E-RAB/QoS-flow or RRC). "(attempts − successes) ÷ attempts" is a setup failure rate, not a drop rate; some notes mislabel it. CSSR in LTE is usually the product RRC setup SR × E-RAB setup SR (×S1 signalling SR in some vendors).
NSA-specific checks (why users "don't get 5G"): B1 threshold and NR measurement configuration (and measurement gaps if the UE needs them), NR neighbour/priority on the anchor, X2 between eNB and gNB up, SgNB addition success and SgNB release causes (NN02), UE band-combination support, NR-leg SINR. A too-high B1 threshold looks like "no 5G coverage" even where NR RSRP is fine.
VoLTE-specific checks: SIP failures (IMS side) vs QCI 1 bearer setup failures (RAN) vs drops (RF/HO/SRVCC). MOS problems split into RTP loss (radio BLER at the edge, HO interruption) and jitter/delay (transport, scheduling, DRX). See DT06.
Latency-specific checks: if ping is high while radio quality and throughput are fine, the RAN is rarely the cause: check backhaul utilisation, transport routing, core (S/P-GW or UPF) location and the test server. Idle-to-connected transitions and DRX add latency to the first packets — always ping with the UE already connected (LAT01).
5G issue taxonomy (useful for structuring an answer): coverage, capacity, interference, mobility, latency, coverage-vs-capacity imbalance (UL-limited edge), QoS/SLA (5QI/GBR mapping), VoNR, massive-IoT (DRX/eDRX/PSM, paging), energy saving (cell DTX/sleep — can look like "missing cell" at night).
STAR answer shape: Situation (KPI, place, time), Task (target), Action (checks in order, then one change at a time), Result (numbers, and that neighbours did not degrade).
Interview Q&A
Q: Throughput is 2–3 Mbps at RSRP −75 dBm. What do you check?
A: SINR and CQI first (interference?), then PRB utilisation and number of users (load?), scheduler limits, CA/MIMO rank, then backhaul and server. Good RSRP rules out coverage, not interference or congestion.
Q: NSA users are rarely on 5G. Where do you look?
A: B1 threshold and NR measurement config on the LTE anchor, NR neighbour definitions, X2 to the gNB, SgNB addition success/failure causes, UE band-combination capability, and NR-leg coverage/SINR.
Q: Ping is 120 ms but radio KPIs and throughput are fine. Cause?
A: Transport or core: backhaul congestion/routing, far core/UPF, test-server location. Confirm by pinging from the site router/transport side vs from the UE; fix routing/capacity/QoS.
Q: A cell has PRB 98–100 % in the busy hour. Options?
A: Short term: load balancing to neighbours/other layers, CA. Long term: add a carrier, sector split (3 → 6), small cells. Parameter tuning does not create capacity.
Q: How do you present an optimisation result in an interview?
A: STAR: the KPI and where/when, the target, the evidence-driven checks and single-step changes, and the before/after numbers including a check that neighbouring cells did not degrade.
DT06 Voice & OTT Quality Testing — MOS / R-factor, SIP flow & timers, jitter and a one-way-audio RCA (VoLTE, VoNR, OTT) Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
Voice testing asks two questions: did the call work (set up, stay up, hand over) and did it sound good. The first is answered by SIP signalling and bearer events in the log. The second is answered by a score from 1 to 5 called MOS (mean opinion score), which drive-test tools compute by comparing the received speech against a reference clip. Speech quality is killed by three things: packets lost, packets arriving unevenly (jitter), and delay. And the classic mystery — "I can hear them, they can't hear me" — is one-way audio, which almost always means the voice packets are flowing in only one direction somewhere in the path. This card covers VoLTE/VoNR and over-the-top (OTT) app calls. PT10 has the IMS architecture and basic SIP flow; this card is the test and debug angle.
Level 1 — MOS, R-factor and the voice KPIs
MOS vs R-factor. The ITU-T G.107 E-model computes a transmission rating R (0–100) from impairments; R maps to MOS. Drive-test tools measure MOS directly with an intrusive algorithm (POLQA, ITU-T P.863, which supersedes PESQ P.862) by playing a known clip at one end and scoring it at the other.
MOS (approx.)R-factorUser rating
4.3 – 4.590 – 100Very satisfied (wideband / HD voice territory)
4.0 – 4.380 – 90Satisfied — typical VoLTE AMR-WB target is MOS ≥ 4.0
3.6 – 4.070 – 80Some users dissatisfied — common "acceptable" floor (R ≥ 70)
3.1 – 3.660 – 70Many dissatisfied
2.6 – 3.150 – 60Nearly all dissatisfied
Typical voice KPI targets (operator examples, not 3GPP limits):
KPITargetKPITarget
Call setup success≥ 98 %Call setup timeVoLTE ≈ 1–3 s (CS legacy 4–10 s)
Call drop rate≤ 1 %IMS registration success≥ 98 %
One-way mouth-to-ear delay< 150 ms (ITU-T G.114 guidance)Jitter≤ 30 ms (absorbed by the jitter buffer)
RTP packet loss≤ 1 %SRVCC success≥ 98 %
Bearers. VoLTE/VoNR speech uses QCI 1 / 5QI 1 (GBR, 100 ms packet delay budget, 10−2 packet error rate); IMS signalling uses QCI/5QI 5. Codecs: AMR-NB, AMR-WB (6.6–23.85 kbit/s; 12.65 kbit/s common), EVS (5.9–128 kbit/s). One RTP packet per 20 ms speech frame = 50 packets/s; during silence AMR sends a SID frame every 160 ms.
Level 2 — how voice is tested: registration, call flow, timers, codes
Call types to script: MO, MT, MO→MT (both ends on test phones), long calls (e.g. 2–3 min) for drop rate, short calls for setup statistics, emergency, VoLTE↔VoNR, EPS fallback, SRVCC. Run POLQA in both directions.
IMS registration (must succeed before any call): REGISTER → P-CSCF → I-CSCF (HSS lookup) → S-CSCF → 401 challenge → REGISTER with the AKA response → 200 OK. Failures: no IMS PDN/PDU session, wrong IMS APN/DNN, P-CSCF discovery or DNS failure, auth failure, SIP timeout, firewall/NAT, poor RF.
MO call with preconditions:
#MessageWhat it tells you
1INVITE (SDP offer, preconditions)Timer B running on the caller
2100 TryingStops INVITE retransmissions (UDP)
3183 Session Progress (SDP answer)Network sets up the QCI 1 / 5QI 1 dedicated bearer via PCRF/PCF
4PRACK → 200 OKReliable provisional response
5UPDATE (resources reserved) → 200 OKPreconditions met on both sides
6180 RingingCalled party alerted — post-dial delay ends here
7200 OK (INVITE) → ACKAnswer; RTP starts both ways
8BYE → 200 OKRelease; dedicated bearer removed
SIP transaction timers (RFC 3261):
TimerDefaultMeaning
T1500 ms (RFC 3261 default)RTT estimate; INVITE retransmit starts at T1 and doubles (Timer A)
T24 sCap on non-INVITE retransmit interval
T45 sMax time a message stays in the network
Timer B64 × T1 = 32 sINVITE transaction timeout → caller sees 408 / call fails
Timer F / H64 × T1Non-INVITE timeout / wait for ACK
Timer D≥ 32 s (UDP)Absorbs response retransmissions after a final response
Expert detail: the IMS profile (TS 24.229) recommends longer values on the UE ↔ P-CSCF leg (T1 = 2 s, T2 = 16 s, T4 = 17 s), so Timer B there is 128 s. Always read the timers actually configured before calling a timeout abnormal.
SIP responses to recognise: 100 Trying, 180 Ringing, 183 Session Progress, 200 OK; 401 Unauthorized (normal during IMS registration — it carries the AKA challenge), 403 Forbidden (provisioning), 404 Not Found, 408 Request Timeout, 480 Temporarily Unavailable, 486 Busy Here, 487 Request Terminated (after CANCEL), 488 Not Acceptable Here (codec/SDP), 500 Server Error, 503 Service Unavailable, 580 Precondition Failure (bearer could not be reserved), 603 Decline. Signalling: SIP on port 5060 (UDP/TCP), 5061 for TLS; RTP on dynamic even ports, RTCP on the next odd port.
Level 3 — the E-model, RTCP jitter and a one-way-audio RCA table
R-factor maths. R = R0 − Is − Id − Ie,eff + A, where R0 ≈ 93.2 by default (basic signal-to-noise), Is = simultaneous impairments, Id = delay impairment, Ie,eff = equipment/codec impairment including packet loss, A = advantage factor (0 for fixed lines). MOS from R (0 < R < 100): MOS = 1 + 0.035·R + 7·10−6·R·(R − 60)·(100 − R). Check: R = 93.2 → MOS ≈ 4.41; R = 80 → 4.02; R = 70 → 3.60; R = 50 → 2.58. Delay costs little below about 150–175 ms, then Id rises steeply; loss costs much more with bursty loss than with random loss, which is why HO interruption hurts more than its average loss suggests.
Jitter as RTCP reports it (RFC 3550): a running estimate J ← J + (|D| − J)/16, where D is the change in packet transit time between consecutive packets. RTCP receiver reports also carry fraction lost and cumulative lost — the quickest way to see which direction is bad.
RCA table for voice symptoms:
SymptomEvidence to pullUsual root causes
One-way audioRTP counters by direction on each leg (UE, eNB/gNB GTP-U, SBC/media gateway)NAT/firewall/SBC pinhole, wrong IP/port in SDP (c= / m= line), UL dedicated bearer or UL TFT missing, core routing, ROHC context mismatch on one side
Robotic / choppy speechRTP loss and sequence gaps, BLER at the moment, HO eventsCell-edge BLER, HO interruption, missing ROHC context (ROHC01), transport loss
Delay / echoRTCP round-trip, jitter-buffer depthTransport delay, transcoding, deep jitter buffer; echo is usually the far-end handset/acoustic path
Mute / gapsRTP silence longer than a few secondsRLF and re-establishment, SRVCC gap, DRX/scheduling starvation of QCI 1
Stuck at ringing / no answerSIP trace: missing 180 or 200 OKCalled side paging/registration, precondition failure (580), IMS routing
Drop while movingEvents before the drop: HO, B2, RLFMissing neighbour, late HO, SRVCC failure (wrong B2, missing IRAT neighbour, Sv failure)
OTT voice/video (messaging and conferencing apps): no QCI 1 bearer — the call rides the default bearer, so quality depends on best-effort scheduling and load. Typical test targets: setup 1–5 s, MOS ≥ 4.0, loss < 1 %, jitter < 30 ms, one-way delay < 150 ms, video freezes < 2 %. Rough bitrates: voice 24–40 kbit/s, 720p video ≈ 0.7–1.8 Mbit/s. Run each sample 3–5 minutes. Compare OTT vs VoLTE in the same spot to separate RF from QoS effects.
SRVCC drive check: start a VoLTE call, drive to the LTE edge, confirm the B2 trigger, the HO command to the CS RAT and speech continuity; the KPI is voice interruption time (PT21).
Interview Q&A
Q: What MOS and R-factor do you expect for good VoLTE?
A: MOS ≥ 4.0 with AMR-WB, i.e. R about 80 or more. R ≥ 70 (MOS ≈ 3.6) is the usual "acceptable" floor. Measured in the field with POLQA (P.863).
Q: A user can hear the other party but is not heard. How do you debug?
A: It is one-way audio: check RTP packet counters per direction at each hop (UE, RAN GTP-U, SBC). Check the SDP IP/port, NAT/firewall pinholes at the SBC, that the UL dedicated bearer/TFT exists, and ROHC context. The direction where packets stop is the fault location.
Q: What is SIP Timer B and what happens when it expires?
A: The INVITE client transaction timeout, 64 × T1 = 32 s with RFC 3261 defaults (IMS UE side often T1 = 2 s → 128 s). On expiry the transaction fails as a timeout (408) and the call attempt fails.
Q: Why does a 401 during IMS registration not mean failure?
A: It is the normal authentication challenge: the S-CSCF returns 401 with the AKA challenge, and the UE re-sends REGISTER with the response; 200 OK completes registration.
Q: Why is bursty packet loss worse than the same average random loss?
A: Packet-loss concealment handles isolated losses well, but consecutive lost frames (e.g. during a HO gap) produce audible dropouts; the E-model's Ie,eff grows faster with burstiness.
DT07 Data-Service Testing — FTP / HTTP / iperf / ping / video methods, pass targets, and what really limits throughput Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
A data test pushes traffic through a pipe made of many sections: the radio link, the scheduler sharing the cell, the phone itself, the backhaul, the core network, the test server and TCP. The speed you measure is set by the narrowest section. Good data testing is therefore about two things: choosing test methods that do not accidentally become the bottleneck (a small file, one TCP stream on a long-delay path, a far-away server), and recording enough evidence to say which section limited the result. UE09 covers iperf in the lab; this card is the field-testing and "why is it slow" angle.
The throughput chain — find the narrowest box
Level 1 — the tests and what each one measures
TestMeasuresHow (field practice)Watch out for
FTP / HTTP download & uploadSustained throughputLarge file (hundreds of MB to GB) so TCP ramp-up is negligible; fixed windows of 10–60 s; multi-thread for high ratesTCP retransmissions, server limits
iperf3 TCPThroughput with a controlled serveriperf3 -c <server> -t 30 -P 8 -R (−R = server sends = DL; −P parallel streams)Needs your own server near the core
iperf3 UDPRadio capacity, loss, jitter without TCPiperf3 -c <server> -u -b 300M -t 30 -R; raise −b until loss appearsOver-driving just measures drops
PingRTT, loss, jitterping <host> -n 100 (Windows 32-byte default; Linux 56 data bytes); UE already connectedFirst ping includes idle→connected setup
HTTP browsingUser experienceDNS time, TCP connect, TLS handshake, time to first byte, page loadContent and CDN dominate
Video streaming (DASH/HLS)User experienceStart-up time, rebuffering ratio/count, resolution reached, dropped frames; ≥ 2 min per sampleAdaptive bitrate hides capacity
Speed-test appsQuick benchmarkStationary, ≥ 3 runs, different servers, averageServer choice varies
Typical pass targets (operator examples — every operator sets its own):
AreaTargetAlso
Ping RTT (UE connected)LTE ≈ 30–60 ms; NR SA often < 30 msLoss < 1 %, jitter < 10 ms
HTTPDNS < 100 ms, TCP connect < 100 ms, TLS < 300 ms, TTFB < 0.8 sPage load < 2 s, success ≥ 98 %
VideoStart-up < 2 sRebuffering ratio < 1 %, dropped frames < 2 %
TCP transferRetransmissions < 1 %Throughput per the cell's design class (DT03)
Level 2 — test method and the triage order
The session ladder. Before blaming RF, localise the failure to one step: attach/registration → PDN/PDU session → IP address → DNS → server reachable → transfer → release. "No data" with a good radio link is usually a session, APN/DNN or DNS problem (CM06 for GTP-U counters).
Method that produces defensible numbers:
  • Use an on-net server close to the core (or at the site for a RAN-only test) to exclude the internet.
  • Run UDP first to find what the radio can carry, then TCP to see what users get. A big gap means TCP/transport/server, not radio.
  • Test DL and UL separately; lock RAT/band when testing one layer; note CA and rank state.
  • Log the RF snapshot with every sample: RSRP, SINR, CQI, MCS, rank, scheduled PRBs, BLER, number of CCs.
  • Report percentiles (5th / 50th / 95th) and cell-edge throughput, not only the average or peak.
  • Repeat at busy and quiet hours — the difference is the load effect.
Low-throughput triage order: RSRP/RSRQ → SINR → CQI → MCS and BLER → CA active? → MIMO rank → PRB share / cell load / users → UE category and band-combo support → TDD pattern (DL:UL share) → backhaul utilisation → core → server/TCP. Stop at the first abnormal item.
Expected radio ceiling comes from TPUT01: PRBs × layers × modulation order × code rate × (1 − overhead) × DL/UL share. Compare the measured value with that ceiling at the logged MCS and rank before calling it low.
Level 3 — TCP maths, radio subtleties and transport traps
TCP limits you must be able to calculate.
  • Window limit: throughput ≤ window ÷ RTT. A 64 KB window over 40 ms RTT caps one stream at ≈ 13 Mbit/s — no matter how good the radio is. That is why high-rate tests need window scaling and parallel streams.
  • Bandwidth-delay product: BDP = rate × RTT. 1 Gbit/s × 20 ms = 2.5 MB must be in flight to fill the pipe.
  • Loss limit (Mathis approximation): throughput ≈ (MSS / RTT) × (1.22 / √p). With MSS 1400 B, RTT 40 ms, loss p = 10−4 → ≈ 34 Mbit/s per stream. Tiny residual loss on transport caps TCP hard.
  • Slow start: a 5 MB file on a 300 Mbit/s link finishes before TCP reaches full rate; the average looks low. Use large files or time-based tests.
Radio-side subtleties. BLER around 10 % on first transmissions is normal — link adaptation targets it; residual loss after HARQ/RLC should be near zero. Rank 1 in a good-SINR area points to antenna/branch imbalance or correlation (check per-branch RSRP). UL is often power-limited at the edge: check PHR (negative = power-limited) before blaming the scheduler. In TDD, a DL-heavy frame pattern caps UL — it is configuration, not a fault.
Transport and core traps. MTU/fragmentation issues on GTP-U tunnels (large packets dropped or fragmented) show up as good ping but poor TCP; a test with smaller MSS confirms it. Policing/shaping on the backhaul shows a flat ceiling at a round number regardless of radio quality. A server with a 1 GbE port cannot demonstrate 1.5 Gbit/s.
Latency split (LAT01): radio scheduling (SR→grant→data, HARQ retransmissions), DRX wake-up, transport, core, server. Ping from the UE and from the site transport side at the same time to split RAN from beyond-RAN.
Interview Q&A
Q: Why run UDP before TCP in a throughput test?
A: UDP shows what the radio and transport can carry without TCP congestion control. If UDP reaches the expected rate but TCP does not, the limit is TCP window, RTT, loss or the server — not the RAN.
Q: One TCP stream gives 13 Mbit/s over a link with 40 ms RTT. Why?
A: Window-limited: 64 KB ÷ 40 ms ≈ 13 Mbit/s. Enable window scaling or use parallel streams (iperf3 -P).
Q: Good RSRP, good SINR, but low throughput. What next?
A: Check scheduled PRBs and cell load/users, CA and rank state, UE category, TDD pattern, then backhaul utilisation, core and server; compare the measured rate with the TPUT01 ceiling at the logged MCS and rank.
Q: What does iperf3 -R do?
A: Reverse mode: the server sends and the client receives, so with the client on the UE it measures downlink.
Q: BLER is 10 % in the log. Is that a problem?
A: Not by itself: link adaptation deliberately targets about 10 % first-transmission BLER for efficiency. Look at residual BLER after HARQ and at whether MCS is dropping.
DT08 Field RCA Playbook — symptom → evidence → root cause from drive-test logs: drop taxonomy, per-drop procedure and CA / EN-DC / MIMO checks Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
A drive-test log is a flight recorder: signal levels every second, every signalling message, and every event (handover, drop, re-establishment) stamped with time and GPS position. Root-cause analysis means walking backwards from the bad event to the first thing that went wrong, and proving it with at least two independent pieces of evidence. PDLAY is the protocol-layer map ("which layer broke"); this card is the field / RF-optimisation map: which radio or network condition caused the drop, slow data or failed handover, and which physical or parameter change fixes it.
The field RCA loop
Level 1 — symptom-to-cause matrix for field logs
Symptom → first suspicion → evidence → typical fix.
Symptom in the logFirst suspicionEvidence that confirms itTypical corrective action
Low RSRP (and low SINR)CoverageServing/scanner RSRP, distance to site, terrain, tilt/azimuth, site down?Uptilt carefully, power, antenna height, new site/small cell
Good RSRP, low SINR / RSRQInterferenceScanner top-N (one far cell = overshoot; flat group = pollution), PCI mod-3 of top neighbours, load vs timeTilt/power on interferer, PCI re-plan (OPT03)
High timing advance / served far from siteOvershooting cell or coverage hole in betweenTA distribution, serving-cell plotDowntilt, power, fill the hole
RLF / drop just before HOToo-late HO or missing neighbourMeasurement reports vs neighbour list; was the target measured?Neighbour add, TTT/offset/CIO (OPT02)
RLF just after HO, back to sourceToo-early HOTarget RSRP/SINR after HORaise offset/TTT, CIO ↓ on that relation
HO to wrong cell / HO failure with good levelsPCI confusion, wrong relationReported PCI vs expected cell global ID, X2/Xn statePCI re-plan, neighbour clean-up
Rising BLER + high PRB useInterference and/or loadPRB utilisation, users, UL RSSI per PRBLoad balancing, capacity, interference fix
UL-only failures (DL fine)UL coverage / UL interference / power controlUE TX power and PHR, UL SINR, UL noise riseP0/alpha, interference hunt, UL diversity check
Good radio, poor data/voiceTransport, core, IMSLatency split, SIP trace, GTP-U countersTransport/core ticket (not a radio change)
Two-source rule: never conclude from one trace. Pair the drive log with PM counters (cell-level, all users) or a scanner, and pair RF with Layer-3. A drive log sees one UE on one route; counters see everyone.
Level 2 — classifying every drop, and feature-specific checks
Per-drop procedure. For every drop: take its time and GPS position → list events in the preceding 10–20 s → read Layer-3 around it (last Measurement Report, any HO command, RRC re-establishment request and its cause) → take the RF snapshot 5–10 s before the drop (serving and best-neighbour RSRP/SINR, CQI, BLER, UE TX power) → classify:
Drop classSignature in the log
RLF / poor coverageRSRP and SINR falling for several seconds, T310 expiry, re-establishment attempt
HO failureMeasurement Report sent, HO command late/absent, or RACH on target fails (T304 expiry)
Congestion / admissionRelease or reject with load-related cause, high PRB, many users
Core / IMSRadio fine; NAS/SIP error or bearer release from the core
Transport / backhaulRadio fine; S1/NG or X2/Xn link problems, TNL cause codes
UE issueOnly one device model affected; capability mismatch
Parameter misconfigurationBehaviour that contradicts the plan (wrong threshold, missing relation, barred cell)
In a typical problem sector the split is dominated by HO failures and coverage, followed by congestion and core/transport — but count your own, do not assume. Fix the biggest class first.
Events to scan in any log: HO start/success/failure, RLF/T310, RRC re-establishment, RRC release (with cause), attach/registration, service request, bearer setup/release, CSFB/SRVCC/EPS fallback, cell reselection, SgNB/SCell add/release, throughput steps.
Feature-specific field checks:
  • CA: SCell added where SCell RSRP/SINR passes the A4/A6 thresholds? Band combo in UE capability? SCell add success and retention, CA drop rate.
  • EN-DC: B1 threshold reached? SgNB addition success, SgNB release causes, NR-leg SINR, X2 to gNB up (NN02, NN03).
  • MIMO / beams: rank distribution vs SINR; persistent rank 1 at high SINR = branch imbalance or correlation; frequent beam switches or beam failures = alignment/coverage.
  • DSS: split of resources NR vs LTE, per-RAT throughput and SINR.
Level 3 — problem statements, evidence hierarchy and false trails
Write a precise problem statement first: "drop rate 4 % in sector X between 10:00 and 14:00 on weekdays, since the change on date Y" beats "sector X is bad". It already tells you to look at load (time), recent changes (date) and one sector.
Evidence hierarchy. Layer-3 messages and cause codes beat RF levels; RF levels beat KPI trends; a reproducible re-drive beats everything. A 3 dB RSRP difference between two drives on the same road is within normal fading and shadowing — compare binned averages (50–100 m bins) and percentiles, not single samples.
Change control. One change per cell per iteration, a baseline drive before, a validation drive after on the same route, speed and time of day, plus about 3 days of counter trend. Check that neighbouring cells did not degrade. Keep a rollback plan.
Common false trails.
  • Blaming HO parameters for drops that are really coverage holes (no neighbour was strong enough either).
  • Blaming RF for a data problem that is DNS, APN/DNN or MTU (good ping, no pages).
  • Treating energy-saving cell sleep at night as a site outage.
  • Test UE issues: overheating throttle, wrong band lock left from a previous test, battery saver.
  • Assuming the scanner and the UE agree: the scanner sees every cell; the UE only reports what it was configured to measure.
Corrective-action menu (from cheapest/fastest): parameter (neighbour list, HO thresholds, PCI) → electrical tilt/power → azimuth/mechanical → hardware (feeder, connector, PIM, antenna) → new sector/site. Transport/core findings go to those teams with the evidence. Always re-drive to validate.
Report: executive summary, KPI vs target, plots (RSRP, SINR, serving cell, throughput, events), each issue with evidence and root cause, actions, before/after comparison.
Interview Q&A
Q: How do you analyse a single call drop in a drive-test log?
A: Locate time and position, list events in the preceding 10–20 s, read Layer-3 (last Measurement Report, HO command, re-establishment cause), take the RF snapshot 5–10 s before (serving and neighbour RSRP/SINR, CQI, BLER, UE TX power), then classify: coverage, HO failure, congestion, core/transport, UE or configuration.
Q: Good RSRP but poor SINR on a route. First thing you look at?
A: Scanner top-N at those points: a single strong distant cell suggests overshoot; several cells within a few dB suggests pilot pollution; then check PCI mod 3 of the strongest neighbours and whether SINR tracks load.
Q: Why is a drive log alone not enough for RCA?
A: It is one UE on one route at one time. Confirm with PM counters (all users), a scanner (all cells), alarms and a repeat drive; correlate RF, Layer-3 and KPI.
Q: After a tilt change the drop rate in the target cell improved. Are you done?
A: No: re-drive the same route, check the 5th-percentile RSRP for new holes, watch neighbouring cells' KPIs for a few days, and document before/after.
Reading real captures
CAP1 X2 Handover Field-by-Field from a Real Capture — HO Request IEs, the RRC HO Command inside the Ack, SN Status COUNTs and measured timings Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
A handover is like a hospital transferring a patient. The first hospital sends the patient file (who the UE is, its security keys, its bearers, its capabilities). The second hospital replies "bed ready, here are the directions" (the RRC message the UE needs to jump across). Then the first hospital says "this is exactly where we stopped the treatment" (the PDCP sequence numbers). Finally the second hospital tells the first one it can close the file. This card opens each of those four X2AP messages in a real lab capture and reads them field by field, with the real timings between them. The overview and the capture-filter lesson are in PDX2.
Measured timeline of one successful X2 HO (HO #5 below), not to scale
Level 1 — message order and the key IEs
Filter used: sctp.data_payload_proto_id == 27 || s1ap.procedureCode == 3 (X2 was on IPv6 and S1 on IPv4 on this site). Order: HandoverRequest → HandoverRequestAcknowledge → SNStatusTransfer → (S1) PathSwitchRequest → PathSwitchRequestAcknowledge → UEContextRelease. Values below are the real ones from the capture, with identifiers removed.

HandoverRequestAcknowledge (target → source): Old- and New-eNB-UE-X2AP-ID (the target now adds its own handle), E-RABs-Admitted-List with both e-RABs (UL and DL GTP forwarding tunnels on the target's IPv6 address, for X2-U forwarding, see LF12), nothing in a not-admitted list, and the TargeteNBtoSource-eNBTransparentContainer. That container is an RRC HandoverCommand which wraps the RRCConnectionReconfiguration the source forwards unchanged to the UE.
SNStatusTransfer (source → target): per AM bearer, the UL and DL COUNT (PDCP SN + HFN). UEContextRelease (target → source): the target tells the source it can free the UE, sent after the Path Switch Ack.
Level 2 — inside the RRC HO command, the COUNTs, and the timings
The RRCConnectionReconfiguration inside the Ack (real values, good for checking a config against the plan):

dl-/ul-CarrierFreq, bandwidthEARFCN 450 / 18450, n75Band 1, 15 MHz (75 PRB), intra-frequency HO
t304ms2000the UE has 2 s to complete random access on the target, or it declares HO failure and re-establishes (TMR01)
newUE-Identity, rach-ConfigDedicatednew C-RNTI, ra-PreambleIndex 5352 preambles are for contention-based access (0-51), so 53 is a reserved dedicated preamble: contention-free RACH on the target
RACH commongroup A 28, ramping 2 dB, initial target −104 dBm, preambleTransMax n10, RAR window sf10, contention resolution sf64, maxHARQ-Msg3Tx 4target-cell PRACH behaviour (PD03)
PRACHrootSequenceIndex 52, configIndex 3, ZCZ 8, freqOffset 3must match the target cell's own SIB2; a mismatch means the UE never gets a RAR
PowerreferenceSignalPower 13 dBm, p0-NominalPUSCH −100, p0-NominalPUCCH −104 dBm, alpha 1, p-Max 23 dBm, antennaPortsCount an4RS power per RE and UL power control of the target cell
RLC AMSRB: t-PollRetransmit 45 ms, pollPDU 4, maxRetx 32 · DRB: 35 ms, pollPDU 16, maxRetx 32maxRetx reached = RLC failure = RLF trigger (RLF01)
SN Status Transfer, decoded. COUNT = HFN ‖ PDCP SN. The message uses the basic COUNT IE (12-bit SN + 20-bit HFN; 15-bit and 18-bit SNs need the "extended" COUNT IEs), so the bearers use 12-bit PDCP SNs: e-RAB 5 (QCI 9) UL COUNT = SN 132 / HFN 0, DL COUNT = SN 2120 / HFN 151, i.e. 151 × 4096 + 2120 = 620 616 PDCP PDUs sent in DL so far. e-RAB 6 (QCI 5) UL 8 / HFN 0, DL 12 / HFN 0. The target must continue from these values: COUNT is an input to ciphering, so a wrong HFN turns every packet into garbage, and the SNs give in-order, lossless delivery with the forwarded data. Only RLC-AM bearers are listed (UM bearers such as VoLTE voice are not).

Measured timings (three full HOs in the pair of captures):
HOReq → AckAck → SN StatusSN Status → Path SwitchPS → PS AckPS Ack → ReleaseTotal
#3 measurement-triggered25.8 ms7.3 ms383 ms0.30 ms1.9 ms418 ms
#5 after an RLF Indication44.2 ms7.9 ms18.2 ms0.36 ms0.9 ms71.5 ms
#6 after an RLF Indication36.9 ms7.3 ms30.6 ms0.28 ms84 ms159 ms
Who owns each gap: Req → Ack = target eNB (admission control, resource allocation, building the RRC config), 26-44 ms here. Ack → SN Status = source eNB (send the RRC Reconfiguration, freeze PDCP, read COUNTs), a steady 7-8 ms. SN Status → Path Switch = air interface: the UE leaves, does RACH on the target and sends RRC Reconfiguration Complete, then the target sends the Path Switch. PS → PS Ack = core (MME + SGW bearer modify), 0.3 ms on this lab core. PS Ack → Release = target eNB housekeeping.
Level 3 — reading the numbers like an expert, and turning them into test checks
• The 383 ms gap in HO #3 is the only real "UE moves" case, and it is long. Contention-free RACH normally lets the UE finish in a few tens of ms. A long SN-Status-to-Path-Switch gap means the UE took several RACH attempts or the target was slow to process RRC Reconfiguration Complete. It is still well inside t304 (2 s), so the HO succeeded, but in a mobility KPI run it inflates interruption time. Confirm with the UE log (RACH attempts, Msg1 power ramping) or target MAC trace.
• HOs #5 and #6 have short 18-31 ms gaps because they follow an RLF Indication: the UE had most likely already re-established in the target cell, so there was no real RACH to wait for. Do not use such HOs to benchmark HO execution time (CAP2 explains this pattern).
• The 84 ms release in HO #6: the target held the source's context for 84 ms after the Path Switch Ack, instead of about 1 ms in the other two. That is harmless if the target is waiting for the GTP End Marker to drain forwarded data (LF12). It is worth a question if the release timer is not documented.
• Bundled chunks: in some packets Wireshark shows x2ap.procedureCode == 0,5. That is two SCTP DATA chunks in one packet (Ack + UE Context Release). Count messages, not frames, when you build statistics.
• Security sanity check: NCC = 0 in the HO Request is normal for the first HO after Initial Context Setup. If a later HO of the same UE still shows NCC 0 after a successful Path Switch, the source ignored the new {NH, NCC} from the MME, which weakens forward key separation.

Pass criteria for an automated X2 HO test built from this capture:
CheckRule
PreparationHandoverRequest with the expected cause and target ECGI; Ack within a budget (e.g. < 100 ms); all E-RABs admitted, none in the not-admitted list
ExecutionSN Status Transfer after the Ack and before UE Context Release, with the same X2AP ID pair as the Ack
CompletionPathSwitchRequest + Acknowledge (no PathSwitchRequestFailure), then UEContextRelease from the target
Stabilityno RLF Indication for this UE within N seconds after the HO (too-early check, LF11)
ConfigtargetPhysCellId, EARFCN, PRACH root/config and t304 in the RRC container match the target cell's planned values
Related: PDX2 (capture overview and the IPv6 filter lesson), CAP2 (RLF Indication and a real ordering defect), LF10 / LF12 (feature view), PT08 / HO04 (X2 vs S1 HO theory).
Interview Q&A
Q: Where is the RRC message that tells the UE to hand over, in an X2 capture?
A: Inside the X2AP HandoverRequestAcknowledge, in the TargeteNBtoSource-eNBTransparentContainer. It holds an RRC HandoverCommand wrapping the RRCConnectionReconfiguration with mobilityControlInfo. The target builds it; the source only forwards it to the UE.
Q: What is in SN Status Transfer and why does it matter?
A: For each AM bearer, the UL and DL COUNT (PDCP SN plus HFN), optionally with a bitmap of missing UL PDCP SDUs. The target continues ciphering from that COUNT and delivers data in order without loss or duplicates. A wrong or missing value causes deciphering errors or data loss after the HO.
Q: HO Request to Ack took 26-44 ms and Path Switch to Ack 0.3 ms. What does each number measure?
A: The first is target eNB processing: admission control and building the RRC reconfiguration. The second is the core: MME plus SGW moving the S1-U path. Neither is the user-plane interruption time, which is measured at the UE from the RRC Reconfiguration to RRC Reconfiguration Complete.
Q: The HO command has ra-PreambleIndex 53 and the cell uses 52 preambles. So what?
A: Preambles 0-51 are for contention-based access, so 53 is one of the reserved dedicated preambles. The UE does contention-free random access on the target, which is faster and avoids collisions. If the dedicated preamble were inside the contention range, two UEs could collide during the HO.
Q: What does nextHopChainingCount = 0 in the HO Request tell you?
A: No fresh Next Hop key has been provided yet, so the target key KeNB* is derived horizontally from the current KeNB. After the Path Switch, the MME returns a new NH with NCC = 1 so the next HO can use vertical derivation.
CAP2 RLF Indication over X2 in a Real Capture — the handover that saves the call, and an SN Status Transfer that arrives after UE Context Release (spotting and reporting a defect) Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
Sometimes a phone loses its radio link before any handover happens. It then knocks on the door of a nearby cell and says "I was just in cell 47, please take me back" (RRC re-establishment). The new eNB does not know this phone, so it sends an RLF Indication over X2 to the eNB that owns cell 47: "your UE failed and turned up here". In the lab captures the old eNB answers by running a handover preparation toward the new eNB, which hands over the UE's file so the call survives instead of dropping. The same captures also show a message arriving after the UE's file was already closed, which is exactly the kind of ordering bug a test engineer should catch and report.
What the capture shows (eNB-B = where the UE re-established, eNB-A = owner of the failure cell). Times from the first RLF Indication.
Level 1 — what an RLF Indication is and what it carries
RLFIndication is X2AP procedure 13, a class-2 message (no response). It is sent by the eNB where the UE re-established (or reconnected) to the eNB that owns the cell where the radio link failed. It is the raw input to Mobility Robustness Optimisation (MRO, LF11 / SON01). The IEs in the capture:
IEValue seenPurpose
Failure cell PCI47the cell the UE was in when it failed (from the UE's re-establishment request)
Re-establishment cell ECGIPLMN + 28-bit cell ID of eNB-B's cellwhere the UE came back
C-RNTI in failure cell16-bit valuelets eNB-A find the UE context
ShortMAC-I16-bit valuelets eNB-A verify that the UE really is that UE (computed with its old keys)
RRCConnReestabIndicatorotherFailurethe UE's re-establishment cause: reconfigurationFailure / handoverFailure / otherFailure. otherFailure = a plain radio link failure, not a failed HO
Rel-9 and later can also carry the UE's RLF Report container (measurements at failure time). It was not present here.

In the pair of captures (PDX2): 5 RLF events, 8 RLF Indication messages, and every event is followed about 2.5-3.3 ms later by a HandoverRequest from the failure-cell owner to the re-establishment eNB. All 5 end with a Path Switch, so the calls survived. This "RLF Indication → HO preparation" pairing is vendor behaviour: LTE X2AP has no dedicated "fetch UE context" procedure for re-establishment (NR XnAP later added Retrieve UE Context), so the HO preparation is reused to move the context.
Level 2 — the two directions behave differently: spot the defect
Filter: x2ap.procedureCode == 13 || x2ap.procedureCode == 0 || x2ap.procedureCode == 4 || x2ap.procedureCode == 5 || s1ap.procedureCode == 3. Then list the X2AP ID pair of every message.
CaseRLF Ind.Order seenX2AP IDs (Old, New)Verdict
UE re-establishes at eNB-A (2 events)1 per eventReq → Ack 37-44 ms → SN Status 7-8 ms → Path Switch → Release(5,8) (5,8) (5,8); (6,9) (6,9) (6,9)textbook
UE re-establishes at eNB-B (3 events)2 per event, identical contentReq → Path Switch (71-102 ms after the Req) → Ack + Release in one packet (~205 ms) → SN Status 7-28 ms laterReq (1) · Ack/Release (1,1) · SN Status (2,1); likewise 3 → (3,2) → (4,2) and 6 → (6,4) → (7,4)three anomalies
The three anomalies in the eNB-B cases:
1. SN Status Transfer after UE Context Release. The release frees the UE on eNB-A; the PDCP COUNTs then arrive for a context the target has already confirmed as complete. Both captures (one at each end) show the same order, so it is not a capture artefact. Possible impact: the target starts PDCP without the right COUNTs, which risks lost or duplicated packets, or a deciphering mismatch on the re-established bearers.
2. Old-eNB-UE-X2AP-ID mismatch. The SN Status Transfer carries Old ID = (value in the HO Request) + 1 every time. The receiver cannot match it to any UE. It should answer with an X2AP ErrorIndication (unknown Old eNB UE X2AP ID); none appears, so the message is silently dropped.
3. Late, bundled Ack and duplicate RLF Indications. The Ack is sent only after the Path Switch, always about 204-207 ms after the Request whatever the Path Switch time. A constant delay like that usually means a timer, not processing. eNB-B also sends every RLF Indication twice (new SCTP TSN, same IEs), which can double-count failures in eNB-A's MRO statistics.

Also in the same capture, on S1: NASNonDeliveryIndication [radio-connection-with-ue-lost] during some attach attempts (Identity, ESM information or Authentication request could not be delivered). It is another sign of a weak-coverage area between these cells.
Level 3 — MRO reading, and how to report the defect
MRO classification (done by the failure-cell owner, using its own HO history and the UE History Information):
PatternClassTypical fix
RLF soon after a successful HO into the failure cell, re-establishment back in the source celltoo earlyraise A3 offset / TTT or lower CIO toward that neighbour
RLF with no recent HO, re-establishment in another celltoo latelower A3 offset / TTT or raise CIO
RLF soon after a HO, re-establishment in a third cellwrong cellfix neighbour list / CIO per relation
A neat trick from the capture: the C-RNTI in one RLF Indication equals the newUE-Identity given in the HO command of an earlier HO into PCI 47. So the same UE handed over into cell 47 and then failed there 44 s later. That is far longer than a normal too-early window (a few seconds), and the cause is otherFailure. The other RLF events carry different C-RNTIs, i.e. different UEs. So these look like too-late events: the UE stayed in cell 47 until the link broke instead of handing over to eNB-B earlier. Check the HO-too-late counter for that neighbour relation to confirm.

How to report it (use the bug template, jira-create-bug style):
FieldWhat to write
Title"X2: SN Status Transfer sent after UE Context Release, with wrong Old-eNB-UE-X2AP-ID, in RLF-triggered HO preparation"
Stepstwo neighbour eNBs with X2; drive the UE out of the source cell until RLF; UE re-establishes on the neighbour
ExpectedAck → SN Status → Path Switch → UE Context Release, same X2AP ID pair in all messages (TS 36.423)
Actualframe numbers and timestamps of Req, Path Switch, Ack+Release packet and SN Status; ID pairs (1)/(1,1)/(2,1); 3 of 3 occurrences in the capture = 100 % reproduction in this scenario
Evidencepcaps from both eNBs (same order at both ends), display filter, the contrast with the reverse direction, which is correct
First-line analysistarget holds the Ack for a fixed ~205 ms and bundles it with the release; source then sends SN Status with a new UE ID. Ask for both eNB software versions and PDCP counters (discarded PDUs, integrity/decipher failures) on the re-established UE. Separate minor issue: duplicate RLF Indication
Pitfall: do not report "HO failure". All five HOs completed and the calls survived; the defect is the message order and IDs, and its impact on data continuity needs a user-plane check (iperf gap or PDCP counters) to size.
Interview Q&A
Q: Who sends an X2 RLF Indication, to whom, and why?
A: The eNB where the UE re-established sends it to the eNB that owns the cell where the radio link failed. It carries the failure-cell PCI, the re-establishment cell ECGI, the old C-RNTI, ShortMAC-I and the re-establishment cause, so the owner can classify the failure for MRO (too early, too late, wrong cell).
Q: Why does a HandoverRequest follow the RLF Indication within 3 ms in the capture?
A: The failure-cell owner pushes the UE context to the eNB where the UE re-established, using the HO preparation, so the re-establishment can succeed and the call survives. LTE X2 has no dedicated context-fetch procedure, so this is vendor behaviour built on standard messages.
Q: The SN Status Transfer comes after UE Context Release. Why is that a problem?
A: UE Context Release means the handover is finished and the source can delete the UE. The COUNTs must reach the target before it starts PDCP on the new bearers. Arriving afterwards (here also with a UE ID that matches nothing), they are ignored, which risks loss, duplicates or ciphering errors on the re-established bearers.
Q: How do you prove an ordering defect is real and not a capture artefact?
A: Capture at both ends and check the same order appears in both, check the SCTP TSNs (sending order), check it repeats every time in the same scenario, and compare with a scenario where it is correct (here the reverse direction). Then attach frame numbers and filters to the bug.
Q: What does RRCConnReestabIndicator = otherFailure mean for MRO?
A: The UE re-established after a radio link failure, not after a failed handover or reconfiguration. With no recent HO into the failure cell this points to a too-late HO toward the re-establishment cell.
CAP3 S1 Instability in a Real Capture — per-UE Resets from the core, ~50 % Service Request success, S1 Setup Failure with TimeToWait 20 s, NAS cause #9 (worked triage) Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
The S1 link is the eNB's phone line to the core network. In these two long lab captures (4.2 h and 7.5 h) the line is technically "up", but every couple of minutes the core says "forget this user, I lost its transport". When that user immediately calls back, the core hangs up again, and only the retry 5 seconds later works. So about half of all attempts to resume data fail first time, and users see short outages. Later the core even refuses the eNB's reconnection for 20 seconds and forgets some users completely, so their phones must register from scratch. This card shows how to read that story out of the S1AP messages step by step, and why the radio is not the culprit.
The repeating cycle (seen about every 1.5-4 minutes)
Level 1 — characterise the capture first (the numbers)
Both files: IPv4, one eNB and its MME-side peer (a gateway in front of the core in this lab), S1AP on SCTP PPID 18, test PLMNs 001-01 and 002-01. Start with tshark -q -z io,phs, then a histogram of S1AP procedure codes (10 Paging, 12 InitialUEMessage, 9 InitialContextSetup, 14 Reset, 17 S1Setup, 11/13 DL/UL NAS transport, 23 UEContextRelease, 40/41 eNB/MME ConfigurationTransfer).
ItemFile 1 (4.2 h)File 2 (7.5 h)
Paging (MME → eNB)4 898 (~1 170 / h)6 808 (~900 / h)
Reset (all from the MME side)247 = 246 per-UE + 1 full393 = 392 per-UE + 1 full
… cause transport-resource-unavailable116154
… cause misc-unspecified123189
… cause om-intervention8 (incl. the full reset)50 (incl. the full reset)
Service Requests (InitialUEMessage)243413
InitialContextSetup Request = Response127 (no failures)192 (no failures)
Full attaches (with Authentication + Security Mode)1018
NAS rejects cause #95 TAU reject + 2 Service reject (+2 TAU reject #17 network failure)2 TAU reject + 2 Service reject
S1 Setupboth files: full Reset (om-intervention) → S1SetupRequest ~5 s later → S1SetupFailure (misc-unspecified, TimeToWait v20s) → retry exactly 20.000 s later → S1SetupResponse
Service-request success from the capture ≈ ICS Responses / Service Requests = 127 / 243 = 52 % and 192 / 413 = 46 %. The ICS count also includes the attaches, so the true figure is slightly lower (about 48 % and 42 %). A live network targets > 98-99 % (NBKPI). Note there are zero InitialContextSetupFailures: the failures happen before ICS, when the core answers a Service Request with a Reset.
Level 2 — reading it step by step
1. Reset direction and type. Every Reset comes from the MME side. S1AP Reset has two types: s1-Interface (reset-all: "the whole interface restarted, drop every UE") and partOfS1-Interface (a list of UE-associated logical connections, by MME-UE-S1AP-ID and/or eNB-UE-S1AP-ID: "forget just these UEs"). Here all but one per file are per-UE. The eNB answers each with ResetAcknowledge in 2-8 ms and releases those UEs locally.
2. The pattern. A Reset [transport-resource-unavailable] for one UE; ~150 ms later that UE (which still had traffic) sends a Service Request; the core answers it 0.4 ms later with another Reset [misc-unspecified] instead of InitialContextSetup. The UE's NAS guard timer T3417 (5 s) expires and it retries ~5.3 s later; this time ICS Request → Response takes 66-80 ms and works. Each cycle = one failed Service Request + about 5 s of user-plane outage. 123 of 243 Service Requests (and 189 of 413) were answered this way, which exactly explains the ~50 % success.
3. What the cause means. transport-resource-unavailable from the core side points at the core or gateway losing the S1-U/GTP path or the bearer context for that UE (SGW/gateway transport problem, a restart, a full tunnel table). The 0.4 ms answer to the next Service Request is too fast for any real processing: the core has simply not recovered that UE yet. No eNB-initiated UEContextReleaseRequest occurs in the 10 s before any transport-cause Reset (the few there are, mostly user-inactivity, happen elsewhere), so the trigger is not radio.
4. om-intervention. 8 and 50 Resets carry om-intervention: someone (or an automated OAM action) restarted things on the core side during the run. In the second file they come in two clusters (about 2.3-3.5 h and 5.5-6.6 h into the run), so mark those periods as "operator activity" and exclude it from stability KPIs.
5. S1 Setup Failure and TimeToWait. About 5 s after the full Reset (most likely after an SCTP association restart) the eNB sent S1SetupRequest (Global eNB ID, eNB name, supported TAs with TAC 0x3131, broadcast PLMNs 001-01 and 002-01, default paging DRX). The core replied S1SetupFailure, cause misc-unspecified, TimeToWait = v20s. The eNB correctly waited exactly 20.000 s and retried; S1SetupResponse followed (MME name, served GUMMEIs, RelativeMMECapacity 1), then eNB/MME ConfigurationTransfer (SON exchange of X2 transport addresses) and X2Setup within 30 ms. TimeToWait values are 1, 2, 5, 10, 20 and 60 s; it exists so thousands of eNBs do not hammer a recovering MME.
6. NAS cause #9. TAU reject and Service reject with EMM cause #9 "UE identity cannot be derived by the network" follow the resets and the S1 re-setup. The core lost the UE's context (GUTI unknown), so the UE must do a fresh attach: in the capture each #9 reject is followed within about 0.3-0.4 s by an Attach request with Authentication and Security Mode Command. That is where the 10-18 full attaches come from (PD05 for the cause codes).
Level 3 — triage verdict, filters and what a test should assert
Verdict: a core/transport-side instability, not an eNB radio problem. Evidence: every Reset originates from the MME side; the first cause is a transport cause; the eNB acknowledges promptly, obeys TimeToWait exactly and re-sets up S1 and X2 correctly; ICS never fails once the core accepts the UE. Route the ticket to the core/gateway team with the counts above, and ask for the SGW/gateway logs (GTP path failures, echo timeouts, restarts) at the Reset timestamps. The eNB's only checkable behaviours are correct: ResetAck, local release, T-to-wait back-off.

GoalWireshark display filter
all Resets / only per-UE oness1ap.procedureCode == 14  /  s1ap.partOfS1_Interface
Resets by causes1ap.transport == 0 (transport-resource-unavailable), s1ap.misc == 4 (unspecified), s1ap.misc == 3 (om-intervention)
S1 Setup and its back-offs1ap.procedureCode == 17, s1ap.TimeToWait
Service Request vs successnas_eps.security_header_type == 12 || s1ap.procedureCode == 9 (header type 12 = Service Request; procedure 9 = ICS)
NAS cause #9 rejectsnas_eps.emm.cause == 9
Tip: make an IO graph of Resets per minute next to Service Requests per minute. A Reset storm shows as a regular comb; OAM restarts show as a burst.

Pass/fail rules for a stability or soak test: Resets from the core = 0 (or each one explained by a planned OAM action); S1 Setup succeeds on the first attempt; if a TimeToWait is received the eNB retries no earlier than the timer; Service Request → ICS success ≥ 98 %; no NAS cause #9 rejects; paging continues after recovery. A run like this one fails the soak, but with a core-side root cause, so it should not be booked against the eNB build.

Pitfalls: (1) Counting ICS Responses as "successful service requests" includes attaches, so correct for them. (2) A per-UE Reset is not an S1 link failure; the SCTP association and other UEs are fine. Do not call it "S1 down". (3) Check whether the two files overlap in time before adding their counts together.
Interview Q&A
Q: What is the difference between an s1-Interface and a partOfS1-Interface Reset?
A: s1-Interface resets everything: all UE-associated connections on that S1 are released. partOfS1-Interface lists specific UEs by their S1AP IDs, and only those are released. Either side can send it, and the receiver answers with ResetAcknowledge.
Q: Service Request success is 50 % but there are no InitialContextSetupFailures. How?
A: The failures happen before ICS. The core answers the Service Request with a per-UE Reset, so no ICS is ever attempted. The UE retries after T3417 (5 s) and then succeeds. Count Service Requests against ICS Responses, not ICS Requests against Responses.
Q: What should an eNB do when it receives S1SetupFailure with TimeToWait = 20 s?
A: Wait at least 20 s before sending another S1SetupRequest to that MME. In the capture it retried after exactly 20.000 s and succeeded. Retrying sooner would violate TS 36.413 and add load to an MME that is recovering.
Q: Why do TAU and Service rejects with cause #9 appear after a core reset?
A: Cause #9 means the network cannot derive the UE identity: the core lost the context behind the UE's GUTI. The UE deletes its GUTI and security context and re-attaches with IMSI, which is why full attaches with Authentication and Security Mode follow.
Q: How do you decide whether a Reset storm is the eNB's fault?
A: Check who sends the Resets, their cause class, and what happens just before them. Resets from the core with a transport cause, no eNB-initiated releases before them, prompt ResetAcks and correct TimeToWait handling all point away from the eNB and toward the core or transport.
NAS failures
PDNAS 5G NAS Cause Codes — 5GMM, 5GSM and authentication failures: what each means, how the UE reacts, first checks Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
When the 5G core says "no", it almost always says why, using a numbered cause inside a NAS message. There are two families. 5GMM causes come from the AMF and are about the UE being allowed on the network at all: identity, subscription, area, slices, congestion. 5GSM causes come from the SMF and are about a specific data pipe: DNN, session type, resources. A third small group covers authentication failures that the UE sends to the network. Read the number and it points you straight at the right config. PD05 covers the LTE EMM codes. This card is the 5G equivalent, with what the UE does next and what to check first.
Start from the message type
