#38 network failureInternal failure (UDM, PCF or UPF unreachable)NF health, N4 association, SBI timeouts
#43 invalid PDU session identity / #54 PDU session does not existState mismatchPSI handling, PDU session status after idle
#46 out of LADN service areaDNN restricted to certain TAsLADN TAI config
#50 IPv4 only allowed / #51 IPv6 only allowedType narrowed (can arrive in an Accept)Not an error if the session came up
#59 unsupported 5QI valueRequested 5QI not supportedQoS rules, PCF policy
#67 / #69 insufficient resources for slice + DNN / for sliceSlice quota reached (back-off T3584 / T3585)Slice admission and quotas
#68 not supported SSC modeRequested SSC mode refusedUE URSP vs SMF allowed modes
#70 missing or unknown DNN in a sliceDNN not configured for that S-NSSAIDNN ↔ S-NSSAI mapping in SMF/UDM
#82 max data rate for UP integrity protection too lowPolicy needs full-rate UP integrity, UE supports only 64 kbit/sUP security policy (use preferred), see PX10
#36 regular deactivation / #39 reactivation requestedUsed in Release Command, not a fault—
#95–#111 protocol errorsMalformed or unexpected messageSoftware interop, capture both sides
  • Timers you will see around rejects (24.501 §10.2): T3510 = 15 s registration guard; T3511 = 10 s retry; after 5 failed attempts T3502 (default 12 min); T3346 = congestion back-off from #22; T3580 = 16 s for PDU session establishment (UE resends, then gives up); T3396 / T3584 / T3585 = DNN / slice+DNN / slice back-off after 5GSM rejects. A UE that "does nothing for 12 minutes" after a few rejects is running T3502, not frozen.
  • Where to find the cause: RRC DLInformationTransfer → dedicatedNAS-Message, or NGAP DownlinkNASTransport → NAS-PDU. After security activation NAS is ciphered (unless NEA0 in the lab), so use UE-side logs or core traces, or give Wireshark the keys. A Registration Reject before security is in clear text.
  • Do not confuse NAS causes with NGAP causes (radioNetwork / transport / nas / protocol / misc). An NGAP UE Context Release with cause nas "deregister" is the consequence. The NAS message before it holds the reason.
Level 3 — real-world patterns
  • #62 on a fresh lab setup: the UE asked for SST 1 / SD 0x000001, the gNB advertised SST 1 without SD in NG Setup, and the subscriber had only SST 1 with an SD. The AMF found no allowed slice and rejected. Align S-NSSAI (SST and SD) across UE, gNB, AMF/NSSF and UDM. NS01 covers slicing.
  • #20 after SIM re-provisioning: the new SIM batch used an OPc while the UDM entry had OP (or the reverse). Every attempt ended in MAC failure. #20 is almost never a radio issue. Compare the key-material flags, not the RF.
  • #26 non-5G authentication: a combined HSS/UDM generated the vector through the EPS path without the separation bit. The UE refused it. It shows up only on SA and never on NSA, which is a giveaway.
  • Registration OK, #90/#91 on every PDU session: the SMF had not registered in the NRF for that DNN/S-NSSAI after an upgrade. The AMF could not select it (see PX09). Fix the SMF NF profile, not the gNB.
  • Accept with #50 read as failure: a test script flagged every IPv4v6 request as failed because the accept carried a cause. It was a narrowed type and a working session. Scripts should check the message type (Accept vs Reject) before the cause.
  • #9/#10 after a core restart: the UE's 5G-GUTI points to a context that no longer exists. A clean re-registration is the correct behaviour. Repeated loops mean the UE keeps offering the old GUTI, so check UE firmware and the core's handling of unknown GUTIs.
Further reading
Techplayon — 5G NAS registration reject causes · PDU session reject causes · Authentication failure causes
Specs: TS 24.501 §5.5.1 (registration), §5.4.1 (authentication), §6.4 (5GSM), §9.11.3.2 (5GMM cause), §9.11.4.2 (5GSM cause), §10.2 (timers), Annex A/B · TS 33.501 §6.1.3 · TS 38.413 §9.3.1.2 (NGAP cause)
Interview Q&A
Q: A UE gets Registration Reject #15 on one cell but registers on another. RF problem?
A: No. #15 means no suitable cells in that tracking area: the TAC broadcast by that cell is not in the TAI list or areas the core allows. Compare the SIB1 TAC with the AMF config.
Q: What is the difference between Authentication Failure #20 and #21?
A: #20 is a MAC failure: the UE could not verify the network (wrong K/OPc or a fake network). #21 is a synch failure: the MAC was fine but the SQN was out of range, so the UE sends AUTS and the UDM resynchronises. One #21 is normal. Repeated #20 is a provisioning fault.
Q: PDU session reject #27 vs #70?
A: #27: the DNN is missing or unknown at all. #70: the DNN exists but not in the requested slice (S-NSSAI). Check the DNN spelling first, then the DNN–slice mapping.
Q: Why does the UE stop trying for about 12 minutes after some registration rejects?
A: After 5 failed attempts it starts T3502 (default 12 min). With #22 it uses the T3346 back-off given by the network.
Q: Can the gNB see why the core rejected a UE?
A: Not from RRC fields. It relays NAS transparently. You have to decode the NAS-PDU in the RRC or NGAP container, and it may be ciphered.
PHY logs & fronthaul capture
PHYDBG01 5G PHY Pod Logs & Crash Reading — l1.sh.log bring-up milestones, nanolog decompress, real crash vs self-stop vs controlled restart Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
In a cloud-native 5G DU the PHY (the gnb_app process) runs in its own pod and talks to the L2 stack through a shared-memory interface (WLS) using FAPI messages. When a cell will not come up or the PHY "crashes", the first question is not where in the code, but which kind of stop it was: a genuine PHY crash (a PHY defect), a self-stop because the stack stopped feeding it (usually a DU problem), or a controlled restart requested by the DU (not a bug at all). The signal name and two or three log lines tell you which, in a minute, if you know where to look.

This card walks the PHY pod log tree, the bring-up milestones in l1.sh.log, how to decompress the binary PHY logs, and the three stop signatures. Related: AUTO14 (crash triage by component), AUTO19/AUTO20 (starting the PHY), CM34 (FAPI P5/P7).
Level 1 — where the logs are and what a healthy bring-up looks like
Log tree on the PHY pod (from inside the pod under /var/log/, on the host under the shared pod-log folder for the PHY node):
File / folderWhat it holdsUse it for
messagespod syslog: gnb_app start / stop / killed timestamps, pod bring-upwhen did the PHY start and end, was it killed
nrlogs/l1.sh.logconsole printf of gnb_app — live cell statebring-up milestones and the stop signature (start here)
phylogs/CellStats, FhStats, PhyStats, PrachStats, UeStats, SlotRecperiodic statisticsthroughput, fronthaul counters, PRACH, per-UE view
phylogs/gnb_phy_rawlog_*the real PHY module log, nanolog-compresseddetail around the failure; size/count set in param_gnb_phy.json (default 5 MB × up to 3 files)
phylogs/gnb_phy_errors_rawlog_*error-only subset, same formatquick scan for errors
phylogs/crash.logsignal and crash detailswhich thread, which signal
Bring-up milestones in l1.sh.log (in order):
#You seeMeaning / failure at this step
1"Start l1.sh", old logs cleared (+ private binary copied, if one is used)a fresh start; private-binary lines only appear when testing a private build
2PHY commit id and daterecord this in every bug report
3WLS init, instance wls0202 (2nd/3rd PHY pod: wls0303, wls0404)SIG6 here = the previous WLS instance was not released, usually by the stack side
4repeating "waiting for stack" printsnormal wait loop, not an error
5stack starts its WLS side → PHY starts a 300 s timerif the stack is not up in 300 s, PHY goes back to waiting: check why the DU did not come up
6CONFIG.request received, radio config applied, fronthaul link up via the xRAN library (version printed)fronthaul or config problems surface here
7CONFIG.response → START.requeststack accepted the config and asked for transmission
8RNTI / slot printsthe cell is running
Figure — classify the stop before you debug it
A fourth class: OOM-monitor kill or SIGTERM ends the process with no core and no crash.log.
Level 2 — collecting, decompressing and classifying a PHY stop
Get the logs the right way. Do not debug inside the live pod: the PHY manager uploads a log bundle (zlogs), cleans the log folder and recovers the cell, so files vanish under you. Use the collected bundle instead:
# from the cluster log collection, PHY node folder:
#   opt/      -> core dump, if one was written
#   staging/  -> logs/zlogs.5g-du...phynode02-<hash>.<date>-<time>.tgz  and the L1 binaries
#   var/      -> messages (syslog)
tar xzf zlogs.5g-du.*phynode02*<date>*.tgz
ls l1/        # gnb_app, gnb_app_with_symbols, libs, configs - use the symbols build with gdb
ls logs/      # l1.sh.log, CellStats, PhyStats, ..., gnb_phy_rawlog_*, crash.log

# decompress nanolog raw logs (tool ships with the PHY build, in the gnb tools dir)
mkdir err && mv logs/gnb_phy_errors_rawlog* err/      # error logs must be in their own folder
./tool_log_decompress logs/
./tool_log_decompress err/
Read the end of l1.sh.log and classify:
ClassLines just before the stopSignal / exitNext step
Real crashred "usec between TTIs exceeds 10% limitation", "id slot mismatch phy/radio", "<thread> [PIPELINE FAULT UNFINISHED]", then handle_gnb_crash naming the thread (e.g. Ul/pusch)SIGSEGV or SIGABRT; details in phylogs/crash.log; core in opt/PHY defect candidate: gdb on gnb_app_with_symbols + core, decompressed rawlog around the time
Self-stop (starved)"no stack messages for 5 TTI, set WLS debug" → "no stack messages for 40 TTI, stop PHY"; crash handler on the Dl/stack_if threadalways SIGINT40 ms with no FAPI from the stack: read DU logs for why it stopped sending ("l1 tick stopped" in the DU log means the PHY itself was stuck)
Controlled restart"Destination is empty ... is remote detached?" then banner "PHY EXITING AFTER STOP.request RX'ed / CONTROLLED RESTART – ZLOGS NOT REQUIRED"gnb_app EXIT status = 0not a PHY bug: find why the DU (pod restart from EMS or kubectl) stopped the PHY
Killedlines simply stop; messages shows the killOOM monitor or SIGTERM, no corememory / orchestration, not PHY code
Level 3 — why the PHY self-crashes, pitfalls and a real pattern
Why the PHY "crashes" on purpose. The PHY is designed to run forever; it has no clean exit path. When it loses its stack (no FAPI for 40 TTIs) or receives STOP.request, it triggers a self-crash so that the PHY manager restarts it and recovers the cell. That is why a crash.log and even a SIGINT handler trace can appear when the PHY is not at fault — and why the signal is the classifier.

Pitfalls
  • Treating the "waiting for stack" loop as an error: it is the normal idle state until the DU starts WLS.
  • Blaming the PHY for SIG6 at WLS init: the previous WLS instance was not released, usually by the stack; restart order matters.
  • Decompressing error rawlogs together with normal rawlogs: the tool needs them in separate folders.
  • Using the stripped gnb_app with the core: take gnb_app_with_symbols from the same bundle (same commit) or the backtrace is useless.
  • Ignoring the commit id printed at start: crash fixes are matched by PHY commit, so a bug report without it is hard to act on.
  • Self-stop is usually a DU issue, but not always: if the DU says the L1 tick stopped, the PHY was stuck and the DU only noticed.
Real-case pattern: a UL PUSCH pipeline thread fell behind (TTI gaps over the 10% limit, slot-id mismatches between PHY and radio climbing into the dozens), the pipeline reported an unfinished fault and the crash handler fired on Ul/pusch with SIGSEGV — a genuine PHY defect, unlike the SIGINT self-stops on Dl/stack_if seen when the DU stalled.
Interview Q&A
Q: The PHY pod restarted. What is the first thing you check?
A: The last lines of l1.sh.log and the signal: SIGSEGV/SIGABRT means a PHY crash, SIGINT after "no stack messages for 40 TTI" means the stack starved the PHY, and the CONTROLLED RESTART banner with exit status 0 means the DU asked it to stop.
Q: Why should you not debug inside the live PHY pod?
A: The PHY manager uploads the logs, cleans the folder and restarts the cell, so the evidence disappears. Work from the collected zlogs bundle.
Q: What does a SIG6 at WLS initialisation usually mean?
A: The previous WLS shared-memory instance was not released, most often by the stack side, so the new PHY cannot create its instance.
Q: The PHY prints "waiting for stack" for minutes. Is that a PHY bug?
A: No, it is the wait loop until the DU starts its WLS side. Once the stack starts, the PHY waits up to 300 s for its initialisation; if that expires, look at why the DU did not come up.
Q: How do you read the PHY raw logs?
A: They are nanolog-compressed; run tool_log_decompress on the folder, with the error rawlogs moved to a separate folder first.
Addendum — "it works on the other setup": diff the PHY pod environment
Much of a PHY pod's identity is injected as environment variables by the deployment (VRU_* on the PW 2G/4G PHY). When a PHY that runs fine on one setup misbehaves on another, a quick, high-yield step before reading any log is to dump those variables on both and compare — the 2G PHY team turned this into a filterable "reference vs configured" report with a Match/Differ flag per key.
# dump the PHY pod environment on the good and the bad setup, then diff
kubectl -n pw exec <phy-pod> -- env | grep '^VRU_' | sort > bad.env
diff good.env bad.env
Variable groupWhat it pins downTypical symptom if it differs
VRU_NUM_PHYS_CORES, VRU_NUM_LOGICAL_CPUSWhat the PHY believes about the server (hyper-threading on/off, socket count)Wrong core plan, real-time threads on shared cores
VRU_GENPURP_CPU_MASK, VRU_ISOL_CPU_MASK, VRU_HALTED_CPU_MASKWhich cores are housekeeping, isolated real-time, or parkedLate slot/TTI processing, timing faults under load
VRU_NUM_VNODES, VRU_VNODE_ID*, VRU_VNODE_NUM_CELLS*, VRU_RAT_MODE, VRU_RAT_SPLITHow many vNodes/cells and which RAT this PHY instance servesA cell never configured, or the PHY waiting for a vNode that does not exist
VRU_STACK_TO_PHY_IP*, VRU_PHY_TO_STACK_IP, VRU_BBU_ICN_ADDRInternal link-local addresses between stack and PHYStack and PHY never connect; no config request arrives
VRU_VNODEn_RRH0_ID, _NIC_PF, _UPLANE_NIC_PCI_ADDR, _NUM_OF_BANDS, _NUM_OF_CARRIERS, _HW_ANTSWhich radio, on which NIC port, with how many bands/carriers/antennasNo fronthaul packets, wrong antenna count, carrier missing
..._UPLANE_RU_MAC_ADDR, ..._SPLANE_RRH_MAC_ADDRU-plane / S-plane peer addresses of the radioU-plane sent to the wrong MAC — RU sees nothing
VRU_HW_FEC_PCI_ADDRESSHardware FEC accelerator deviceDecoder falls back or fails to start
Ignore the Kubernetes *_SERVICE_* / *_PORT_* variables: cluster service addresses change on every deployment and only add noise. Also expect legitimate differences (core counts on a bigger server, RRH IDs and MAC addresses of different radios) — the question is whether each difference is consistent with the hardware, e.g. a 64-core reading on a 32-core server with hyper-threading off is a red flag; a different RU MAC is only a problem if it does not match the radio actually cabled to that NIC port.
Addendum — zlog bundles, decoding half-written logs, fh_faults.log and changing PHY log levels from the EMS
The PHY manager's log collection packs everything into a bundle named zlogs.5g-du.vru_node.<date-time>.tgz, found under /opt/pw/logs/ inside the pod and under a per-phynode folder of the same path on the host. A few details the walkthrough above does not cover:
  • Half-finished bundles. If the pod crashed while the bundle was being built, untarring the top level leaves raw *.args* files instead of readable logs. Decode them with tool_log_decompress (shipped in the PHY tools directory under the nr5g gnb install): copy it into the phylogs directory and run it there. It needs the .dict file and the thread-name log in the same directory, and it may refuse to run while *_errors_rawlog_* files are present — move those to their own folder first. Output appears as decompressed.log files.
  • The first rawlog is special. gnb_phy_rawlog_<id>.args1 is kept even after later rawlogs rotate away, because it holds the start-up information. If the failure is at bring-up, start there.
  • fh_faults.log (in the bundle's l1/ folder): fronthaul faults reported by the xRAN library with set/clear times — faults that persist long enough become alarms passed to the stack — plus the history of PHY starts and restarts. The same folder carries gnb_app_with_symbol_info, the binary to open a core with, and the PHY's libraries and config files; version.txt gives the PHY version and messages keeps several rotations of PHY-manager history.
  • Log levels. l1.sh.log is the PHY's stdout and its level is fixed. The module rawlogs start at the levels in param_gnb_phy.json (globally or per module). After the CONFIG request/response exchange the EMS can override them — only if the stack_socket setting in that file is true — via the DU device configuration: DU tab → log level configuration → PHY function → PhyLayer. Modules usually raised: PDCCH, PUCCH, PUSCH, PDSCH, PRACH, SCF, SCF_DL, SCF_UL.
  • Stats CSVs (one per thread: CellStats with throughput, PrachStats, SlotRec with per-TTI configuration, UeStats) are switched on and their period set in param_gnb_phy.json. Raising their level or rate costs real CPU in the PHY — turn them up only for the test that needs them.
FHCAP01 Capturing fronthaul and FAPI traffic — passive fibre tap, tcpdump vs DPDK capture, and L1/L2 FAPI message mirroring to pcap Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
When a DU and an O-RU disagree — wrong VLAN, wrong eAxC ID, late U-plane, odd compression — the fastest proof is to look at the packets on the fibre. But fronthaul is special: the NIC ports are owned by DPDK (invisible to Linux), traffic runs at many Gbit/s in jumbo eCPRI frames, and the L1↔L2 FAPI messages never touch a network at all (they go through shared memory).
This card collects three practical techniques:
1. Passive fibre tap — a 50/50 optical splitter in the DU–RU fibre copies the light to two extra "sniff" NIC ports without breaking the link.
2. Choosing the capture engine — tcpdump on a kernel driver for low-rate traffic (M-plane, PTP, C-plane), a DPDK capture application for full-rate U-plane.
3. FAPI mirroring — ask the L2 stack to copy every L1/L2 (SCF FAPI P5/P7) message to a TCP port and write it into a pcap, so you can read DL_TTI / UL_TTI / CRC / UCI indications in Wireshark.
Passive tap between DU and RU
Each sniff port receives one direction; the tap itself is unpowered and invisible to DU and RU.
Level 1 — core facts
TechniqueSeesLimits
Optical splitter + tcpdump (kernel driver)Everything on the fibre: eCPRI C/U-plane, PTP, M-plane, DHCPKernel drops packets at fronthaul line rate; fine for PTP / M-plane / C-plane, not for full U-plane
Optical splitter + DPDK capture appFull-rate U-planeNeeds a DPDK-bound port (igb_uio or vfio-pci) and enough disk bandwidth
FAPI (L1/L2) mirroringDL_TTI, UL_TTI, TX_DATA, RX_DATA, CRC, UCI, RACH indications, CONFIG/STARTDebug only: costs L2 time; only what crosses the L1/L2 interface
Capture on the DU FH port itselfNothing (port owned by DPDK)Use the tap or the DPDK app, not tcpdump on the DU
Splitter facts: the lab coupler is a 50/50 passive part for 850 nm / 1300 nm multimode links; each pass costs a little over 3 dB of optical power on the live path. Use a single-mode splitter for single-mode (1310/1550 nm) links — match the fibre type, just like the SFP rules in the bench checklist.
Frame size: eCPRI U-plane uses jumbo frames, so the sniff port needs MTU 9200 or large frames are dropped/truncated before they reach the capture.
Direction: a sniff port sees only the direction its splitter copies; to correlate a C-plane request with the U-plane it schedules (DL) or the UL response, capture both ports and merge by timestamp.
Level 2 — capture recipes
A. Low-rate fronthaul with tcpdump. Make sure the sniff port has a normal kernel driver, open it up, capture:
dpdk-devbind.py --status                    # which driver owns which port
dpdk-devbind.py --bind i40e <pci_addr>       # give the sniff port back to the kernel (ice for E810)
ip link set mtu 9200 dev <sniff_if>          # jumbo eCPRI frames
ip link set promisc on dev <sniff_if>        # frames are not addressed to us
ip link set up dev <sniff_if>
tcpdump -i <sniff_if> -s 0 -w dl.pcap 'ether proto 0xAEFE or ether proto 0x88F7 or vlan'
#   0xAEFE = eCPRI, 0x88F7 = PTP over Ethernet; "vlan" keeps tagged frames
tcpdump ... 2>&1 | grep "dropped by kernel"  # non-zero = use the DPDK capture instead
B. Full-rate U-plane with a DPDK capture app. Bind the sniff port to a DPDK driver (dpdk-devbind.py --bind igb_uio <pci_addr> or vfio-pci) and run the DPDK packet-capture tool; it polls the port in user space and writes pcap without kernel drops. Pin it to an isolated core and write to fast local storage.

C. FAPI L1/L2 message mirroring (DU stack).
# in the DU data-model XML of the stack, e.g.
#   /opt/pw/nrstack/exec/gNB_DU/cfg/Proprietary_gNodeB_DU_Data_Model.xml
<L1L2InterfaceLogLevel>4</L1L2InterfaceLogLevel>
pkill gnb_du_layer2                          # L2 restarts and reads the new level
# P5/P7 messages are mirrored to TCP port 38666; a tcpdump on that port writes
ls -l /var/run/nrlogs/l1l2.pcap0
Set the level back afterwards — mirroring every TTI costs real-time budget.

D. Reading the captures. Recent Wireshark versions decode eCPRI and the O-RAN fronthaul C/U-plane headers (enable the O-RAN dissector and give it the bit-width/compression if it cannot infer them). Check, in order:
CheckWhere in the packetTypical finding
VLAN ID / PCP802.1Q tagRU expects VLAN 1, DU sends 3 (or the reverse)
eAxC ID (DU port / band-sector / CC / RU port)eCPRI pc_id / ecpriRtcidDL ports 0-3, UL 0-1, PRACH on its own IDs; wrong CC bits = wrong carrier
Section type, startPrb / numPrb, symbolC-plane section headerPRB range does not match the cell bandwidth
udCompHdr (bit width, method)C/U-planeBFP 9 vs 16-bit mismatch = garbled IQ at RU
Frame / subframe / slot / symbol vs arrival timeApplication header + capture timestampPackets outside the receive window (see CM19)
Level 3 — pitfalls and expert notes
  • Timestamps: sniff-port timestamps come from the capture host clock, not the RU. To judge early/late arrival against CM19 windows the capture host should be PTP-synced with hardware timestamping (tcpdump -j adapter_unsynced on a capable NIC), otherwise compare relative timing only.
  • Two directions, two files: merge with mergecap -w all.pcap dl.pcap ul.pcap; both ports must share a clock or the merged order lies.
  • Optical margin: the splitter loss can push a marginal link (long fibre, dirty connector) over the edge — if the link flaps after inserting the tap, clean and check Rx power before blaming the DU.
  • Absence of evidence: a kernel capture that shows "no U-plane" may simply have dropped it; check the drop counter or use the DPDK path before concluding the DU did not send.
  • FAPI mirror vs IQ capture: the FAPI pcap tells you what L2 asked L1 to do and what L1 reported back (CRC, UCI, RACH); pair it with the PHY uplink IQ capture (ULIQCAP01) to see the radio input for the same slots. FAPI captures can also be converted into PHY test vectors.
  • Control plane above F1/NG is a different job: see PDCAP for reading NGAP/F1AP captures.
Interview Q&A
Q: Why can you not just run tcpdump on the DU fronthaul port?
A: The port is bound to a DPDK driver and owned by the user-space fronthaul service, so the kernel never sees its packets. Use a passive tap or a DPDK-based capture.
Q: Each sniff NIC behind the optical splitter only sees half the traffic. Why?
A: A fibre pair carries one direction per strand; each splitter copies one strand, so one sniff port gets DU to RU and the other RU to DU.
Q: What MTU do you set on a sniff port for eCPRI and why?
A: Around 9200, because U-plane frames are jumbo frames; with 1500 they would be dropped or truncated.
Q: How do you capture SCF FAPI messages between L1 and L2?
A: Raise the L1/L2 interface log level (4) in the DU data model, restart L2; the stack mirrors P5/P7 messages to a TCP port and a pcap is written for Wireshark.
Q: Your capture shows U-plane packets well before the C-plane that schedules them. What do you check?
A: Capture timestamps/sync first; if valid, compare with the DU transmit window (T1a) and RU receive window (Ta4) and the RU early/late counters (CM19).
Logs & proof — MAC-TTI, UE logs, is the cell really up?
MACTTI01 Reading the LTE FDD MAC-TTI log — capture with session_capture.sh, DCI/UL_CONF/PHY_SDU/PHICH records, following HARQ, SINR/TPC/RNTI decoding, RBG bitmap to PRBs, UL RLC segmentation Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
The MAC-TTI log (mac_tti) is the eNB's diary written once every millisecond: every grant the scheduler sent on the PDCCH, every PUSCH and PUCCH the PHY received, every ACK/NACK, every CQI/RI report and every RACH preamble. Counters tell you that throughput is low; this log tells you why — small grants, repeated CRC failures, DTX on DL HARQ, a low rank, a UE starved of grants because its SR is never seen.

The trick is that one transmission is spread over several lines in different subframes. In LTE FDD the links are fixed: a UL grant is followed by the PUSCH exactly 4 TTI later and the PHICH exactly 4 TTI after that; a DL grant is answered by HARQ feedback exactly 4 TTI later. Once you know the record names and those offsets, you can follow one UE (one RNTI) and one HARQ process at a time and reconstruct exactly what happened on the air.

This card covers: getting a clean capture with session_capture.sh, the record catalogue, following UL and DL HARQ, decoding SINR/TPC/RNTI, turning the DL RBG bitmap into PRBs, and the newer UL RLC segmentation fields. Related: PD03 (MAC debugging), PT06 (HARQ), PX08 (UL scheduling rules), PHYDBG01 (PHY pod logs).
LTE FDD record timing in the MAC-TTI log
UL: trigger → DCI:0 → UL_CONF_LOG + PHY_SDU_LOG (same TTI) → PHICH-INFO. DL: DCI_LOG → PHY_SDU_LOG Msg:HARQ.
Level 1 — record catalogue, line anatomy and value decoding
Where it comes from. The stack writes rotating binary regions MAC_RLC_REGION0[0-6].bin per vNode. merge_mac_tti_logs.sh (which calls the decoder ltebinread_x64, shipped with the logs) turns them into MAC_MERGED_TextLogs/merged_MAC_RLC_REGION*.bin.txt. The regions are consecutive pieces of one timeline — read them in order.

Line header. Every record is one line: UTC timestamp, [Tti:n] (absolute 1 ms counter), record tag, SFN 0–1023, SF 0–9, RNTI, CELL, FLAG (CRNTI for a connected UE, TC-RNTI during random access), then key:value fields.
2026-07-19 10:59:40.016613 [Tti:4767836], DCI_LOG, SFN:624, SF:7, RNTI:476, CELL:1, FLAG:CRNTI, DCI:2A, MCS1:27, ...
<---- UTC time ----------> <-- TTI -->  <tag>  <frame> <sf> <UE>    <cell> <rnti type> <fields...>
RecordDirWhat it means
DCI_LOGUL/DLA grant on the PDCCH. DCI:0 = UL grant; DCI:1A / 2 / 2A = DL grant
UL_CONF_LOGULStack tells the PHY to receive a PUSCH — one per UL transmission, new or retransmission
UCI_LOGULStack tells the PHY which PUCCH to expect (HARQ, SR, CQI)
PHY_SDU_LOGULWhat actually arrived. Msg: ULSCH (PUSCH OK, DataLen bytes), CRC (PUSCH failed, CrcFlag:BAD), HARQ (UE's DL ACK/NACK), SR, CQI CL / RI CL
PHICH-INFODLOur ACK (ack:1) or NACK (ack:0) for a PUSCH
CQI_LOGULDecoded periodic CQI or RI report
PHY_STATUS_LOGULMsg:RACH preamble detected: PreambleId, timingAdvance, peakToAvgPowerRatio
MSG2_LOGDLRandom Access Response: TC-RNTI, TA correction, Msg3 grant (GrantType:MSG3); RaPid = PreambleId
Encoded valueDecodeExample
UlCqi / Ulcqi (PUSCH or PUCCH SINR)dB = (value − 128) / 2181 → 26.5 dB
AvgSnr (on DCI:0)dB = ((value / 100) − 128) / 217896 → 25.5 dB
peakToAvgPowerRatio (RACH)linear → 10·log10(value)122 → 20.9 dB
TPC index0 / 1 / 2 / 3 = −1 / 0 / +1 / +3 dBaccumulated PUSCH/PUCCH power step
ULBSR, DLBO, DataLen, TB1Size/TB2SizebytesDataLen:185 = 185 bytes
DL HARQ TB statusACK[1], NACK[2], DTX[7], INVALID[0]INVALID = TB not present
RV sequence0 (new) → 2 → 3 → 1shows how many retx so far
is256QAM:1read MCS against the 256QAM tableflags the table, not the modulation; DCI:1A always uses the 64QAM table
RNTI tells you the PCell. A UE keeps its PCell RNTI on every cell, and each cell hands out RNTIs from its own range: cell 0 104–10000, cell 1 10104–20000, cell 2 20104–30000, cell 3 30104–40000. A record on CELL:3 with RNTI 476 is therefore an SCell allocation for a UE anchored on cell 0. The same RNTI/10000 bucket trick is handy when indexing logs by cell.
Level 2 — capture, then follow HARQ, CSI, RACH and PRBs
1. Capture without losing anything — session_capture.sh. The MAC regions rotate quickly, so copying them by hand after reproducing an issue often misses the interesting seconds. The session_capture.sh collector (si-tools, LTE4G-24012) is a start/stop recorder for a vRAN BBU:
sudo ./session_capture.sh start --name DL-FTP --vnode 1   # returns at once; reproduce the issue
sudo ./session_capture.sh status                         # RECORDING/idle, elapsed, size, bins, lteStats heartbeat
sudo ./session_capture.sh stop                           # decode + package one verified .tgz
BehaviourDetail
MAC-TTI binssnapshots the rotating MAC_RLC_REGION*.bin every 5 s (--interval-bin) per vNode
UE rate/link statsevery 2 s (--interval-stats) runs lteStats showuerate / showuelink via kubectl exec; output lands in ltelogs/lteStats.log
On stopsnapshots the other pod logs, decodes MAC-TTI (merge_mac_tti_logs.sh) and dbgLog (rl2ds_lnx), writes session_info.txt, builds one archive
Archivevnode0N/mac_tti, stats, rlog, pcap/controlplane.pcap, pods_snapshot/; decoders bundled so it also decodes offline
Safetyauto-finalises after --max-hours 2 or --max-disk-pct 70; niced + ionice idle; single instance (flock); never writes or deletes the live ltelogs
Gotchasmust run as root (kubectl uses the admin kubeconfig); --vnode d maps to pods named vnode0d-... in namespace pw; omit it to capture all running vNodes
A typical verified run: 56/56 stats polls succeeded and a ~109 MB archive decoded cleanly.

2. Follow an UL HARQ process. Pick one RNTI, then one Ulharq.
Tti:4767830 PHY_SDU_LOG RNTI:478 Msg:ULSCH DataLen:120 ULBSR:15        <- BSR>0: UE still has data
Tti:4767832 DCI_LOG     RNTI:478 DCI:0 MCS1:28 RBs:2 RBStart:3 Ulharq:5 NDI:1   <- new grant (NDI toggled)
Tti:4767836 UL_CONF_LOG RNTI:478 pduType:PUSCH_DATA_HARQ RV:0 NDI:1 Ulharq:5   <- +4: PHY told to receive
Tti:4767836 PHY_SDU_LOG RNTI:478 Msg:ULSCH DataLen:185 UlCqi:181 TimingAdv:31  <- same TTI: CRC OK, 26.5 dB
Tti:4767840 PHICH-INFO  RNTI:478 ack:1 rbStart:3                              <- +4: ACK, rbStart == RBStart
Rules: a new transmission = DCI:0 + UL_CONF_LOG with RV 0 and NDI toggled. A retransmission is almost always non-adaptive: no new DCI:0, just another UL_CONF_LOG for the same Ulharq, NDI unchanged, RV 0→2→3→1. An adaptive retx does carry a new DCI:0 (same HARQ, same NDI). Msg:CRC means NACK will follow. Grant triggers are either Msg:SR on PUCCH or ULBSR>0 inside the previous PUSCH.

3. Follow a DL HARQ process. The PDSCH goes out in the same subframe as the DCI; feedback arrives as PHY_SDU_LOG Msg:HARQ exactly 4 TTI later, matched on (RNTI, Harq). DCI:1A = one TB; DCI:2/2A = up to two codewords. A codeword with MCSx:0 and RVx:1 is cancelled — expect INVALID[0] for it, not a bug. Feedback may ride on PUCCH format 1A/1B/2A/2B or on PUSCH (IsPusch:1).
Tti:4767839 DCI_LOG     RNTI:478 DCI:2A MCS1:27 RV1:0 MCS2:27 RV2:0 BITMAP:0xfff80 TB1Size:6378 TB2Size:12237 Harq:5
Tti:4767843 PHY_SDU_LOG RNTI:478 Msg:HARQ NumTBs:2 TB1:ACK[1] TB2:ACK[1] Harq:5 IsPusch:0
UCI_LOG pduTypePUCCH formatCarries
PUCCH_HARQ1A / 1BDL ACK/NACK, 1 or 2 codewords
PUCCH_SR1Scheduling Request (optional — UE may not send)
PUCCH_HARQ_SR1BHARQ + SR
PUCCH_CQI2periodic CQI
PUCCH_HARQ_CQI2BHARQ + CQI
4. CSI. Periodic CSI on PUCCH appears as CQI_LOG; aperiodic CSI on PUSCH appears as UL_CONF_LOG with pduType:PUSCH_DATA_CQI* (RI Size:2 = an RI was reported, 0 = a CQI). CQI and RI arrive in different subframes: on an RI subframe WideBandCQICW0 is N/A and RI_bits is non-zero (1 = 2 antennas, rank 1–2; 2 = 4 antennas, rank 1–4). Ulcqi on a CQI_LOG is the PUCCH's own SINR, not the DL CQI.

5. Random access. PHY_STATUS_LOG Msg:RACH (PreambleId, TA, PAPR) → MSG2_LOG with the TC-RNTI, TA and the Msg3 grant; RaPid must equal the PreambleId. A preamble with no MSG2_LOG, or a Msg3 that only ever shows CRC, is your RACH failure point.

6. DL RBG bitmap → PRBs (DCI:2/2A only). Type-0 allocation (TS 36.213 §7.1.6.1) assigns resource-block groups of P PRBs: P = 1 (≤10 RB), 2 (11–26), 3 (27–63), 4 (64–110); NRBG = ceil(NRB/P) = 25 at 20 MHz. The log prints a 32-bit left-justified hex value: RBG k is bit 31−k, bits 6..0 are padding. Decode: for each set bit b, k = 31−b (ignore k≥25); PRBs 4k..4k+3, clamp at 99.
BITMAP (20 MHz)RBGsPRBs
0xffffff800–240–99 (full band)
0xe00000000–20–11
0x0000070021–2384–95
0x000fff8012–2448–99
0x800000800 and 240–3 and 96–99 (non-contiguous)
The RBs/RBStart printed on the same DCI:2/2A line are only a contiguous approximation — the bitmap is authoritative. DCI:0 and DCI:1A never carry a bitmap; they use a contiguous RIV (RBs + RBStart).
Level 3 — UL RLC segmentation fields, pitfalls and TDD
UL RLC segmentation fields (LTE4G-26177). For every UL PHY SDU the logger now also decodes the RLC header of the first RLC PDU in it and appends four fields to the PHY_SDU_LOG line:
FieldMeaning
RlcLcIdlogical channel of the bearer (e.g. 3 = first data DRB, 5 = VoLTE voice in the example)
RlcModeAM, UM, or AS (control/status PDU — no data SN)
RlcFiFraming Info: 0 whole SDU, 1 first segment, 2 last segment, 3 middle segment
RlcSnSN decoded at the bearer's configured length (5- or 10-bit, read from the RLC config); 65535 = none
... PHY_SDU_LOG, SFN:872, SF:2, RNTI:108, CELL:1, FLAG:CRNTI, Msg:ULSCH, DataLen:7, ..., RlcLcId:3, RlcMode:AM, RlcFi:3, RlcSn:312
Reading it: under good RF almost every SDU is FI 0. When the channel degrades or grants shrink to one or two RBs, the UE has to cut SDUs into pieces and FI 1/2/3 take over. Average segments per SDU = total PDUs ÷ (FI0 + FI1) — 1.0 means no segmentation. An SN above 31 proves a 10-bit bearer, because a 5-bit field cannot exceed 31. A quick per-mode summary:
cat MAC_MERGED_TextLogs/merged_*.bin.txt | awk '
/PHY_SDU_LOG/ && /RlcMode:/ {
  match($0,/RlcMode:[A-Za-z]+/); m=substr($0,RSTART+8,RLENGTH-8)
  match($0,/RlcFi:[0-9]+/);      fi=substr($0,RSTART+6,RLENGTH-6)+0
  match($0,/RlcSn:[0-9]+/);      sn=substr($0,RSTART+6,RLENGTH-6)+0
  n[m]++; if (sn>31 && sn!=65535) wide[m]++; if (fi!=0 && fi!=255) seg[m]++ }
END { for (x in n) printf "%s: n=%d SN>31=%d segmented=%d\n", x, n[x], wide[x]+0, seg[x]+0 }'
Real result: with the channel attenuated on purpose, about 99.8 % of AM SDUs and about 98.8 % of UM VoLTE SDUs were segmented, and both bearers ran SN 0–1023 (both configured 10-bit in RRC Reconfiguration).

Pitfalls
  • Exclude RlcSn:65535 and RlcMode:AS lines (FI 255) from SN and segmentation stats — they are sentinels.
  • AM is decoded as 10-bit only; bearers with extended 16-bit AM SN log a garbage SN/FI. Observability only — the read is bounds-guarded, no crash risk.
  • The 5-bit UM path had not yet been exercised in a capture; to prove it, configure a UM bearer with a 5-bit sn-FieldLength and check RlcSn stays ≤ 31.
  • Clean sequential 0..1023 is a real 10-bit counter; a mis-decode looks non-sequential.
  • PHICH-INFO has no HARQ field. If two UEs or two grants share a TTI, match on RNTI and rbStart, never on order of lines.
  • Many DTX[7] on DL HARQ usually means the UE missed the PDCCH (aggregation level / DL SINR) rather than failed the PDSCH.
  • Repeated UL_CONF_LOG with RV 2, 3, 1 and CRC each time, then a fresh NDI: the TB hit max retransmissions — RLC must recover it.

TDD and NR. Everything above about fixed +4 offsets is FDD. In LTE TDD the PUSCH delay after DCI:0, the PHICH delay and the DL HARQ feedback delay depend on the UL/DL configuration and the subframe (TS 36.213 Tables 8-2, 9.1.2-1 and 10.1.3.1-1; typically 4–7 subframes, with several DL subframes bundled or multiplexed into one UL ACK). NR uses K0/K1/K2 signalled per grant. Re-derive the matching rule before applying this method to a TDD or NR log.
Interview Q&A
Q: In an LTE FDD MAC-TTI log, how do you find the PHICH for a given PUSCH?
A: PHICH-INFO has no HARQ id. Take the PUSCH's TTI + 4, same RNTI, and match the PHICH rbStart to the PUSCH RBStart (the lowest PRB of the grant). ack:1 = ACK, ack:0 = NACK.
Q: You see UL_CONF_LOG lines for a HARQ process but no DCI:0 before them. Is something missing?
A: No. They are non-adaptive retransmissions: same HARQ, NDI unchanged, RV 2, 3, 1. Only new transmissions and adaptive retx carry a new DCI:0.
Q: UlCqi is 181. What is the UL SINR? And what does TPC index 3 mean?
A: (181 − 128)/2 = 26.5 dB. TPC 3 is a +3 dB power step (0/1/2/3 = −1/0/+1/+3 dB).
Q: A DCI:2A at 20 MHz shows BITMAP 0x80000080. Which PRBs are used?
A: Bits 31 and 7 are set → RBG 0 and RBG 24 → PRBs 0–3 and 96–99. Non-contiguous, so the printed RBs/RBStart are misleading; trust the bitmap.
Q: UE RNTI 476 appears on CELL:3. What does that tell you?
A: RNTIs 104–10000 belong to cell 0, so this UE's PCell is cell 0 and the CELL:3 grant is an SCell (carrier aggregation) allocation.
UELOG01 UE-side visibility without QXDM — SCAT diag to GSMTAP to Wireshark, and an ADB-only live RF/throughput dashboard Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
Network-side logs (MAC-TTI, PHY stats, S1/NG pcaps) tell you what the base station saw. Half of every call-flow problem, though, is on the phone: which RRC message it really decoded, why it rejected a reconfiguration, what RSRP it measured. The commercial way to see that is QXDM/QCAT on a Qualcomm handset (see UE01). It needs licences and a Windows PC per phone, so on a bench with many phones you often have nothing.

Two free alternatives cover most needs: SCAT, an open-source tool that reads the modem's diagnostic stream over USB and turns it into GSMTAP packets that Wireshark decodes like any other protocol (RRC, NAS, MAC, RLC); and a pure-ADB poller that reads RF values and byte counters from Android itself and draws a live per-phone dashboard. The first gives you signalling; the second gives you a quick "are all 10 phones attached and how fast are they going" view. Related: MACTTI01 (eNB-side per-TTI log), AUTO06 (ADB/iperf automation), PDCAP (protocol captures).
Two routes to UE-side visibility without QXDM
Top: signalling via SCAT + GSMTAP. Bottom: ADB-only live RF and throughput.
Level 1 — what each route gives you
SCAT + GSMTAPADB dashboard
What you getdecoded RRC, NAS, MAC, RLC (and more) from the modemRSRP, RSRQ, RSSNR, RSSI, PCI, TAC, EARFCN, bandwidth, PLMN, UL/DL Mbps, service state
ModemsQualcomm (-t qc), Samsung, HiSilicon; USB, serial or a saved dump fileany Android (field names differ per vendor)
Root neededyes — to switch the USB mode to expose the diag portfor throughput (reads /proc/net/dev); RF values work without
Host needsLinux, Python ≥ 3.7 + pyusb, pyserial, bitstring, packaging (libscrc optional); Wireshark ≥ 2.6 for 2G/3G/4G, ≥ 3.0 for 5G NR RRC / NAS-5GS (4.2.5+ recommended)Linux, Python ≥ 3.8, adb, Streamlit + pandas
Not availablesome handsets do not emit PDCP/RLC even when requestedBLER, HARQ, MCS, any PHY metric
GSMTAP is a simple UDP encapsulation (default port 4729) designed so that air-interface messages captured by non-IP tools can be shown in Wireshark. SCAT re-emits each diag log item as one GSMTAP packet; Wireshark recognises the channel type and hands it to the right RRC/NAS dissector.
Level 2 — set-up and commands
Route 1 — SCAT
git clone https://github.com/fgsect/scat.git && cd scat
pip install -e .[fastcrc]            # or: pip install -e .

# expose the diag port (string depends on the handset)
adb -s <serial> shell su -c "setprop sys.usb.config diag,diag_mdm,adb"
#   alternative seen on other phones: diag,serial_cdev,rmnet,adb
lsusb            # find Bus:Device of the phone; lsusb -v to confirm a diag interface

# Wireshark helper dissector
cp wireshark/scat.lua ~/.local/lib/wireshark/plugins/

# start decoding (Qualcomm, USB, layers rrc/rlc/mac, first radio)
scat -t qc -u -a <bus:dev> -L "rrc,rlc,mac" -i 0
# then capture on the loopback interface with capture filter: udp
Filter in Wireshark with gsmtap, lte-rrc, nas-eps or nr-rrc. Save the pcap with the eNB-side captures of the same test so both ends of a procedure can be lined up by time.

Route 2 — ADB-only live KPIs
adb devices                                   # skip anything "unauthorized"
adb -s <serial> shell dumpsys telephony.registry    # look for mSignalStrength / CellIdentityLte: rsrp, rsrq, rssnr, pci, tac, earfcn, bandwidth
adb -s <serial> shell su -c "cat /proc/net/dev"     # rx/tx bytes for rmnet_data* / rmnet_ipa*
Throughput = Δbytes × 8 ÷ Δt, computed per interface and taking the active one (the one whose counters move). A minimal poller:
import subprocess, time, re
def bytes_(s):
    out = subprocess.run(["adb","-s",s,"shell","su -c 'cat /proc/net/dev'"], capture_output=True, text=True).stdout
    rx = tx = 0
    for ln in out.splitlines():
        if re.match(r"\s*rmnet_(data|ipa)", ln):
            f = ln.split(":")[1].split(); rx += int(f[0]); tx += int(f[8])
    return rx, tx
r0, t0 = bytes_(S); time.sleep(1); r1, t1 = bytes_(S)
print("DL %.1f Mbps  UL %.1f Mbps" % ((r1-r0)*8/1e6, (t1-t0)*8/1e6))
The dashboard pattern: one backend collector polls every authorised phone in a loop and shares the latest values; a Streamlit page shows one row per UE. Run it headless in the background (nohup python3 -m streamlit run ue_dashboard.py --server.port 8501 --server.headless true &) and check it is listening with ss -lntp | grep 8501.
Level 3 — pitfalls and a real-world pattern
  • USB mode strings are per handset. If lsusb -v shows no diag interface after setprop, try the other combination or the vendor's engineering menu; a reboot resets it.
  • Missing layers. On some phones PDCP and RLC never appear even with -L; RRC and NAS are the reliable part. For user-plane detail use the eNB/gNB side (MACTTI01).
  • Wireshark version. NR RRC over GSMTAP needs a recent Wireshark; an old one shows "malformed" or raw data for 5G.
  • dumpsys is a framework view. Values are refreshed at the modem's reporting rate (often 1–2 s), rounded, and sometimes stale after a handover; 2147483647 (INT_MAX) means "unavailable". Never use them for conformance-grade measurements.
  • Throughput from byte counters includes all traffic on the bearer (background apps, retransmitted TCP) and misses nothing at IP level, but it is IP goodput, not PHY throughput — compare with iperf and the eNB stats.
  • Many phones on one host: use USB2 ports/hubs, disable ModemManager (it grabs modem ports) and USB autosuspend, otherwise devices drop off the bus mid-test.
  • Privacy. UE logs contain IMSI/IMEI and sometimes location; treat captures as sensitive and strip them before sharing.

Typical use. A phone fails to add an SCell: SCAT shows the RRC Reconfiguration arrived and the UE answered with Reconfiguration Complete, but measurement reports never include the SCell frequency — the problem is the UE band combination, not the eNB. Meanwhile the ADB dashboard shows nine phones at full rate and one at 2 Mbps with RSRQ −18 dB: a placement problem, found in seconds.
Interview Q&A
Q: What is GSMTAP and why is it useful?
A: A UDP pseudo-header format (port 4729) that wraps air-interface messages so Wireshark can dissect them. Tools like SCAT convert modem diag logs to GSMTAP, giving free RRC/NAS decoding.
Q: What do you need to capture UE RRC messages with SCAT on a Qualcomm phone?
A: A rooted phone with the USB diag interface enabled via setprop, a Linux host with SCAT and its Python deps, the scat.lua plugin, then scat -t qc -u -a bus:dev and a Wireshark capture on loopback filtered on udp.
Q: How do you compute UE throughput with only ADB?
A: Read rx/tx byte counters for rmnet_data*/rmnet_ipa* in /proc/net/dev twice, take the delta times 8 divided by the interval. Needs root.
Q: Which KPIs can ADB not give you?
A: BLER, HARQ statistics, MCS, rank and other PHY/MAC metrics — only framework-level values (RSRP, RSRQ, SNR, cell identity, IP byte counters).
Q: When would you still need QXDM or a network-side log?
A: For PHY/MAC detail (BLER, HARQ, grants), for layers the handset does not emit through diag, or when a vendor requires official logs for a defect.
REXDBG01 Proving a Cell Is Really UP on the vBBU — 4G vs 5G cell-up evidence, the REX/NATS IPC triage tree, core link vs IPC and five false positives Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
"The cell is up" is the most common claim in a bring-up report and one of the most often wrong. On a containerised vBBU a cell is the end of a long chain: pods start, the RU heartbeats, the OAM manager asks the RRH manager to set the frequency, the PHY side confirms L1 is done, the RRH manager says the RRH is ready, the stack starts the cell, and separately the backhaul (S1 or NG over SCTP and IPsec) comes up to the core. Between the pods, those control messages travel over an internal IPC transport — on newer builds a NATS/JetStream message broker called REX instead of the legacy point-to-point UDP "UniTask" messages.

This card is the evidence view: what "cell up" literally looks like in 4G versus 5G logs (they are completely different), a stop-at-the-first-NO triage tree through the IPC chain, how to tell an IPC problem from a core-link problem, five ways to fool yourself, and a shutdown-crash lesson from the new transport. The RU-side activation chain itself (SET_FREQ → L1_DONE → RRH_READY) is explained in CM16 and RRHMGR01, the 4G vNode bring-up sequence in CELLUP01; backhaul IPsec is in IPSEC01.
Figure — two independent paths behind "cell up" (own drawing)
A healthy IPC chain with a dead core link is a backhaul problem; a healthy core link with no RRH_READY is an IPC/RU problem.
Level 1 — what REX is, and what "cell up" means in 4G vs 5G
  • REX = the platform IPC transport replacing legacy UDP UniTask messages between pods: a NATS server (v2.10; client port 4222, monitoring 8222) running as the rex pod, JetStream for persistence, a bridge library compiled into each BBU binary that hooks the old send call, and a reverse proxy for the RRH manager (which sends with raw UDP sendto, not the hooked call). Intra-pod and fallback delivery is still UDP.
  • Cross-pod messages use subjects xpod.<facility> (e.g. xpod.oammgr, xpod.rrhmgr) on the stream IPC_XPOD. Each pod has one durable consumer per facility, so expected consumers = broker pods × 5 (40 on a CU + 3 DU + 3 PHY topology). A destination-IP header lets receivers drop messages that are not for them.
  • Healthy consumer: pending 0, redelivered 0, ack_floor tracking consumer_seq. A watchdog thread health-checks consumers every 10 s and recreates lost ones.
  • The transport is switched per pod with the env var IPC_TRANSPORT=broker (otherwise legacy UniTask).
4G (monolithic BBU)5G (CU + DU + PHY)
Cell upsyslog Cell Oper: UP (printed by the eNB stack; OAM's own condition is admin state AND op state AND RF TX status all true)no such string. oran_up_config_active=[1] (M-plane controller: TX + frequency configured), ORNC_L1_DONE + ORNC_RRH_READY per RRH, FAPI cell-start response with error 0 and RACH indications in the DU L2 log
Access linkAccess Link: UPF1 Setup Response in the CU logs
Core linkCore Link: UPNG Setup Response / AMF SCTP association in the CU logs
Where to lookone place: the BBU/vnode syslogsplit over BBU syslog (RU/M-plane), DU L2 log (FAPI, RACH) and DU syslog (OAM)
RRH manager / OAM managerBBU pod / vnode podBBU pod (still central) / CU and DU pods
Searching a 5G system for Cell Oper: UP, RFTX_ON or RRH_IN_SYNC always returns nothing, even when every cell is on air — those are 4G-only patterns.
Level 2 — the triage tree and the commands
Master tree — follow top-down, stop at the first NO:
#QuestionIf NO
1All pods Running? (kubectl get pods -n pw)pod events / why the stack did not launch
2IPC_TRANSPORT=broker on every relevant pod?system is on legacy UniTask — REX is not being tested at all
3rex pod 1/1 Running?broker down; pods fall back to UniTask
4JetStream consumer count = broker pods × 5?consumer creation failed — check watchdog REBOUND/CREATED lines
5RRH heartbeat present (ORU_STATUS_HEARTBEAT_ACTIVE)?fronthaul / RU connectivity, not IPC
6SET_FREQ_REQ sent?OAM manager not configuring: provisioning / OAM state (see ACSRCA01)
7L1_DONE received?M-plane controller / PHY did not finish carrier set-up
8RRH_READY sent to OAM?reverse-proxy path from the RRH manager
9all yes but cell still downthe eNB / NR stack — not IPC
kubectl exec -n pw <pod> -- env | grep IPC_TRANSPORT         # on EVERY pod, not just rex
kubectl exec -n pw <rex> -- nats consumer ls IPC_XPOD          # count vs pods x 5
kubectl exec -n pw <rex> -- nats consumer info IPC_XPOD <name> # pending / redelivered / ack floor / last delivered
kubectl exec -n pw <rex> -- nats stream info IPC_XPOD          # last sequence advancing? bytes below the limit?
grep -E 'HEARTBEAT_ACTIVE|SET_FREQ|L1_Done|RRH_Ready' /var/log/messages   # BBU syslog, 4G chain
grep -E 'ORNC_L1_DONE|ORNC_RRH_READY|oran_up_config_active' /var/log/messages   # BBU syslog, 5G per RRH
Multi-DU twist. If all DUs are affected, suspect the shared broker or stream. If one DU is affected, check that DU's consumers exist, that the destination-IP header on its messages is right, and that its consumer ack floor advances. A stuck consumer can be deleted and left for the watchdog to recreate. "Not for us" drops on the other DUs should be zero.

Core link is backhaul, never IPC. Core Link: DOWN (4G) or no NG Setup Response (5G) with healthy consumers means: check the IPsec SAs (ip xfrm state — empty output = no tunnel), the routes towards the core, the SCTP association (timeouts, retransmissions) and S1/NG Setup Failure causes such as PLMN or TAC mismatch or core overload.
ip xfrm state | head -20                  # active SAs present?
ip route | grep -i 'core\|default'
grep -i 'core.*link' /var/log/messages     # 4G
grep -i 'ng.*setup\|amf\|sctp' <CU L3 log>  # 5G: NG Setup Response vs Failure
Minimum evidence for a "cell up via REX" claim (put this in the PR or test report): topology and manifest version; transport env confirmed per pod; rex pod age and restart count; consumer count actual / expected; pending and redelivered (or an explanation); the RRH chain lines HEARTBEAT → SET_FREQ → L1_DONE → RRH_READY → cell up; and the stream bounded below its byte limit.
Level 3 — five false positives and a transport side-effect
#False positiveHow it fools youPrevention
1Pods on legacy transportthe cell comes up the old UDP way while you believe REX was tested, because the rex pod is Runningcheck IPC_TRANSPORT on every pod
2Stale consumersconsumers from a previous deploy inflate the count (40 listed, 20 alive)check each consumer's last-delivered time, or purge the stream before validation
3Watchdog says "all healthy"a health-check false positive (fixed later) reported 5/5 healthy while consumers were missing on the serveralways cross-check the watchdog log against nats consumer ls
4Cell up during a broker outagethe bridge fell back to UniTask for new messages and the cell came up in that windowstream last-sequence must advance and there must be zero "publish failed" lines after cell up
55G "active" from pods Running5G has no Cell Oper string, so someone infers it from pod staterequire L1_DONE + RRH_READY per RRH, ideally oran_up_config_active=[1] (not every config logs it)
Transport side-effect — the upgrade-only SIGABRT (NR5G-27067). After the new transport was added, the CU's stack-manager crashed with signal 6 on every upgrade (exit code 134 = 128 + 6). Symbolised stack: main had already returned → static destructor of the RAT singleton → transport object destructor → disconnect() → a log call into a logger that the shutdown path had already torn down. A classic C++ static-destruction-order problem.
Why it hid: a plain pod restart/kill never reproduced it, because SIGKILL ends the process before static destructors run; an image upgrade sends SIGTERM, the graceful shutdown path runs, and exit-time destructors execute. The release branch without the transport was unaffected.
Fix (belt and braces): tear the transport down explicitly in the shutdown sequence before the logger shuts down, and remove logging from the destructor. Validated over 10/10 upgrade cycles. Test lesson: "restart the pod" and "upgrade the pod" exercise different exit paths — an upgrade cycle belongs in the regression set of any component that changes shutdown or adds singletons.
Pitfalls
  • Blaming REX for a DU stack that keeps restarting: DU stack restarts are usually NR stack issues; the consumers will look fine.
  • Reading receiver-side filtering in syslog: those traces are debug-level only; use consumer info (pending 0) and UDP socket queues instead.
  • Letting the stream grow without bound: compare stream bytes with the configured maximum after long runs.
  • Calling a cell "UP" in 5G after grepping 4G strings and finding nothing — then calling it "DOWN" for the same reason.
Interview Q&A
Q: How do you prove a 5G cell is active when there is no "Cell Oper: UP" log?
A: Combine three sources: in the BBU syslog the RU heartbeat, carrier configured and activated, and per-RRH ORNC_L1_DONE and ORNC_RRH_READY (ideally oran_up_config_active=[1]); in the DU L2 log the FAPI cell-start response with error 0 and RACH indications; and in the CU logs F1 Setup Response and NG Setup Response for access and core links.
Q: All JetStream consumers are healthy but Core Link is DOWN. Where do you look?
A: Not at IPC. The core link is backhaul: check IPsec SAs (ip xfrm state), routes to the core, the SCTP association and any S1/NG Setup Failure cause such as PLMN/TAC mismatch.
Q: The rex pod is Running and the cell came up. Have you validated REX?
A: Not yet. Confirm IPC_TRANSPORT=broker on every pod, the consumer count equals pods x 5 with no stale consumers, pending and redelivered are zero, and the stream sequence advanced with no publish failures — otherwise the cell may have come up over the legacy or fallback path.
Q: Why did a crash appear only on upgrades and never on pod restarts?
A: A kill does not run C++ static destructors; an upgrade does a graceful SIGTERM shutdown, after which a singleton destructor logged through an already-destroyed logger and aborted. Fix by tearing the object down before logger shutdown and not logging in destructors.
Q: What is the stop-at-first-NO order for an RRH that never becomes ready?
A: Pods Running, transport env, rex pod, consumer count, RU heartbeat, SET_FREQ_REQ sent, L1_DONE received, RRH_READY sent. If all are yes and the cell is still down, it is the eNB/NR stack.
CORE01 Lab Core Bring-Up and Symptom-to-Root-Cause Playbook (4G/5G/IMS) - ports, UPF SR-IOV, PFCP/NSSAI/PLMN/IMS failures, S1AP ServedGUMMEIs multi-PLMN bug Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
A RAN lab is only as good as the core behind it. When a phone "attaches but has no internet", or VoLTE calls time out, the radio is usually innocent - the fault is a placeholder address, a slice or PLMN mismatch, or a user-plane NIC that never linked. This card condenses a full bring-up of a commercial unified core (2G/4G/5G + IMS on one bare-metal server, each network function in its own VM) into what a RAN tester needs: which NF does what, which port to look for, how the user plane must be wired, and a symptom → root cause table that saves days.

It also documents a real S1AP encoding bug in that core's multi-PLMN support - a good example of how to prove "it's the core, not the eNB" with one Wireshark filter. 2G CS/PS core integration with the vBSC is covered separately; CSFB theory is in CSFB02/PT20, NAS in PD05.
Converged lab core - NF split and the ports a RAN tester greps for
Simplified. Management/OAM, signalling (S1/N2), N3 and N6 are separate VLANs; N4 and IMS SIP ride the management plane in this build.
Level 1 - who does what, and the port list
VMNetwork functionsRAN-tester relevance
Core-CPMME, AMF, SMF, PCF (one VM, so one management address by default - must be split for OMC polling)S1 Setup, NG Setup, attach/registration, PDU sessions; read mme.log / amf.log
UDMUDM with AUSF built in, HSS; Redis-type KV storeSubscriber keys, slices, PLMN list, IMS profiles
IMSP/I/S-CSCF, MMTEL AS, BGCF, MRF, media relay, IWF (Rx<->N5)VoLTE registration and call ladder

Plane separation: management/OAM (also carries N4 and IMS SIP here), signalling (S1 + N2), N3 (GTP-U in) and N6 (to the data network, NAT'd; also the IMS media leg) each on their own VLAN. Note: VoNR was not available because the gNB in that lab did not support it - VoLTE over 4G was the voice path.
Level 2 - user plane done right, and the verification sequence
UPF NIC under KVM. Passing the whole Intel E810 physical function into the UPF VM looks fine to the kernel driver (10 Gbps) but the DPDK ice PMD inside a KVM guest reports speed 0 and zero queues - phones register and carry no data. The fix is an SR-IOV virtual function per port (iavf PMD links fine), with the VLAN assigned on the VF so the NIC tags/untags in hardware and VPP sees plain untagged interfaces. VFs vanish on host reboot, so a oneshot systemd unit must recreate them (VLAN, MAC, trust on, spoof-check off) before the VMs autostart - and you must prove it with a real reboot.
Hugepages. Back the UPF VM with host 1 GB pages (the hypervisor refuses unless NUMA is enabled for the VM). Inside the guest, the UPF package's sysctl vm.nr_hugepages=1024 was written for 2 MB pages; with 1 GB default pages it means 1024 GB and starves the OS - cap it (8 x 1 GB for DPDK in a 40 GB VM).
Trust the CLI, not the log: the forwarder log's "speed 0" line is written once at init and goes stale. 
# Core-CP: listeners and setups
ss -ltn | grep -E ':(36412|38412|8080)'
grep -i 'S1 Setup\|NG Setup' /var/log/mme.log /var/log/amf.log
# UPF: PFCP association + heartbeats, then the VPP CLI
grep -i ASSOCIATION /var/log/upf*
printf 'show hardware-interfaces\r\n' | nc localhost <cli-port>   # N3/N6 up, 10 Gbps, iAVF
printf 'show interface\r\n'          | nc localhost <cli-port>   # rx/tx counters climbing
printf 'show nat44 sessions\r\n'     | nc localhost <cli-port>   # UE flows NAT'd to N6
printf 'show upf session\r\n'        | nc localhost <cli-port>   # live UE sessions
Acceptance order: NFs licensed and active → UPF link + PFCP association → S1 Setup and NG Setup → 4G attach per PLMN (mme.log: Attach Request, AIR, auth, security, Create Session, Attach Accept) → 5G registration + PDU session (amf.log) → browsing with N3/N6 counters climbing → IMS REGISTER → VoLTE ladder INVITE, 100, 183, PRACK/UPDATE (preconditions), 180, 200, ACK, BYE with a CDR showing cause 200.

Lightweight alternative for SIP-only tests: a Kamailio 5.4 based IMS in a single Docker container on host networking, with the SIP domain passed as an environment variable, subscribers added with kamctl add after kamdbctl create, and a netstat check that 5060 is listening. Enough for registration/call-routing tests; no Rx/PCC preconditions.
Level 3 - symptom to root cause playbook
SymptomRoot causeFix

Registration Reject, allowed NSSAI mismatchUDM subscribed slice differs from AMF/gNB/UE sliceAlign SST/SD, re-provision subscriber
4G UE never attaches; MME asserts on mme_self()->sgw before authMME peer lists (S11, S5/S8, S6a) on one PLMN with a placeholder TAC - no SGW matches the TAIExpand peer lists to every served PLMN and real TACs
"Roaming not allowed" on the home PLMNPLMN missing from UDM PLMN list (esp. a test MCC such as 002)Add the PLMN to the UDM list
UE never sends SIP REGISTERSMF P-CSCF address is a placeholder - PCO carries a dead P-CSCFCorrect P-CSCF address in SMF
Every call 408MMTEL AS name placeholder in UDM; S-CSCF caches the stale iFCFix AS config, restart CSCFs
183 then 580 Precondition FailureRx pointed at the PCF (3868) instead of the IWF (4001)Point Rx at the IWF
MO calls work, MT callee never ringsStale IMS contact with no live UPF sessionRe-register callee; static IMS address for a durable fix
5G works, 4G broken, nobody noticedSeparate code pathsValidate 4G and 5G separately; read mme.log, not SIM data
Real case - S1AP ServedGUMMEIs multi-PLMN bug (TS 36.413 9.1.8.5). In S1 SETUP RESPONSE the core put each served PLMN in a separate ServedGUMMEIs item. That outer list is per RAT (maxnoofRATs = 8) and the LTE pool must be the first item; all PLMNs of one MME belong in that item's servedPLMNs list (up to 32). Effect on a conformant eNB: if the eNB is provisioned for the PLMN in item 0, S1 comes up but the second PLMN silently never routes; if provisioned for the PLMN in item 1, S1 Setup Failure with cause unknown-PLMN. Proof: capture S1, filter s1ap.procedureCode == 17 (S1 Setup) and compare with a reference core in the same lab that sends one item with several PLMNs. Fix requested from the vendor for both S1 Setup Response and MME Configuration Update.

Other traps: UDM REST "add/delete subscriber" calls that report success and change nothing (use the vendor CLI and always read back); a permanent "Redis not available" critical alarm that is a health-probe auth artefact; one golden VM image with the same address on every VM; co-located NFs sharing one management address.
Interview Q&A
Q: A UE registers on 5G but shows no data and the UPF N3 RX counter is zero. Where do you look?
A: The UPF data path: under KVM a fully passed-through E810 PF never links in DPDK (speed 0, zero queues). Use an SR-IOV VF with the VLAN set on the VF, then check show hardware-interfaces in VPP rather than the stale forwarder log.
Q: What does PFCP cause 75 "no resources" usually mean in a fresh lab core?
A: The UPF forwarder still has placeholder PCI/MAC/driver values, so the UPF cannot create the session. Fix the forwarder YAML (the generated start script is rebuilt from it).
Q: VoLTE calls get 183 then 580 Precondition Failure. Likely cause?
A: The Rx (Diameter) path from the P-CSCF points at the PCF instead of the IWF that translates Rx to N5, so the QoS precondition is never met.
Q: How should an MME advertise two PLMNs in S1 Setup Response?
A: One ServedGUMMEIs item (the LTE pool, first in the list) with both PLMNs in its servedPLMNs list. Separate items are per RAT; splitting PLMNs across items makes a conformant eNB ignore or reject the second PLMN.
Q: Why validate 4G and 5G attach separately on a converged core?
A: They are different code paths (MME vs AMF, different peer lists). 5G can work perfectly while 4G asserts on a missing SGW for the TAI; check mme.log per PLMN.
GSMCORE01 2G CS/PS Core + vBSC Integration — A-interface over M3UA, Gb/vPCU, handshake-then-ABORT, silent BSSAP drop, LU Reject 0x0B, GPRS diagnostic order Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
A 2G network has two halves behind the base-station controller. The circuit-switched (CS) half carries voice: the BSC talks to the MSC/VLR over the A-interface (BSSAP on SCCP, carried over SIGTRAN M3UA/SCTP in an all-IP lab). The packet-switched (PS) half carries GPRS/EDGE data: the BSC's packet unit (PW calls it the vPCU) talks to the SGSN over Gb (Network Service + BSSGP over UDP). Both the MSC and the SGSN must reach the HLR over Gr (MAP) before any phone can register. This card is the integration playbook from a lab where a PW vBSC was attached to a commercial unified 2G/4G/5G core: what each link needs, what "healthy" looks like, and the silent failure modes that cost days. The common thread: almost every fault was one unset or mismatched value with no alarm and no log line. The radio side of 2G is in G2PHY01 / G2RUN01; the 4G/5G side of the same core is in CORE01; LTESIM01 covers the simulator alternative.
2G core around a PW vBSC - interfaces and who must agree with whom
In this lab the HLR is a function of the UDM (one subscriber database for all RATs) and there is no separate GGSN: Gn points at the SMF, so 2G data lands on the same UPF as 4G/5G.
Level 1 — the five 2G links and what "up" means
InterfacePeersTransport / roleHealthy looks like
A (BSSAP / AoIP)vBSC → MSCM3UA over SCTP, port 2905; MSC is the server (SGP), vBSC the client (ASP)SCTP ESTAB with heartbeats both ways, no ABORT; BSC shows Core-Up
Gr (MAP)MSC/VLR ↔ HLRM3UA; HLR SSN 6, VLR SSN 7, MSC SSN 8ESTAB; first MAP traffic appears at Location Update
Gr (MAP)SGSN ↔ HLRM3UA; SGSN SSN 149ESTAB; needed for GPRS attach / authentication
GsSGSN ↔ MSCM3UAESTAB (combined attach / paging coordination)
Gb (NS / BSSGP)vBSC (vPCU) ↔ SGSNUDP, no client/server; SGSN listener 23000 in this productNS-ALIVE / NS-ALIVE-ACK every ~3 s
Gn (GTPv1-C/U)SGSN → SMF acting as GGSNUDP 2123 / 2152; GTPv1 and GTPv2 share port 2123 (version bits)Create PDP Context Request/Response, then G-PDUs both ways
Idle is normal. With no BTS attached, A and Gb come up and sit idle: no M3UA DATA and no BSSGP traffic is expected. Do not chase "no traffic" until a handset attaches.
SS7 identity is never validated. Point codes, network indicator, routing context and global titles are free choices that must simply mirror on both ends — a wrong value produces silence, not an error. Write a numbering plan first. In this lab: network indicator national (2) on both ends, the core's factory MSC and SGSN point codes kept, the BSC point code chosen to match the core's existing template entry, and an HLR point code invented because the factory one collided with the MSC's (two nodes can never share a point code).
Reading the core's config files: point codes are written as 6 hex digits (24-bit ITU PC, e.g. 0003e8 = 1000), addresses written by the GUI are little-endian hex, and routes/linksets/links/gateways reference each other by index — delete one row and others are silently orphaned, so edit the tables as a set.
Level 2 — how to configure and verify the vBSC side
A. A-interface (vBSC in the PW GUI/ACS vs MSC)
ParametervBSCMust match on the MSC
Network indicatornationalMTP3 routing / linkset NI = 2 (the vBSC wiki example says international — silently mismatches)
vBSC / MSC point codesown PC / MSC PCroute + linkset DPC and the MSC's OPC
M3UA peerMSC signalling address, port 2905MSC M3UA listen address/port (a documented example of 2915 has nothing listening)
RoleASP (vBSC dials)SGP (MSC listens) — exactly one server per link
M3UA optional paramsrouting context, ASP id, network appearance all offrouting context unset (0). Copying "RC = 1" from another lab breaks the association
Bind addressesthe pod macvlan address, not the K8s node address—
LACzone LACone location area per BSC in the MSC, pointing at a trunk group that has an A-interface socket
B. Gb (vBSC vs SGSN)
ParametervBSCMust match on the SGSN
NSEIcore NSEIsame value in the SGSN's NSEI list — NSEI is end-to-end (TS 48.016), not local
NSVCIunique valuesame in the SGSN list
NS endpointcore pod address + UDP portSGSN remote IP/port for that NSEI
NS config modestatic while first bringing a link updynamic makes the vBSC send SNS-SIZE, which a statically provisioned SGSN never answers
RAC / NRIRAC, NRI length (5 bits here), null-NRI 0must match; null-NRI must not fall inside any node's NRI list
C. Pod networking traps in the vBSC deployment step
  • Every bind address the ACS asks for (M3UA, NS, RTP) is a pod address from a macvlan network attachment, not the node IP.
  • The chart default bridge name does not exist on the host — set the real host device (it is hidden until the parameter view is switched to "All").
  • Leave the VLAN id empty if the hypervisor already tags the VLAN; setting it double-tags and nothing works.
  • Only the m-plane leg gets a gateway; MSC and SGSN are on-link on the core leg. A macvlan child cannot ping its own parent's IP — not a valid test.
D. PS-side checklist (nine settings, any one wrong = "bars, no data")
  • MSC home-PLMN admission list (the sgs_plmn key, despite its name) = served PLMN; otherwise LU Reject 0x0B.
  • MSC location-area file reduced to one LA per BSC, no factory entries, no dangling trunk-group id.
  • HLR SCCP: one E.212 (numbering plan 6) GTT row per served PLMN, else every MAP query returns UDTS "no translation for this address".
  • SGSN local IMSI-prefix list includes every served PLMN; SGSN Gn remote address = SMF GTP-C address (same subnet as the SGSN Gn local address).
  • SMF GGSN function enabled with a real Gn address (ships off with a loopback placeholder); afterwards confirm the S11 GTP-C and N4 PFCP sockets still bind.
  • SMO ps-config: assoc-vbsc-instance = the node-level CS instance name (ships empty!), Gb config-mode, SGSN pool PLMN.
  • Cell: GPRS Admin and EGPRS Admin = true (reboot-gated). Without them SI13 never advertises packet service and the handset never tries to attach.
E. Verify Gb by socket, not by status. A healthy vPCU binds the access NS port and the core NS, signalling and data UDP ports. No sockets = the vPCU never started, whatever the GUI says.
Level 2 — GPRS diagnostic order (work from the core outwards)
#QuestionHow to read it
1Refused or not arriving?Decode BSSMAP on A: LU Reject cause 0x0B = PLMN identity; 0x03 (illegal MS) = authentication; no CR at all = radio/cell
2Does the MSC query the HLR?No MAP at all = LA config or home-PLMN gate; UDTS back = HLR GTT missing
3Is Gb up?NS-ALIVE/ACK plus FLOW-CONTROL-BVC/ACK. Periodic flow control on a BVCI is the healthy steady state, not a reset loop
4Any UL-UNITDATA?None = handset never attempted GPRS = cell not advertising it. Some = attach progressing
5PDP context?Create PDP Context Request/Response on Gn
6User traffic?G-PDU in both directions on GTP-U at the UPF
7Where is the latency?Match ICMP echo pairs at the SGSN with what the handset sees. Single-digit ms at the SGSN but seconds at the phone = core exonerated, delay is Gb/BSS/radio
Measuring 2G data. Use UDP iperf (e.g. iperf3 -u -b 150k -t 60), not TCP: on a two-PDCH EDGE cell queueing delay reaches tens of seconds and TCP measures the queue, not the link. Silence the phone's background sync first. A stock cell with two PDCHs cannot realistically load a modern web page; validate with ping, plain HTTP and UDP.
Level 3 — silent failures and the real case
  • Handshake-then-ABORT. The SCTP 4-way handshake completes and one side sends ABORT a few ms later, no M3UA DATA, no alarm. Almost always (a) the M3UA bind IP is the management address instead of the signalling address (printed only on stdout), or (b) both ends configured as M3UA clients — nobody listens. Check both before anything else. Lock/Unlock does not activate a link; in this EMS it only exists so a link can be deleted.
  • A-interface accepts the link and drops every message. The MSC creates one A-interface socket per trunk group, and only for trunk groups with the "extended protocol" flag = 0. If the location area points at a trunk group without a socket, BSSAP for that LA is discarded silently. Outside view: SCTP/M3UA ESTAB, BSSAP peer Active, cell in service, the handset gets a channel then loses it, the BSC retransmits the SCCP CR after 5 s; the PW vBSC logs UeAipEntry not found once per attempt. Only a pcap on the MSC (inbound DATA, zero replies) shows it. Fix: the LA's trunk group must have the BSC point code as DPC, the flag 0, and appear in a socket-creation log line after restart.
  • LU Reject 0x0B in ~150 ms with no MAP. Identity, not subscription: the cell's PLMN (BSC zone home PLMN, read from the CGI in COMPLETE LAYER 3 INFO on the wire), the MSC's served/home PLMN and the IMSI's MCC+MNC must all agree. Fingerprint: a roamer accepted while a home subscriber is refused.
  • One GSM cell broadcasts exactly one PLMN (LTE SIB1 can list up to six). Plan one cell per PLMN. Cell Identity and LAC on the BTS are reboot-gated.
  • vPCU never starts when assoc-vbsc-instance is empty: cfg-status reads Success, no alarm; only the vBSC pod log says the vPCU "is not associated with VBSC instance".
  • "Rejecting NS PDU for non-existing NS-VC" on the SGSN looks like an intruder — it is almost always your own BSC with the wrong NSEI. If dynamic NS (SNS-SIZE retries exhausted) fails, first give the cell unique, valid NSVCI/BVCI values.
  • Throughput. Initial DL MCS ships at 1 and link adaptation did not climb (~8.8 kbps per PDCH); setting initial EGPRS DL MCS to 9 applies live and raised peak DL from ~22 to ~97 kbps. UL is capped at MCS-4 by the data model. A minimum-free-TCH default of 4 silently disables dynamic PDCH on any small cell.
  • Rules that avoided outages: change reboot-gated cell parameters one at a time with a completed reboot between; never use cell lock/unlock as a reboot; never kubectl delete pod the vBSC (its running config store is ephemeral and the SMO does not re-push).
Interview Q&A
Q: An SCTP association to the MSC completes the handshake and is ABORTed within milliseconds. What do you check first?
A: The two classic causes: the M3UA bind address is the management IP rather than the signalling IP, or both ends are configured as M3UA clients so nobody accepts. It is not an admin-state problem — Lock/Unlock does not bring a link up.
Q: The BSC shows Core-Up, the cell is in service, the phone gets a channel and then loses it, and the MSC logs nothing. Next step?
A: Capture on the MSC signalling interface. Inbound M3UA DATA with zero replies means the MSC discards BSSAP for that location area — typically the LA points at a trunk group with no A-interface socket. Logs alone never localise it.
Q: Location Update is rejected with cause 0x0B within 150 ms and there is no MAP traffic. Subscription or identity?
A: Identity. The MSC refuses locally when the PLMN in the cell's CGI or the IMSI is not one it serves. Compare the BSC zone home PLMN, the MSC home-PLMN list and the IMSI MCC+MNC.
Q: Gb is up but no handset ever does a GPRS attach. Why?
A: Gb up only proves the BSC-SGSN half. If there is no UL-UNITDATA at all, the cell is not advertising packet service (GPRS/EGPRS admin off, SI13). If attach starts but fails at authentication, the SGSN cannot reach the HLR over Gr.
Q: Why use UDP rather than TCP to measure EDGE throughput?
A: With only two PDCHs the queue delay reaches seconds; TCP throughput is window divided by RTT, so a phone's receive window caps the result far below the radio rate. UDP at a fixed rate shows what the link can actually carry.
HNG01 HNG (HetNet Gateway) — vENB S1 proxy, X2GW, HNBGW/vRNC, vBSC/vPCU, ePDG/TWAG, SecGW; access-vs-core counters, CLI cheat-sheet and 3G HNB registration Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
The HNG (HetNet Gateway) is the PW gateway that sits between many radio nodes and the operator's core. Instead of every small cell or vNode opening its own S1, Iu or A connection to the core, they all connect to the HNG, and the HNG presents itself to the core as a few large nodes. On the access side it sees "cws" access nodes (the HNG's name for the radios it manages); on the core side it sees MMEs, SGWs, MSCs and SGSNs. Almost every HNG counter and CLI command is split the same way: access = towards the radios, core = towards the EPC/MSC/SGSN. Comparing the two is the fastest way to find out which side is dropping messages. It also acts as the TR-069 management server for nodes registered to it, an IPsec security gateway and, in 2G/3G, as the virtual RNC/BSC. GSMCORE01 covers the 2G core side of the vBSC; NT4/ZT4 cover NSA X2 behaviour through an aggregator.
What the HNG aggregates
Function list as exercised by the HNG developer sanity suites (S1AP standalone/redundancy, X2AP, X2GW (+redundancy), HNBGW, ePDG, TWAG DHCP/RADIUS, IPsec, DPDK, IPv6).
Level 1 — functions and their key status command
FunctionRATWhat it doesFirst command to run
vENB (S1AP proxy)4GTerminates S1 from many eNBs and presents one S1 towards each MME; GTP-U relay; paging fan-out to cellsshow venb s1ap peer-state <venb> cws|mme
X2GW / X2AP4GAggregates X2 between cells so neighbours do not need full-mesh SCTPclear x2gw x2ap statistics access|core <x2gw>
HNBGW / vRNC3GIuh from HNBs; RANAP + SCCP/M3UA towards MSC and SGSNshow vrnc ranap, show vrnc m3ua
vBSC / vPCU2GAbis from BTSs; BSSAP/SCCP and RTP to the MSC; Gb NS/BSSGP to the SGSNshow vbsc sccp peer-state <x> msc, show vpcu ns
ePDG / TWAGWi-FiUntrusted (ePDG) and trusted (TWAG with DHCP/RADIUS) non-3GPP accesssuite-level only
SecGW + fastpathallIPsec termination for backhaul, DPDK packet path, ACLsshow ipsec ike-statistics global, show fastpath statistics ipv4
RedundancyallActive/standby cluster with switchovershow redundancy status, show switchover count
Reading the counters: every protocol keeps access and core statistics plus per-peer statistics. Clear both, run the test, then compare: a request counted on access but not on core means the HNG did not forward it (config, admission, mapping); counted on core with no response means the core side is at fault.
Level 2 — CLI cheat-sheet (what automation actually uses)
# ---- access nodes (the radios registered to this HNG) ----
show accessnodes summary
show accessnodes cws oper-data connection-status
show accessnodes cws <node> version verbose
show accessnodes cws <node> inventory capability
show accessnodes cws <node> <rat> cell <cell> counters
show accessnodes cws <node> <rat> cell <cell> neighbors
accessnodes cws <node> <rat> cell <cell> admin-state <locked|unlocked>
accessnodes cws <node> reboot
accessnodes cws <node> software update file-url <sftp url>
accessnodes cws <node> software update-status

# ---- 4G ----
show venb s1ap peer-state <venb> cws        # towards the cells
show venb s1ap peer-state <venb> mme        # towards the MME
clear venb s1ap statistics access|core
clear venb gtpu statistics access|core
clear venb sctp statistics access|core
clear venb paging-statistics to-CWS | to-EPC
clear x2gw x2ap statistics access|core <x2gw>

# ---- 3G / 2G ----
show vrnc ranap | show vrnc m3ua
show vbsc bssap | show vbsc abis | show vbsc sccp peer-state <x> msc
show vbsc rtp statistics access|core <x>
show vpcu status | show vpcu ns | show vpcu bssgp statistics access|core

# ---- platform health ----
show system version verbose | show alarm list | show events history
show system crash list | show resource cpu | show resource memory
show redundancy status | show switchover count | system cluster switchover
show ipsec ike-statistics global | show fastpath statistics ipv4 | show fastpath statistics acl
system configuration backup file-url file:///<path>
  • Typical test pattern: clear ... statistics on both access and core, run the call model, then show both sides and the per-peer counters. Automation does exactly this around every test case.
  • Profiles: cell behaviour comes from profiles (frequency, protocol parameters, TDD frame, UE measurement events) attached to cells; inspect with show running-config profiles ... and show running-config accessnodes cws <node> ... cell <c> profiles.
  • Engineering shell: unhide engg then system debug-shell exposes the Linux shell for core files and logs — use only on lab systems.
Level 2 — how a 3G HNB registers through the HNG
When a 3G stack (the "HNB" model, e.g. a 3G vRU running on a vBBU) registers to the HNG, the HNG is its TR-069 management server and its HNBGW at the same time:
  • The node's factory config sets ManagementServer.URL to the HNG (its cpe.php endpoint) — that is how the node finds its ACS.
  • On the HNG, the access node accessnodes cws <name> has an id that must equal the node's DeviceInfo.SerialNumber, plus a hardware-class-id. The value itself does not matter; a mismatch means the node is never recognised.
