  • Other identity fields: ModelName HNB, ProductClass, ManufacturerOUI, personality NodeH; the transport layer name in the attributes file must match the stack build.
  • On a vBBU the 3G stack is two processes: oamc (OAM + TR-069 client) and rnc, which runs as a DPDK secondary process sharing the PHY's file prefix. Start order: LRCP server and PHY first, then oamc, then rnc. The -e<iface> argument must name the interface that has IP reach to the HNG. Enable core dumps (ulimit -c unlimited) before starting.
Northbound, the management system creates such a device by REST with an id of the form OUI-ProductClass-SerialNumber and the device type for a vNode, then pushes single- and multi-instance parameters (neighbour lists intra-freq/inter-freq/GSM/E-UTRAN, measurement instances, SCTP associations) to /api/v1/deviceconfiguration/params/<id>.
Level 3 — debugging patterns and pitfalls
  • Cell up, no calls: check show venb s1ap peer-state towards both cws and mme. A healthy cell side with a down MME side means every S1 message is queued or rejected at the HNG.
  • Node never appears in show accessnodes: wrong management URL, serial number not matching the cws id, or IPsec to the SecGW not established (show ipsec ike-statistics global).
  • Paging problems: compare paging-statistics to-EPC (received from the MME) with to-CWS (fanned out to cells). A big ratio difference points at TAI/LAC mapping on the HNG.
  • After a switchover: verify show redundancy status, the switchover count and that SCTP peers re-established on both access and core; redundancy suites check S1AP and X2GW behaviour across exactly this event.
  • Crashes: show system crash list and show events history first; the alarm list may already have cleared.
  • 2G/3G peers: SCCP/M3UA peer-state to the MSC/SGSN is the first check before any RANAP or BSSAP analysis — same rules as GSMCORE01 (point codes, network indicator, server/client roles must mirror).
Interview Q&A
Q: What does the HNG do in a 4G network?
A: It aggregates many eNBs/vNodes: it proxies S1AP (one S1 per MME instead of one per cell), relays GTP-U, fans out paging, and can act as an X2 gateway so cells do not need full-mesh X2. It also provides IPsec SecGW and TR-069 management functions.
Q: What do "access" and "core" mean in HNG statistics?
A: Access is the radio-node side (cws); core is the EPC/MSC/SGSN side. Clearing and comparing both tells you whether a message was lost before, inside or after the gateway.
Q: A 3G node boots but never registers to the HNG. What do you check?
A: That its ManagementServer.URL points at the HNG, that the HNG cws id equals the node's DeviceInfo.SerialNumber, that the stack interface argument is the one with IP reach to the HNG, and IPsec if a SecGW is in the path.
Q: How would you prove the HNG is not the cause of an S1 setup failure?
A: Clear S1AP statistics on access and core, retry, then compare: if the S1 Setup/initial messages are forwarded on the core side and the MME answers with a failure, the problem is on the core side; peer-state towards the MME must also be up.
Q: Why does the HNG need redundancy tests specifically for S1AP and X2GW?
A: Because it concentrates many cells: a switchover must re-establish every SCTP association on both sides and preserve or cleanly rebuild UE contexts, otherwise a single failure drops all cells behind it.
Added: isolated libvirt network for an HNG redundancy pair
Lab tip — a redundancy pair on one KVM host. Two HNG VMs acting as a redundant pair need a private link between them that the rest of the lab cannot see. Create an isolated libvirt network and attach both VMs to it:
virsh net-define hng-ha-net.xml     # <network> with its own bridge name and no <forward>
virsh net-start hng-ha-net
virsh net-autostart hng-ha-net      # survives host reboot
virsh attach-interface <vm> network hng-ha-net --model virtio --config   # for each VM
virsh net-list --all
Leaving out net-autostart is the classic mistake: after a host reboot the VMs start but the redundancy link is missing, and both may decide to go active.
Protocol-level bugs, negative tests & transport
S1AP01 Decoding an S1 Setup Response Byte by Byte - the ServedGUMMEIs Multi-PLMN Interop Bug (TS 36.413 9.1.8.5 / 9.3.4) Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
When an eNB connects to an MME it sends S1 SETUP REQUEST and the MME answers with S1 SETUP RESPONSE, telling the eNB which PLMNs, MME group IDs and MME codes it serves. One information element, ServedGUMMEIs, is a list of lists: the outer list is per RAT, the inner servedPLMNs list is per PLMN. A third-party core put each of two PLMNs into its own outer entry. Wireshark decoded it happily (the ASN.1 allows up to 8 entries), but a conformant eNB reads only the first entry as the LTE pool - so the second PLMN silently disappeared, or S1 setup failed with unknown-PLMN. This card teaches how to read that IE byte by byte and how to turn a capture into a vendor bug report the vendor cannot argue with. For the S1 Setup call flow itself see ENBUP01; for S1/X2 capture filters see CAP3.
Figure 1 - ServedGUMMEIs: outer list = RAT, inner list = PLMN
Both shapes satisfy the ASN.1 SIZE(1..8); only the right one satisfies the semantics in TS 36.413 section 9.1.8.5.
Level 1 - Core facts: what S1 SETUP RESPONSE carries
S1 Setup is procedureCode 17; S1AP runs over SCTP with payload protocol identifier (PPID) 18 on port 36412. The successful outcome carries:
IEidCriticalityMeaning
MMEname61ignoreOptional text name of the MME
ServedGUMMEIs105rejectPLMNs + MME group IDs + MME codes this MME serves
RelativeMMECapacity87ignore0-255, weight for MME selection in a pool
ServedGUMMEIs ::= SEQUENCE (SIZE (1..maxnoofRATs)) OF ServedGUMMEIsItem
ServedGUMMEIsItem ::= SEQUENCE {
    servedPLMNs     ServedPLMNs,      -- SIZE(1..32)    OF PLMNidentity (3 octets)
    servedGroupIDs  ServedGroupIDs,   -- SIZE(1..65535) OF MME-Group-ID (2 octets)
    servedMMECs     ServedMMECs,      -- SIZE(1..256)   OF MME-Code     (1 octet)
    iE-Extensions   ... OPTIONAL,     -- may carry GUMMEI Type
    ... }
Bound (36.413 9.1.8.5)ValueScope
maxnoofRATs8outer ServedGUMMEIs list - one item per RAT
maxnoofPLMNsPerMME32servedPLMNs inside an item
maxnoofGroupIDs65535servedGroupIDs - "per node per RAT"
maxnoofMMECs256servedMMECs - "per node per RAT"
The rule that matters - the semantics column for Served GUMMEIs says the LTE pool configuration goes in the first place in the list. Extra items exist only for GUMMEIs of other RATs and should carry the GUMMEI Type extension (native / mapped / mappedFrom5G) so the eNB can route the UE to the right MME (36.413 section 8.7.3.2). The same structure and rule apply to MME CONFIGURATION UPDATE.
Level 2 - Decode the IE by hand (APER, aligned)
Filter s1ap.procedureCode == 17, select the ServedGUMMEIs ProtocolIE-Field and copy its value bytes. Length determinants come straight from the bounds above:
FieldConstraintEncoding
ServedGUMMEIs countSIZE(1..8)3 bits, value = count - 1
Item preambleextension bit + iE-Extensions present bit2 bits
servedPLMNs countSIZE(1..32)5 bits, value = count - 1, then pad to octet
PLMNidentityOCTET STRING (3)3 octets, aligned
servedGroupIDs countSIZE(1..65535)2 octets, value = count - 1
MME-Group-IDOCTET STRING (2)2 octets
servedMMECs countSIZE(1..256)1 octet, value = count - 1
MME-CodeOCTET STRING (1)1 octet
Failing core - 21 octets, 2 outer items:
20 00 00 f1 10 00 00 00 02 00 01 | 00 00 f2 20 00 00 00 02 00 01

20        001 ..... outer count-1 = 1  -> 2 items
          ... 00 .. item[0] ext / iE-Ext absent (no GUMMEI Type!)
          ...... 000 + next octet top 2 bits = servedPLMNs count-1 = 0 -> 1 PLMN
00        (rest is padding)
00 f1 10  PLMN  MCC 001 / MNC 01
00 00     servedGroupIDs count = 1
00 02     MME-Group-ID = 2
00        servedMMECs count = 1
01        MME-Code = 1
-- item[1] --
00 00 f2 20 ...  1 PLMN = MCC 002 / MNC 02, MMEGI 2, MMEC 1 (byte-identical pool data)
Expected for the same two PLMNs - 14 octets, 1 outer item:
00 40 00 f1 10 00 f2 20 00 00 00 02 00 01
00        outer count = 1, ext/iE-Ext absent
40        servedPLMNs count-1 = 1 -> 2 PLMNs, then padding
00 f1 10  001-01      00 f2 20  002-02
00 00 | 00 02 | 00 | 01   one MMEGI = 2, one MMEC = 1
PLMN BCD (TS 23.003 / 24.008): octet 1 = MCC2|MCC1, octet 2 = MNC3|MCC3, octet 3 = MNC2|MNC1 (low nibble first; MNC3 = F means 2-digit MNC). So 00 f1 10 = 001-01 and 64 f0 20 = 460-02. Sanity check: the number of octets your decode consumes must equal the open-type length on the wire (21, 20 and 14 for the three messages here) - if it does not, your field boundaries are wrong.
Level 3 - Attribution method, impact and the bug report
Why it is the core, not the eNB. Capture a reference core against the same eNB in the same lab. The reference answered with 1 outer item holding 4 PLMNs, one MMEGI and one MMEC (20 octets) and interoperated. The failing core used 2 items x 1 PLMN with identical MMEGI/MMEC and no GUMMEI Type. Item[1] carried no new GUMMEI information at all - it existed only to add a PLMN, which is not what the outer list is for. Note the subtlety: the message is ASN.1-valid (SIZE(1..8) is satisfied), so every decoder shows it cleanly; the non-conformance is against the semantics text. That is the argument you must cite.
eNB provisioned forObserved result
PLMN in item[0] (001-01)S1 setup completes; the second PLMN never enters the routing table - UEs of that PLMN have no path. Silent failure.
PLMN only in item[1] (002-02)No configured PLMN matches -> S1 SETUP FAILURE (or MME CONFIGURATION UPDATE FAILURE) with cause unknown-PLMN.
Why it hides: single-PLMN bring-up works perfectly; it only bites in multi-PLMN / RAN-sharing deployments, where it is blocking. Re-test after every core upgrade with at least two PLMNs.

Bug-report template that worked: component and interface (MME, S1-MME), message and IE id (S1 SETUP RESPONSE, IE 105), spec version and clause (TS 36.413 Rel-17, 9.1.8.5 and 9.3.4), severity (blocking for multi-PLMN), evidence (failing pcap and reference pcap), annotated hex of the one IE in both, the exact expected encoding, the octet-level diff, and the requested action ("encode all PLMNs of one MME in servedPLMNs of the first ServedGUMMEIsItem, in S1 Setup Response and MME Configuration Update").
Interview Q&A
Q: What is the difference between the outer ServedGUMMEIs list and the servedPLMNs list?
A: The outer list is dimensioned by RAT (max 8) and the first entry must be the LTE pool. servedPLMNs (max 32) inside an entry lists every PLMN that MME serves. Multiple PLMNs of one MME go in servedPLMNs, not in extra outer entries.
Q: Wireshark decodes the S1 Setup Response with no errors, yet multi-PLMN fails. How can that be?
A: The encoding is ASN.1-legal (2 items is within SIZE(1..8)) but violates the semantics: the receiver treats item[1] as another RAT. Decoders check syntax, not semantics, so compare against the spec text and a known-good core.
Q: Decode the first two octets 20 00 of a ServedGUMMEIs value.
A: 0x20 = 001 00 000: outer count-1 = 1 so 2 items, ext and iE-Extensions bits 0, then the first 3 bits of the 5-bit servedPLMNs count; with the top 2 bits of 0x00 the count-1 is 0, i.e. 1 PLMN; the rest is padding.
Q: How do you decode PLMN bytes 64 f0 20?
A: Nibble-swapped BCD: 64 -> MCC digits 4,6; f0 -> MCC digit 0 and MNC3 = F (2-digit MNC); 20 -> MNC 0,2. Result MCC 460, MNC 02.
Q: Why is this bug easy to miss in lab bring-up?
A: Most labs run a single PLMN, which works. The failure appears only with two or more PLMNs on one MME - either silently (second PLMN unusable) or as unknown-PLMN rejection depending on the eNB provisioning.
NEGT01 Negative & robustness testing of RAN signalling - SCTP/S1AP/X2AP mangling, reproducing field crashes and configuration-maximum limits Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
Positive tests prove that a gateway or base station works when every peer behaves. Field crashes almost never come from that path. They come from a peer that sends an unusual-but-legal option, skips an answer, answers late, or from an operator who configures more objects than anyone tested. Negative and robustness testing deliberately creates those conditions in the lab. The PW protocol simulator (PW-SIM / LTESIM) has a mangling engine for this: it can rewrite any field of an outgoing SCTP packet (IP header, SCTP common header, chunk header, chunk parameters), inject IPv6 extension headers, and at the application layer add, change, skip or delay S1AP / X2AP messages. This card covers how the mangler works, how real customer escapes were turned into repeatable test cases, and a configuration-maximum crash that shows why "the YANG schema allows 32" does not mean "32 works".
Where the mangling happens
SCTP rewriting happens after the kernel has formed the packet, so even fields the kernel normally owns (verification tag, TSN, checksum) can be corrupted.
Level 1 - core facts
Supported nodes: HNB, MSC, SGSN, eNodeB and MME objects in the simulator. Enable per node with keyword arguments when the object is created:
  • sctp_mangle=True - rewrite outgoing packets (needs the bundled python-nfqueue / netfilterqueue modules installed).
  • sctp_mangle_incoming=True - also rewrite received packets (off by default).
  • crosspath=True - if an outgoing INIT_ACK, COOKIE_ACK, HEARTBEAT_ACK or SACK leaves from a source IP different from the destination IP of the last received packet, rewrite the source to match. This imitates a multi-homed peer answering on a different path - something the Linux kernel itself never does.
Three call styles: mangle_sctp(msg_id, key, value, ...) for the next matching chunk, mangle_sctp_n(msg_id, n, ...) for the n-th, mangle_sctp_range(msg_id, from, to, ...) for a range. SIGTRAN nodes (SGSN, MSC) take an extra ASP id from the simulator config.
Chunk ids: Id_SCTP_INIT, INIT_ACK, COOKIE_ECHO, COOKIE_ACK, DATA, SACK, HEARTBEAT, HEARTBEAT_ACK, ABORT, ERROR, SHUTDOWN, SHUTDOWN_ACK, SHUTDOWN_COMPLETE, ECNE, CWR.
LayerKey prefixWhat can be changedTypical negative test
IPv4 headerip.version, header length, DSCP, total length, id, flags, TTL, protocol, checksum, source/destination addressWrong source address on INIT_ACK (path mismatch), bad checksum
SCTP common headersctp_header.source/destination port, verification tag, checksumStale verification tag: value their_flow_-1 reuses the peer tag of the previous association
Chunk headersctp_chunk.type, flags, lengthLength that disagrees with payload, unknown chunk type
Fixed chunk fields<Field>.value / .hexINIT: initiate tag, a_rwnd, in/out streams, initial TSN; SACK: cumulative TSN ack, gap-ack blocks; DATA: TSN, stream id, SSN, PPIDZero outbound streams, a_rwnd of 0, gap-ack blocks pointing at TSNs never sent
Variable parameters<IE>.<n>.value|hex|delete|positionIPv4/IPv6 address, cookie preservative, host name, supported address types, ECN, forward-TSN, state cookie, heartbeat info, all error causesDelete ECN, duplicate an address, move a parameter to last
Message levelkeyword argsskip, delay (seconds), reject (sends ABORT instead)Never answer INIT, answer COOKIE_ECHO 2 s late
IPv6 extension headers
For IPv6 associations the mangler can insert extension headers in front of SCTP with keys ip.extension.<n>.type / value / next / length (n = 0, 1, ... in chain order). Types use the protocol numbers of RFC 2460: 0 hop-by-hop, 60 destination options, 43 routing, 44 fragment, 51 AH; 50 (ESP) is not supported. type and value are mandatory; next and length are filled in automatically unless you override them (override them on purpose to build malformed chains). value is hex without the next/length bytes, and you must pad so that the whole header is a multiple of 8 bytes; length counts 8-byte units beyond the first 8 (0 means an 8-byte header). ip.extension.<n>.delete removes one. Classic test: a hop-by-hop Router Alert option (050200000000) on the SCTP INIT - a correct stack must still bring the association up.
Level 2 - how to run a negative test
  1. Create the simulated peer with the mangler enabled (above). For SGSN/MSC pass the ASP id from the simulator config.
  2. Arm the rule before the procedure: the rule fires when the kernel next emits that chunk (or the n-th one).
  3. Run the procedure (association set-up, S1 Setup, attach, handover) and capture on both sides - the capture is the proof that the field really went out wrong.
  4. Judge the device under test against the spec: correct ABORT / ERROR cause, no crash, no stuck state, association recovers, counters move.
Multi-homing / ASCONF: enable net.sctp.addip_enable=1 and net.sctp.addip_noauth_enable=1 (sysctl -p after every reboot), then add_ip(...) / del_ip(...) on the node to send ASCONF to a live association; the ASCONF itself can be mangled too.
Mangle sets (MME only): add_sctp_mangle_set(client_ip, [chunk ids], key, value, ...) applies a standing rule to every packet sent to one gateway address (empty list = all chunk types); delete_sctp_mangle_set and print_sctp_mangle_set manage them. A one-shot rule given later overrides the set. Useful to make a whole association look as if it comes from a different interface.
# create nodes with the mangler attached
enb1 = eNodeB("enb1", sctp_mangle=True, crosspath=True)
mme1 = MME("mme1", sctp_mangle=True, sctp_mangle_incoming=True)

# transport-layer examples
enb1.mangle_sctp(Id_SCTP_INIT, "NumberOfOutboundStreams.value", "0")
enb1.mangle_sctp(Id_SCTP_COOKIE_ECHO, "sctp_header.verification_tag", "their_flow_-1")
enb1.mangle_sctp(Id_SCTP_INIT, "ECN.0.delete", "None")
enb1.mangle_sctp(Id_SCTP_INIT, delay="2.0")           # answer late
enb1.mangle_sctp(Id_SCTP_INIT, reject="True")         # ABORT instead
enb1.mangle_sctp(Id_SCTP_ABORT, "sctp_chunk.flags", "1")

# send an unsolicited chunk (mangle it first if needed)
enb1.mangle_sctp(Id_SCTP_ERROR, "ErrorOutOfResource.0.hex", "")
enb1.send_sctp_msg(Id_SCTP_ERROR)

# application-layer examples (per UE session on the simulated MME)
sess = mme1.s1SessionsByIMSI["<imsi>"]
sess.mangle(Id_HandoverRequest, "TraceActivation.interfacesToTrace", "255")
sess.mangle(Id_HandoverRequest, "SRVCCOperationPossible", "possible")
mme1.mangle(Id_PathSwitchRequestAcknowledge, skip="True")
Field symptom (anonymised)How it was reproduced in the simulator
Aggregator crash on S1 Handover Required with a very large RRC containerEnable jumbo frames in the simulator globals (buffer 9600 bytes) and load an oversized RRC container payload, then hand over
vNode reboot on handover when rarely-used optional IEs are presentOn the MME session, add Handover Restriction List with several equivalent PLMNs, Trace Activation (all interfaces), SRVCC Operation Possible, then S1 handover
Session drop for a stationary UE - path-switch timeoutMME told to skip the Path Switch Request Acknowledge; check the aggregator does not send MME UE S1AP ID 0 back to the vNode in the failure
X2 GUMMEI all-zero, X2AP message-transfer decode abort, checksum assertMangle the X2AP IE to the edge value (all zeros, truncated, wrong length)
Cells stuck Out-of-Service after a gateway switchover; X2 Setup Failure on second cellSwitch over mid-traffic with TimeToWait in X2 Setup Failure and delayed (60 s) responses
Handover success rate fell from ~97.7 % to 87-90 % after an upgradeNot a crash - catch with a KPI regression run, not only pass/fail checks
Level 3 - the configuration-maximum crash (case study)
Symptom: a gateway configuration manager crashed repeatedly at a customer once the vENB peers list grew past 26 MME pools (3 MMEs per pool). The YANG schema allowed 32 pools. Crashes came during subscriber-replay events (task restart, HA fail-over, pod bounce), as heap corruption ending in an assert inside the configuration-database diff walk.
Root cause: the peers configuration was serialised into a fixed 8 KB IPC buffer (4 x 2048; 8,152 B usable after a 40 B header) with no chunking. Cost model: ~400 B top structure, ~110 B per MME, ~228 B per pool with 3 MMEs, ~150 B per RIC peer. With ~3.1 KB fixed cost only ~5 KB remained for pools, i.e. ~22-28 pools depending on how many unique MMEs - exactly the field signature "26 works, 27 crashes". The old advice "stay at 25" was this math plus margin.
Fix (LTE4G-20276): buffer raised to 64 KB (0xFFFE, one byte under the IPC maximum). At full YANG maximum (32 MMEs, 32 pools x 32 MMEs, 32 RICs) the payload is ~34.6 KB, leaving ~30 KB head-room.
The audit found the next one: zone configuration is serialised into a fixed 64,000 B buffer, also unchunked. A code comment assumed ~500 B per zone, but an empty zone already costs 1,096 B because it embeds a 255-entry TAC list header (1,022 B). Per-zone cost = 1,096 + 12/TAC + 28/PLMN + 12/emergency-area id, so the real ceiling is ~54 light zones, ~49 moderate, ~35 heavy and only ~14 at YANG-max content - against a schema limit of 128. Other families were safe: virtual-network config uses a stateless iterator (no buffer), and vENB instance / routing config already chunk (iterate-and-resume with a space-available check) - the pattern recommended everywhere.
LessonTest-design rule
Schema limit is not a supported limitFor every list, test at YANG max and with every sub-list at its max (worst-case bytes, not just count)
Crashes appear on replay, not on first commitAfter loading max config, restart the config task, fail over HA and bounce the pod
Edge IEs come from other vendorsKeep one test per rare optional IE seen in a field trace; mangle it into the simulator
"No answer" is a protocol stateSkip, delay and reject every response the device waits for; verify timers and cleanup
Related cards
SCTP and S1AP basics: see the Protocol page cards; X2 handover debugging: see PDX2; X2 gateway / S1 aggregation: see LF21; IPsec drop counters and backhaul debugging live on this page too.
Interview Q&A
Q: Why mangle after the kernel instead of building packets in user space?
A: The real kernel SCTP stack keeps the association state (tags, TSNs, retransmissions), so the rest of the conversation stays valid. Rewriting one field on its way out tests exactly one deviation at a time.
Q: What does crosspath simulate?
A: A multi-homed peer that answers from a different address than the one the request was sent to. The simulator rewrites the source of INIT_ACK, COOKIE_ACK, HEARTBEAT_ACK and SACK to the last received destination IP.
Q: How would you test IPv6 extension-header handling on an S1 link?
A: Insert a hop-by-hop Router Alert option (type 0, value 050200000000) or a routing header in front of SCTP INIT with ip.extension.0.*; the association must still come up, or be rejected cleanly - never crash.
Q: A gateway crashes at 27 pools although the schema allows 32. How do you find the root cause?
A: Measure the serialised size per object, compare with the IPC buffer, look for fixed buffers without chunking. Here 8 KB held only ~22-28 pools. Fix with a bigger buffer or chunked iterate-resume, then test at the full YANG max with full sub-lists.
Q: How do you turn a field escape into a regression test?
A: Find the exact unusual message or missing response in the customer trace, reproduce it with the simulator mangler (add the optional IEs, skip the ack, oversize the container), and add it to the automated suite.
ACSRCA01 Cell Permanently DOWN With No Alarm — a TR-069 BOOTSTRAP retry that wiped the provisioning queue (CLD-29105 RCA) Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
On a vBBU each 4G vNode is a TR-069 device. When it boots for the first time it sends the cloud ACS an Inform with the event 0 BOOTSTRAP ("I am new, provision me"). The ACS then runs a queue of jobs against it: read a value, create the multi-instance objects (PLMN list entries, inter-frequency carriers), push several hundred parameters in one bulk SetParameterValues, and finally set AdminState=true. One of those hundreds of parameters is the RRH id each cell belongs to — and without it the OAM manager never asks the RRH manager to set the cell's frequency, so the cell never comes up.

This card is a real root-cause analysis (CLD-29105): a 60-second TCP timeout in exactly the wrong place, followed by an ACS retry handler that threw away the half-finished provisioning queue, left one vNode of a two-vNode BBU permanently down with no alarm. It is a model for how to debug "one of N cells never comes up", and a lesson in non-idempotent operations and retries. See REXDBG01 for the cell-up evidence chain, CM16/RRHMGR01 for the RRH activation messages and CELLUP01 for the 4G vNode bring-up.
Figure — two vNodes, one timeout, two outcomes (own drawing, simplified)
The divergence is how far the first BOOTSTRAP session got before the timeout, not the timeout itself.
Level 1 — the symptom, the happy path and the discriminator
Symptom. A 4G BBU with two vNodes and two RUs. After an upgrade or a vNode pod restart, vNode 2's cells stay DOWN for 12–17 hours and counting, with no alarm, no error, no user-visible indication. Seen twice on two different builds.
  • RRH manager syslog: thousands of lines for vNode 1, zero for vNode 2.
  • vNode 2 OAM manager: Frequency configuration not received for cell X, repeated forever.
  • vNode 1 received 791 parameters by SetParameterValues; vNode 2 only about 32 (the ones created by AddObject).
  • LTE_RRH_ID populated on vNode 1, empty on all four cells of vNode 2.
The happy path (it is entirely TR-069 driven — not an inventory IPC to the RRH manager, as first assumed):
StepWhat happens
1vNode Inform with 0 BOOTSTRAP, 1 BOOT, 4 VALUE CHANGE → InformResponse
2GetParameterValues (AdminState)
3AddObject × N — PLMN list entries, inter-frequency carriers (one job per multi-instance key)
4bulk SetParameterValues — all single- and multi-instance parameters (791 here), including LTE_RRH_ID, using the instance numbers the AddObjects returned
5SetParameterValues AdminState = true
6MIB updated → OAM manager sends SET_FREQ_REQ → RRH manager → RRH_READY → cell up
The discriminator that cracked it: compare sibling nodes. Count of parameters received by SPV (791 vs ~32) and one key identifier (RRH id: set vs empty) point straight at provisioning, long before anyone reads ACS code.
Level 2 — how to check it on a live system and how to recover
Checks, in order, for "one of N vNodes never comes up":
# 1. which vNode does the RRH manager know about? (BBU pod syslog)
grep -c 'vNodeId=1' /var/log/messages ; grep -c 'vNodeId=2' /var/log/messages
# 2. is the OAM manager waiting for frequency config? (affected vNode pod)
grep 'Frequency configuration not received' /var/log/messages | tail -3
# 3. is the RRH id in the MIB empty for the affected cells?  (LTE_RRH_ID per cell)
# 4. TR-069 client log of the affected vNode: list every session, its Inform events and RPCs
grep -E 'EVENT_0_BOOTSTRAP|SetParameterValues|AddObject|CONNECTION_FAILURE|BOOTSTRAP_COMPLETED' <tr069 log>
What vNode 2's TR-069 log showed:
SessionTypeRPCs from the ACSResultSPV?
1BOOTSTRAPGPV + 8 AddObject, then the CPE waits for the next commandfail: 60 s TCP timeoutno
2BOOTSTRAP retryGPV + 4 AddObject, then an empty response (= session end)success, bootstrap marked completeno
3VALUE CHANGEnonesuccessno
4–17PERIODICGPV polling of operational state onlysuccessno
The TR-069 client behaved correctly: it saw the failure, re-injected 0 BOOTSTRAP, accepted the session end and recorded bootstrap complete. The defect was on the ACS side.

WorkaroundResultWhy
GUI "Save & Deploy" on the affected node (recommended)deterministic recoverycreates a fresh provisioning job through the REST path, outside BOOTSTRAP; it appends bulk SPV + SPV directly
Delete the vNode podmay recovera new BOOTSTRAP — but the same timing race can recur
Delete the local cell-config snapshot + restartsame as abovethe client was not loading that file anyway
Hand-edit the ACS databasedo notdirect DB mutation risks inconsistency
Fault-injection test plan (the fix is only proven if the bug reproduces first): drop the TCP session at each boundary — before the GPV response, after GPV, after AddObject #4 (vNode 1 equivalent), after all AddObjects (the vNode 2 case — must fail before the fix and recover after), before the bulk SPV response, and after the CPE applied SPV but before the ACS saw the response (bulk SPV must be safely re-sent). Then regress: clean 2-vNode deploy, both vNodes failing at the same point, 4-vNode concurrent bootstrap, ACS pod restart during bootstrap — pass = every cell up with SPV delivered.
Level 3 — the root cause, why it was permanent, and the lessons
Root cause. On every BOOTSTRAP Inform the ACS unconditionally deleted the device's whole pending job queue (the code even carried a "for time being" comment) and rebuilt it from the template. When session 1 died after all AddObjects had committed but before the bulk SPV, the surviving queue held exactly the two jobs that mattered — bulk SPV and AdminState SPV — and the retry destroyed them. The rebuilt queue re-issued AddObjects; AddObject is not idempotent in TR-069 (each call creates a new instance with a new number), so the instance indices the bulk SPV depends on no longer matched, and session 2 ended without any SetParameterValues.

Why vNode 1 survived the same timeout: its first session had only got through 4 AddObjects, so the rebuilt queue mostly created fresh instances with valid indices and the SPV went through. The boundary — how many AddObjects had committed — decided the outcome.

Why permanent: after bootstrap is marked complete, every session is periodic polling; nothing checks "bootstrap completed but no SPV ever received"; an empty RRH id means the OAM manager never sends SET_FREQ_REQ; and nothing alarms on that.

Fix and hardening (deliberately separate changes):
  • ACS: on a BOOTSTRAP retry, resume an existing non-empty queue instead of deleting it (~15 lines); later, skip AddObject for instances that already exist.
  • ACS observability: log/alarm when bootstrap completes without the bulk SPV executed.
  • TR-069 client: warn when bootstrap completes with zero SPV received.
  • OAM manager: alarm when the RRH id stays empty for more than ~10 minutes after full configuration.
Method lessons. (1) A first hypothesis — a timing race in the RRH manager's vNode lookup — was falsified by showing the function was never called (zero inventory requests in 17 hours); always prove the code path runs before blaming it. (2) Retries plus non-idempotent operations equal corrupted state; a retry must resume, not restart. (3) "Silent forever" failures need a watchdog on the absence of an expected event.
Interview Q&A
Q: One of two vNodes never brings its cells up and there is no alarm. What do you compare first?
A: The healthy and the broken sibling: how many parameters each received by SetParameterValues and whether key identifiers such as the RRH id are populated. 791 vs ~32 and an empty RRH id point to incomplete TR-069 provisioning rather than RU or IPC problems.
Q: Why does an empty RRH id keep a 4G cell down?
A: The OAM manager only sends SET_FREQ_REQ to the RRH manager when the cell has an RRH id. With it empty, no frequency is configured, no L1_DONE/RRH_READY follows and the cell never starts.
Q: Why is re-running AddObject on a retry dangerous?
A: AddObject is not idempotent: every call creates a new instance with a new instance number. Re-running them duplicates objects and invalidates the instance indices that the following SetParameterValues uses.
Q: What is the safe recovery for an affected node in the field?
A: Trigger a fresh provisioning job from the management GUI (Save & Deploy) which pushes the bulk SPV outside BOOTSTRAP. Restarting the pod may or may not help; never edit the ACS database by hand.
Q: How would you prove the fix?
A: Fault injection at the exact boundary — drop the TCP session after all AddObjects but before the bulk SPV. It must reproduce the dead cell before the fix and recover after it, plus regressions for other boundaries, multi-vNode and ACS restart.
IPSEC01 Backhaul IPsec on the vBBU — IKE SA vs CHILD SA, per-plane and per-pod child SAs, PFS and PSK, step-by-step tunnel debug, drop counters and a stale-conntrack RCA Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
A cell site's backhaul usually crosses networks the operator does not trust, so the base station wraps its S1/NG, X2/Xn, management and E2 traffic in an IPsec tunnel to a Security Gateway (SecGW) in front of the core. IPsec has two layers that are easy to confuse. The IKE SA (phase 1) is the authenticated control channel between the two peers — certificates or a pre-shared key, Diffie-Hellman, lifetimes, dead-peer detection. The CHILD SAs (phase 2) are the actual encrypted data channels, one per pair of traffic selectors ("these local addresses ↔ those remote subnets"), negotiated inside the IKE SA. One IKE SA can carry many CHILD SAs, and each can be added, removed or rekeyed without disturbing the others.

On the containerised vBBU the tunnel lives in the netcon network pod: strongSwan (the charon daemon, driven with swanctl) does IKE, and VPP does ESP encryption in the fast path. This card covers the design (child SAs per pod and per plane, PFS, PSK vs certificates), a step-by-step debug from "IKE won't come up" to "traffic flows one way", the drop counters, VPP throughput, and a field RCA in which tunnels did not come back after a routing change. Air-interface security is in SEC01; M-plane TLS/SSH in RUSEC01; certificate bundle install in CM39.
Figure — one IKE SA, several CHILD SAs (own drawing)
The BBU is the IKE initiator; children are split per plane (multi-child SA) or per pod so an address can move between workers.
Level 1 — the building blocks and the BBU defaults
  • Configuration path. Tunnel settings come from the vBBU deployment (backhaul profile → IPsec section) into the netcon ConfigMap; netcon renders connection files under its IPsec conn.d directory and loads them into strongSwan. A connection that inherits from a parent (also=<parent>) becomes an extra CHILD SA of that parent.
  • BBU client behaviour. The BBU is always the IKE initiator; DPD every 60 s with action restart; keyingtries 0 (retry forever); retransmit 1 s × 2, so a retry every ~2–3 s with no back-off; NAT traversal always on (ESP inside UDP 4500); masquerade rules on UDP 500/4500. Expected recovery once the path is back: about 3–5 s.
  • Child-SA PFS (childPfs in the IPsec section): default DH0 = no PFS, nothing is appended to the ESP proposals. Allowed groups: 14–18 (MODP 2048/3072/4096/6144/8192), 19–21 (ECP 256/384/521) and 31 (Curve25519). The chosen group is appended to every ESP proposal (AES-CBC 128–256 with SHA1…SHA512, and AES-GCM with 8/12/16-byte ICV). Both ends must agree.
  • Multi-child SA per plane. When the C, U, M and E2 planes sit in different subnets behind the SecGW, enable it in the backhaul profile and give each plane a remote network + mask; each becomes its own child (visible as tocore, tocore2 … or tocore-cplane, tocore-uplane). Disabled = one child with the classic single remote subnet. CLD-21798 widened the plane list from 4 to 11: u, x2-u, xn-u, f1-u, m, c, e2, x2-c, xn-c, f1-c and 2g-cu. U-plane children use the GTP-U addresses as local selector; the other planes use the manually configured local addresses minus the GTP-U ones.
  • Per-pod child SAs (cluster design rule). One IKE SA per remote endpoint per Kubernetes worker; one CHILD SA per fixed address set (vBBU, database) and per CU / DU / 2G / 4G vNode pod. On redeploy only the affected child is removed and re-added, so a pod address can float to another worker — a lab demo kept a 500 ms ping running through a neighbouring child while one child was moved.
  • Authentication. Default is certificates (public key). Per-BBU bundle with an end-entity cert, CA and key; the cert CN is the server serial and the SAN must list the OAM address and every local IPsec tunnel endpoint (every one, in multi-VLAN set-ups). Restart netcon after a certificate change. PSK is the lab-friendly alternative: one secret for all tunnels of a BBU (per-tunnel PSK is not supported), 8–128 characters with upper, lower, digit and special, no spaces or quotes. Certificates are preferred in live networks and at scale.
Level 2 — debugging a tunnel step by step
Top-down, stop at the first NO: netcon pod healthy (READY 2/2) → charon alive → IKE SA ESTABLISHED (phase 1) → every CHILD SA INSTALLED (phase 2) → bytes moving both ways.
kubectl get pod -n pw -l app=netcon -o wide          # 2/2 Running? restarts?
kubectl logs -n pw <netcon> -p --tail=100             # previous container log after CrashLoopBackOff
# inside the netcon pod:
swanctl --list-sas      # THE command: state, proposal, "rekeying in", per-SPI in/out bytes, selectors
swanctl --list-conns    # what is configured (not just what is up)
swanctl --log           # live IKE_SA_INIT / IKE_AUTH / CREATE_CHILD_SA
swanctl --stats         # fails if charon is hung
vppctl show int / show int addr / show ip fib / show errors
swanctl --initiate --child <child> ; swanctl --terminate --ike <conn> ; swanctl --load-all ; swanctl --rekey --child <child>
What --list-sas showsMeaningLook at
ESTABLISHED + all children INSTALLEDhealthybyte counters both directions, positive "rekeying in"
ESTABLISHED, no childphase 2 failedtraffic selectors, ESP proposal, PFS group mismatch
CONNECTINGphase 1 failingreachability on UDP 500/4500, IKE proposal, certificates
nothing listedcharon dead, gateway unreachable or certificate not loadednetcon log, --stats, cert files
negative "rekeying in"rekey overdue / stuck SAlog around the rekey, peer lifetimes
Log / errorUsual cause
retransmit ... giving upno reply on UDP 500/4500 — firewall, routing, NAT path
NO_PROPOSAL_CHOSENno common cipher / integrity / DH group (on CREATE_CHILD_SA: often PFS on one side only, e.g. gateway phase 2 with MODP 2048 while the BBU has PFS off)
DH group ECP_256 unacceptable, requesting CURVE_25519DH group mismatch
AUTH_FAILED / "peer didn't accept any certificate"CA chain or identity mismatch
TS_UNACCEPTABLEphase-2 subnets differ between the two sides
no private key found for <local IP>misleading: the device certificate SAN does not contain that local tunnel endpoint — check with openssl x509 -text
One-way traffic is as bad as down — applications time out while IPsec "looks up". In the child's counters: in = 0, out > 0 → the remote side is not routing return traffic into the tunnel (or its selectors are wrong); in > 0, out = 0 → local routing — check vppctl show ip fib.
INSTALLED but zero bytes: trace the fast path (vppctl trace add dpdk-input 50, then show trace): ipsec4-output-feature → error-drop means the destination is outside the negotiated remote selector.
Test properly: never use Linux ping inside the pod — it bypasses VPP and the IPsec policy; use vppctl ping <dst> source <loopback-if>. Capture with tcpdump "esp or udp port 500 or udp port 4500".
Three sources must agree per tunnel: the ConfigMap local/remote endpoints, the swanctl local/remote addresses, and an address actually bound on a VPP interface. A mismatch means config generation went wrong — restart the pod to regenerate.
netcon log signatures: "VPP did not become ready after 90s" (SR-IOV VF / PCI device missing), "VICI not responsive" (charon hung), exit code 137 = OOM-killed, 139 = segfault, a rising restart count.

Fast-path drop counters (same families for IPv4 and IPv6) — which family rises localises the fault:
Counter familyMeaning
no-SA dropspacket matched a policy but there is no SA (yet) — tunnel down or rekey gap
no-SPD / no-policyno security policy matched — selectors do not cover this traffic
policy-blockpolicy says discard
sequence / replay errorsanti-replay window: reordering, duplicates or a peer restarted counters
ESN-flag dropsextended-sequence-number mismatch between peers
pre-frag DF droppacket needed fragmenting before encryption but had DF set — an MTU problem
SecGW side (lab firewall example): phase 1 IKEv2 with mutual certificates, AES-256 / SHA-256 / DH 14, the peer identity matching the certificate (distinguished name or SAN); phase 2 tunnel mode, ESP AES-256 / SHA-256, PFS matching the BBU, selectors that mirror the BBU's (local = core host or SMO southbound subnet, remote = the pods' inner addresses, kept tight). The core needs a return route via the gateway; in a cloud VPC disable the source/destination check on the gateway instance and private-address blocking on its WAN.
Level 3 — scale, throughput, design-review lessons and a field RCA
Scale. 11 children per tunnel means 22 ESP SAs per tunnel, and with ~27 manual addresses about 190 policies per direction — keep selectors tight and watch rekey load.
Design-review lessons from the 11-plane change:
  • Plane chosen by substring match: "2g-cu-plane" contains "u-plane", so it was handed GTP-U selectors. Match names exactly.
  • The secondary-SecGW connection file reused the primary's child names, so after a failover only the first child negotiated (CLD-32361). Child names must be unique per connection.
  • A stale SA after a device reboot: the gateway still holds the old SA (DPD not yet expired) and ignores the new IKE_SA_INIT — clear the SA on the gateway.
VPP throughput (one worker core, 10G NIC, server-class embedded CPU, 1308-byte packets, AES-CBC-128, one tunnel): about 6.1 Gbit/s UL GTP-U + IPsec encrypt, graph pw_s1u_agg_ul → ipsec4-output → esp-encrypt → tx. The DL test topped out near 1.1 Gbit/s — limited by the test SecGW's transmit side, not netcon. Always check which end is the bottleneck before reporting a number.

Field RCA — "tunnels did not come back" (a customer, 4G). A route change on the gateway side deleted all routes in each table before adding the replacements, creating "blind windows" of 5 to 113 s with no route to the sites. 88 sites lost S1; 76 recovered on their own; 12 stayed down until an operator stopped chronyd, waited 120 s and started it again.
Analysis. There is no code path from chronyd to the IPsec manager — the 120 s wait was the fix, and it matches the Linux nf_conntrack_udp_timeout_stream default (120 s). Leading hypothesis: stale netfilter conntrack entries for the IKE/NAT-T flows (ESTABLISHED UDP, 120 s timeout, refreshed by every retry every 2–3 s) kept the old NAT mapping, so the BBU's IKE retries silently vanished. Sites whose entries were still NEW (30 s) or whose blind window was short recovered. The built-in "connection flush" (conntrack flush + restart all tunnels) existed but was commented out on all automatic triggers.
Method lesson. An attractive "software defect" hypothesis was disproven by checking which tunnel class actually runs — the suspected down-event handler was a no-op for that tunnel type. Proving tests: flush conntrack right after the route is restored (immediate recovery?), watch the stale entries during the outage, and wait exactly 120 s.
Ops lesson. Route changes must be add-before-delete (make before break).
Pitfalls
  • Calling a tunnel "up" because the IKE SA is ESTABLISHED — check every child and both byte counters.
  • Chasing "no private key" as a key problem when it is a missing SAN entry.
  • Enabling PFS on one side only: IKE comes up, children never do.
  • Testing with a kernel ping that never enters VPP.
  • Copying keys, PSKs or certificates into tickets or wiki pages — reference them, never paste them.
Interview Q&A
Q: What is the difference between an IKE SA and a CHILD SA?
A: The IKE SA (phase 1) is the authenticated control channel that negotiates keys, does DPD and rekeys. CHILD SAs (phase 2) are the ESP data channels, one per pair of traffic selectors, each a pair of unidirectional SAs with its own SPI. Many children can share one IKE SA and be added or removed independently.
Q: IKE is ESTABLISHED but the child never installs. What do you check?
A: Phase-2 parameters: ESP proposals, PFS group (on one side only gives NO_PROPOSAL_CHOSEN on CREATE_CHILD_SA) and traffic selectors (TS_UNACCEPTABLE). swanctl --log shows which.
Q: The child is INSTALLED, out bytes rise, in bytes stay zero. Where is the fault?
A: On the remote side: return traffic is not routed into the tunnel or its selectors do not cover our addresses. If it were in>0 and out=0 the problem would be local routing in VPP.
Q: strongSwan logs "no private key found for" a local IP. What is wrong?
A: Usually not the key: the BBU certificate SAN lacks that local tunnel endpoint address. Every local endpoint must be in the SAN, especially with multiple VLANs.
Q: After a backhaul route outage some sites recover only after a 120 s wait. Why?
A: Stale conntrack entries for the IKE/NAT-T UDP flow (120 s established-stream timeout, refreshed by retries) keep the old mapping so retries vanish. Flushing conntrack or waiting for expiry restores them; route changes should be make-before-break.
Added: geo-redundant SecGW failover and key-file permission checks
Geo-redundant security gateways. The backhaul profile can hold a primary and a secondary SecGW plus a revertible flag. The deployment service passes them through the configuration bundle into the netcon configmap; netcon reads them at start-up and tries the gateways one at a time:
situationrevertible = onrevertible = off
start-upIKEv2 to primaryIKEv2 to primary
primary goes downmove to secondarymove to secondary
primary comes back while on secondaryswitch back to primarystay on secondary
Test matrix: primary down / primary restored with revertible on and off; check tunnel state with swanctl --list-sas (or ipsec statusall) after each step, confirm the IKE peer address changed, measure the traffic gap during the move, and make sure there is no flapping back and forth when the primary is unstable. Only tunnel status at netcon was covered in the original feature test — end-to-end traffic continuity is the part worth adding.

Private-key permission hygiene. Extracted private keys must be readable by root only: -rw------- (mode 600). This applies to the default IPsec end-entity key (/opt/pw-config/ipsec_cert/Certificates/Certificate00/EndEntityCert/key.crt) written during FOCOM provisioning, and to the operator certificate key under /opt/pw-config/opcert/key/ that the certificate manager packages and distributes to the control-path and netcon components. Verify after each of: fresh FOCOM initialisation, a modify-cluster re-init (keys re-extracted), and the CMP certificate-manager flow:
ls -l key.crt; stat -c '%a %U %n' key.crt      # expect 600 root
find /opt/pw-config /var/run/pw -name 'key.crt' -exec stat -c '%a %n' {} \;
DPDKDBG01 Debugging a DPDK Fast-Path Gateway - Interface/NUMA Checks, Linux vs Fast-Path Tables, Drop Counters, SR-IOV VFs in KVM, VLAN Strip and the iova-mode Boot-Time RCA Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
A gateway such as the HNG (or a CU-UP built on VPP) does not forward user traffic through the Linux kernel. A DPDK fast path takes the NIC away from the kernel and polls it from dedicated CPU cores, processing packets in user space against flows the application installed. Anything it cannot handle - no flow, no route, unresolved ARP, missing IPsec policy - is sent to Linux as an exception. So when packets vanish you debug two stacks: is the fast path seeing the packet, does it agree with Linux about addresses, rules, routes and neighbours, and where exactly is the drop counter climbing? This card gives that checklist, the virtualisation traps (SR-IOV VFs in KVM, VLAN strip, memory pinning) and two real cases: a DPDK upgrade that tripled boot time, and VPP as an IPsec/GTP-U fast path. It complements CM29 (cloud-native platform) and OCUDU01 (DPDK in the DU).
Figure 1 - Fast path vs Linux: where a packet can go
Ports bound to the fast path are invisible to normal kernel tools; the kernel sees only exception traffic via a shared-memory queue.
Level 1 - Core facts: first five checks on any fast-path box
Set up the environment for fpdebug first (source the datapath setenv script from the runtime directory and add its bin directory to PATH). Then:
CheckCommandWhat it tells you
DPDK-owned portscat /proc/sys/dpvi/list_interfacesOnly these ports are processed by the fast path
Driverethtool -i <if>Poll-mode driver name and PCI bus-info
NIC NUMA nodecat /sys/bus/pci/devices/<bus-id>/numa_nodeFast-path cores must all come from this node
CPU / virtualisationlscpuThreads per core 1 = HT off (recommended); "Hypervisor vendor: KVM" = VM, "Virtualization: VT-x" only = bare metal
Fast-path configfast-path.env and pwenvCore masks, ports and options as intended
Linux and fast path must agree. Compare each pair - a missing entry on the fast-path side is a classic cause of traffic being punted or dropped:
Linux                              Fast path
ip addr show                       fpdebug addr4 <if>   /  fpdebug addr6 <if>
ip rule show   / ip -6 rule show   fpdebug dump-pbr-rule4 all / dump-pbr-rule6 all
ip route show table all            fpdebug route4 type all / route6 type all
ip neigh show                      fpdebug neigh4 type all / neigh6 type all
Level 2 - Measure: counters, captures and VM set-up
Walk the counters from the wire inwards - NIC, fast-path core, fast-path stats, exception queue, kernel:
ToolLook at
fpdebug stats non-zerofp_dropped, IpInDelivers (to local stack), IpForwDatagrams (forwarded), exception counters such as ECMP_NDISC_NEEDED, ICMP_NEEDED, IKE_NEEDED
ethtool -S <if>Drops at the NIC before DPDK ever saw the packet (rx_missed, no-buffer)
fp-cpu-usagePer-core fast-path load - a core at 100 % drops under bursts
fp-shmem-dpviDrops in the queue between the fast path and the kernel (exception path overloaded)
Per-module debug statsGroups exist for IPsec, GTP-U, GRE, RTP, ePDG/TWAG and drop reasons - check the module that owns the flow
Capturing under load: never run an open-ended tcpdump on a busy gateway. Rotate a 10 s ring and keep only the newest two files:
tcpdump -i <if> -w cap.pcap-%s -G 10 &      # new file every 10 s
# companion loop: every few seconds delete all but the newest 2 cap.pcap-* files
# stop tcpdump explicitly when done
SR-IOV VFs into a KVM guest:
  • Use <interface type='hostdev' managed='yes'> (vfio driver) instead of a raw <hostdev> - libvirt then sets the VF MAC and VLAN before passthrough, so the guest does not see "new hardware" after every host reboot.
  • Keep the PCI slot/function identical on both redundant VMs so interface names match (virsh dumpxml, virsh edit).
  • Device assignment pins all guest memory - memory over-commit with SR-IOV caused OOM crashes at VM boot. Size host RAM for the full guest allocation.
  • Bonding: active-backup between VFs from different PFs is fine but needs ip link set <pf> vf <n> trust on so the guest MAC change is accepted. Put LACP on the host PF, never between VFs in the guest, and never on VFs of the same PF.
  • VM hygiene: virtio disk with cache=none (not IDE); VGA rather than QXL video on old 3.10 kernels (memory leak); serial console via GRUB console=ttyS0,115200; 2 MB or 1 GB huge pages.
The VLAN-strip trap: a VLAN set on the VF from the host (ip link set <pf> vf <n> vlan <id>) is stripped in hardware for a Linux guest. DPDK, however, defaults VLAN strip off, so once the fast path owns the port, tagged frames arrive and are dropped. Start the fast path with the VLAN-strip option (FPNSDK_OPTIONS=--vlan-strip in the pwenv file).

IPsec in user space - VPP + strongSwan in a VM: strongSwan does IKE (control plane) while VPP does ESP in user space through the vpp_sswan plugin; VPP's own ikev2 plugin is disabled and linux_cp is enabled with lcp-sync so Linux sees mirrored interfaces (build used DPDK 21.11, VPP 23.10, Ubuntu 22.04).
dpdk-devbind.py -s                              # list NICs and drivers
dpdk-devbind.py --bind=vfio-pci <pci-addr>     # stop VPP before unbinding again
# VM without IOMMU only (lab): vfio enable_unsafe_noiommu_mode=1
iperf3 -s -p 5000 -B <local-ip>
iperf3 -c <peer> -u -l 1024 -b <rate> -t 10 -B <src-ip>
# server ignores SYNs? checksum offload mismatch on a veth:
ethtool -K <veth> rx off tx off
Measured with one vCPU: kernel IPsec topped out near ~2 Gb/s with the client at 100 % CPU; VPP/DPDK IPsec reached ~5 Gb/s UDP before VM-interface loss. Older Xeon hosts (Haswell / Cascade Lake VMs) managed only ~1 Gb/s even without IPsec - host CPU generation matters; prefer Ice Lake class hosts.
Level 3 - Two real cases and the expert lessons
Case 1 - fast-path boot time tripled after a DPDK upgrade (UD-41020). After moving from DPDK 17.05 to 18.11 (via the vendor framework) the fast path took 150-180 s to start, about 200 % worse. Profiling showed mempool creation dominated: ~4-4.7 s per mempool. Root cause: 18.11 reworked memory management - instead of reserving physical segments and IOVA mappings up front at EAL start, it allocates physical memory, IOVA and virtual addresses at run time for every mempool. A vendor patch gave no improvement; the DPDK community recommended the EAL option --iova-mode=va. That requires a driver supporting both VA and PA modes and vfio-pci with a working IOMMU - which many lab servers did not have without BIOS/firmware updates, and forcing it there can break boot. So it was shipped as a CLI option (fast-path EAL options), not the default. Result where supported: boot time ~30 % above the old baseline instead of +200 %. Lesson: after any DPDK/vendor upgrade, measure boot time and per-mempool time, and treat IOMMU/vfio readiness as a platform prerequisite.

Case 2 - VPP as a CU-UP fast path (POC). A GTP-U tunnel with static IPsec was built in VPP: an SA per direction (ipsec sa add <id> spi <n> esp crypto-alg aes-cbc-256 ... integ-alg sha-256-128 ... tunnel), an SPD bound to the interface (ipsec spd add 1, set interface ipsec spd <if> 1), protect policies at priority 10, a bypass policy for ESP itself (protocol 50) at priority 100, then create gtpu tunnel src .. dst .. teid 14 tteid 13. The far end used Linux ip xfrm state/policy with matching SPIs (tunnel mode, cbc(aes) + sha256). Load came from the fast-path packet generator (GTP-U flow, 700-byte payload) on a 10G XL710 VF. Design finding: the kernel-module fast path could not run multiple instances per host and needed /dev and /dev/shm exposed to the container; VPP ran several instances without shared /dev/shm - more cloud-native. Open points: crypto offload (QAT), exception-traffic handling, separate C-plane and U-plane addresses.

Latency method: capture at one packet generator, send the probe tunnel at a very low rate, latency = return timestamp - send timestamp through one GTP-U tunnel (no IPsec). The source table's time units are inconsistent, so read the numbers as relative:
ConditionAverageMaxObservation
Low load (only the probe tunnel)~0.19~0.40Returns arrive in groups of ~4 - a sawtooth
Moderate/heavy load (8G background on a 2nd tunnel)~0.023~0.037Flat, low jitter
Latency dropped under load. At low load VPP's vector (batch) processing and polling return packets in bursts of ~4, so most packets wait for the batch; with background traffic the vectors fill quickly. Teaching point: always measure latency at more than one load point - a single idle measurement on a polling/vector data plane is misleading.

Other pitfalls: fast-path cores on the wrong NUMA node (cross-socket memory access); hyper-threading siblings shared with the fast path; exception queue drops hiding as "random loss" of control traffic (ARP, IKE); and a gateway that works in Linux tests but not via DPDK because a PBR rule or neighbour entry never synced.
Interview Q&A
Q: What is an "exception" packet in a DPDK fast path, and give three causes.
A: A packet the fast path cannot handle itself and hands to the Linux stack: no matching flow for a local packet, route not present, ARP/ND not resolved, or (ingress) IPsec security policy not found.
Q: Traffic works with the kernel but drops once the fast path owns a VF that has a host-set VLAN. Why?
A: The host VF VLAN is stripped in hardware for Linux, but DPDK defaults VLAN strip off, so tagged frames reach the fast path and are dropped. Start the fast path with the VLAN-strip option.
Q: Why should fast-path cores be on the same NUMA node as the NIC?
A: Packet buffers and descriptors live in memory local to the NIC's node; cores on another socket pay cross-socket memory latency on every packet, reducing throughput and adding jitter. Check /sys/bus/pci/devices/<bus-id>/numa_node and lscpu.
Q: A DPDK upgrade made boot time jump from tens of seconds to ~3 minutes. How do you approach it?
A: Profile the boot - here mempool creation dominated because the newer DPDK allocates memory and IOVA at run time. Use --iova-mode=va where vfio-pci and the IOMMU work, keep it optional elsewhere, and re-measure.
Q: Why did VPP latency look better under heavy load than at idle?
A: VPP processes packets in vectors and polls; at low rates packets wait to be batched (bursts of ~4), at high load vectors fill immediately. Measure latency at multiple load points.
Added: fast-path bring-up checks, CUPS user-plane CLI, sanity list, throughput method and more VM traps
1. Is the fast path up at all? Before reading drop counters, prove the fast path is running and owns the intended ports: 
fast-path.sh status ; linux-fp-sync.sh status   # both services running
cat /proc/sys/dpvi/list_interfaces             # ports the fast path processes
ethtool -i                                  # FP-owned ports show a net_* (PMD) driver
ls /var/run/fastpath/bound/                     # ports bound at start
ip link show fpn0                               # fast-path <-> kernel exception interface exists
ps -ef --forest | grep fp-rte                   # fast-path process in the tree
Usual “not up” causes: a port on an unsupported (non-Intel) NIC, too few cores for the requested mask, too few huge pages (minimum 1024–2048) or too few NICs (2, or 3 with a redundancy link on its own private network).
Start-up order and sync chain. The kernel stays the master copy: Linux state → cache manager → fpsync → fast-path tables. The fast path is started only after the config manager has applied the network configuration; a later fast-path config change prompts for a reboot rather than being applied live. A known side effect: the network manager applied IPs/routes twice because swapping the NIC to the DPDK driver re-creates the netdevs.
Port selection granularity. FP_PORTS accepts PCI ids or interface names with “-” exclusions, but it is turned into a PCI whitelist at start – you cannot put only some ports of one PCI device into the fast path.

2. CUPS user-plane CLI. With the control/user-plane split the fast path holds PFCP sessions; dump them with pwfp-cli:
CommandShowsUse it for
UPLANE-SESS-DUMP <pfcp-id> (0 = all)Per session: PDRs (source interface, precedence, local F-TEID, rx packets, AMBR drops, rule drops, SDF filters), FARs (apply action, destination, outer header), QERs (gate status, MBR/GBR), BARs (buffered packet count)Packets reach the right PDR? Dropped by AMBR or by an SDF rule? Downlink buffered during paging?
UPLANE-DEBUG-STATS-DUMPSession create/modify/delete received vs acknowledged, plus duplicate (dup_*) and missing-object (no_*) countersControl-plane/fast-path disagreement, lost or repeated PFCP messages
UPLANE-PEER-STATS-DUMPPer GTP-U peer Rx/Tx/DropWhich eNB/SGW peer the loss is on
fpdebug dump-sad / dump-sad6Fast-path IPsec SA tableCompare with the kernel SA table (ip xfrm state) after a rekey
3. Datapath sanity list (developer smoke set for a GTP-U gateway, still a good regression checklist): default-bearer data UL and DL; GTP-U echo both ways; end-marker on handover; error indication when no bearer exists (this one failed first time round); S1 handover with one and with several E-RABs, and a partially successful one; E-RAB setup, modify (including unsuccessful modify) and release initiated by MME or by eNB; dedicated-bearer data; S1 recovery after MME/eNB restart and S1-U recovery after an SGW restart during data; “clear subscriber all”; data loss across an HA switchover.

4. Throughput method and reference numbers. Drive access-side and core-side generators against the gateway with the same tunnel and peer counts on all three machines so TEIDs line up. Watch per-interface rates (vnstat -l), perf top, fp-cpu-usage and the generators' own CPU – otherwise you measure the generator. Historic figures with one fast-path core serving two 10G ports: ~5 Gbit/s per direction at 2 peers (core ~59 %), ~8.5 Gbit/s at 4–5 peers (81–83 %); with IPsec only 1.5–3.7 Gbit/s with the crypto core at 100 % (CPU-bound); 512-byte packets lower everything further. See UNIPERF01 for the packet generator.

5. Design lessons. RSS hashing spreads flows unevenly and caps a single fat flow at one core (needs pipelining); per-meter locks hurt throughput (anchor each meter to a core); hugepages were split evenly across NUMA nodes regardless of where the NIC sits; IKE without ESN/large anti-replay windows rekeys more often.

6. More virtualisation traps. VMware SR-IOV bond: two VFs passed into a VM get different auto-generated MACs and the bond fails – power off, set both pciPassthru* MAC entries to the same value in the VM's advanced configuration, power on, verify with ip link. Plain KVM VLAN trunk: a macvtap on the host NIC drops tagged frames unless the NIC is promiscuous – put the physical NIC in a Linux bridge, give the guest a bridge-type interface, and create the VLAN sub-interfaces inside the guest (Open vSwitch is the alternative).
7. FPGA SmartNIC (N3000 class). DPDK first does a normal PCIe scan (device bound to vfio-pci), then an AFU scan on the FPGA bus that matches the accelerator function by UUID to its driver. Line-side port statistics come from the FPGA PMD's port representors, host-side statistics from the XL710 (i40e) PMD – check both when counting drops.
NBFAPI01 NB-IoT MAC↔PHY over SCF FAPI — per-channel contracts, why repetitions need one identical PDU per subframe, and where the code departs from SCF 082 Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
NB-IoT reaches deep indoor coverage by repetition: the same block is sent tens or hundreds of times and the receiver adds the copies together (LF34). Between the MAC scheduler and the PHY sits the SCF FAPI interface. The natural question is: who loops the repetitions? Read the procedure diagrams of SCF 082 and you would say "the PHY — the MAC sends one request with R = 128". Read the field tables and you find per-subframe indices that only make sense if the MAC sends a PDU every subframe.

The PW PHY follows the per-subframe model: the MAC must send the same PDU in every subframe of the repetition window, byte-identical except for a position counter. A MAC that sends the PDU only on the first subframe gets R = 1 on the air and loses all the combining gain — with no error anywhere. This card collects the per-channel contracts (NPBCH, NPDCCH, NPDSCH, NPUSCH, NPRACH) and the places where the code departs from SCF 082. Related: LF34 (air interface), CM34 (FAPI P5/P7 in general), NBSIM01 and NBCI01 (benches that exercise this flow), PHYCI01 (test vectors).
Figure — who loops the repetitions?
An R = 8 grant needs eight PDUs from the MAC. NPBCH is the only channel the PHY repeats on its own.
Level 1 — PDU types and the per-subframe rule
Spec: SCF 082.09.05 (FAPI/nFAPI, 2017) on 3GPP Rel-13. NB-IoT reuses the LTE CONFIG / DL_CONFIG / UL_CONFIG containers with its own PDU types.
PDU typeChannelDirectionKey points
9NBCH (NPBCH)DLMIB-NB, 34 bits in 5 bytes
10NPDCCHDLDCI N0/N1/N2, aggregation 1 or 2 NCCE
11NDLSCH (NPDSCH)DLunicast, SIB1-NB, BCCH; payload inline
16NULSCH (NPUSCH)ULdata (format 1) and HARQ-ACK (format 2)
17NRACH (NPRACH)ULper-TTI PDU is ignored; NPRACH is configured at init
PHY→MAC indications: NB_HARQ, NRACH, RX (data) and CRC.

The core rule: the PHY does not loop repetitions (NPBCH excepted). For an N_SF × N_Rep grant the MAC sends N_SF × N_Rep PDUs; payload and grant fields stay byte-identical; only the position counter walks. A second spec rule: DL_CONFIG.request and UL_CONFIG.request must be sent every subframe, even when empty, because they carry SFN/SF and trigger the subframe (this PHY is lenient about missing ones, but the spec is not).
ChannelContract in one line
NPBCHCRC16 → TBCC → rate-match to 1600 bits = 800 QPSK; 8 slices of 100 symbols, each on 8 consecutive frames = 64 frames = 640 ms. Payload identical within the block.
NPDCCH1 NCCE = 160 bits (1 Tx) or 152 (2 Tx); CRC16 XOR RNTI; c_init re-seeded every 4 NPDCCH subframes; up to 2 DCIs per TTI share the 2-NCCE region.
NPDSCHCRC24A + TBCC; repetitionIndex walks 0…N−1; npdschType 4 unicast / 5 SIB1-NB / 6 BCCH.
NPUSCHsubcarrier_indication picks tones; I_RU → number of RUs; RV alternates {0,2}; sf_idx = 1 opens the UE context, decode on the last SF; nothing in the 40 ms UL gap.
NPRACHconfig-driven: periodicity, start time, subcarriers, repetitions set at init; only one CE level consumed today.
Level 2 — per-channel contracts the MAC must honour
NPBCH (pdu_type 9). The 34-bit MIB-NB travels in 5 bytes. The two hyper-SFN LSBs must be encoded inside the payload (bits 7:6 of byte 4); the separate hyper_sfn_2_lsbs field is ignored by this PHY. The PHY re-encodes from scratch on every SF#0 (no cache), so the MAC must present the same 5 bytes for all 64 frames of the 640 ms block and change them only at the block boundary. Each frame also gets a frame-dependent phase overlay on its 100-symbol slice.
MIB-NB (34 b) + CRC16 -> TBCC -> rate-match E = 1600 b -> scramble (c_init = cell id) -> 800 QPSK -> precode
slice = ((nf % 64) >> 3) * 100 symbols   # slice 0 on frames 0-7, slice 1 on 8-15, ... slice 7 on 56-63
NPDCCH (pdu_type 10). Aggregation level 1 or 2 NCCE; CRC16 masked with the RNTI. The scrambler seed is
c_init = (initialSubframe << 9) + cellID       # initialSubframe = first SF of the current 4-SF block
so the four subframes of a block are byte-identical (soft-combinable); for R ≥ 8 initialSubframe steps once per block. A bug report claiming this was wrong was closed as spec-compliant (TS 36.211 §6.8.2 / §10.2.5.2). The PHY does not loop NPDCCH repetitions either.

NPDSCH (pdu_type 11). Worked example: N_SF = 8, N_Rep = 2 → 16 PDUs. repetitionIndex counts 0…15; a block counter and the scrambler anchor (initial frame/subframe) step at block boundaries. Unicast with N_Rep > 4: each N_SF slice is held for 4 SFs, then the 4 × N_SF block repeats N_Rep/4 times. SIB1-NB / BCCH: the N_SF slices play in sequence, then the block repeats. The PHY does not range-check the computed slice index — a wrong counter gives garbage, not an error.

NPUSCH (pdu_type 16).
FieldValues
subcarrier_indication (15 kHz)0–11 single-tone, 12–15 three-tone, 16–17 six-tone, 18 twelve-tone
subcarrier_indication (3.75 kHz)0–47, single-tone only
I_RU0–5 → 1–6 RUs, 6 → 8 RUs, 7 → 10 RUs
slots per RU16 / 8 / 4 / 2 for 1 / 3 / 6 / 12 tones
RValternates {0, 2}
sf_idx1 opens the UE context; later PDUs look it up by RNTI; decode runs on the last SF
format 2 (HARQ-ACK)single tone, 4 slots; 4 dedicated subcarriers at 15 kHz
No NULSCH PDU is sent inside the 40 ms UL gap.

NPRACH. Driven by cell CONFIG, not by grants: periodicity 40–2560 ms, start time 2^(i+3) SF, 12/24/36/48 subcarriers, 1–128 repetitions. Format 0: CP 66.7 µs, preamble ~5.6 ms; format 1: CP 266.7 µs, preamble ~6.4 ms; a UL gap is inserted above 64 repetitions. The per-TTI NRACH PDU is never read. Known gap: only one CE level is consumed, while a real cell needs up to three.
Level 3 — spec deviations, adapter design and real findings
Where the code departs from SCF 082:
TopicSpecCode
Repetition modelprocedure diagrams: single PDU; field tables: per-SF (SFidx up to ~10240, per-SF cinit/RV)per-subframe; ~15 code-only fields (initialSubframe, repetitionIndex, npdschType…) so the MAC pre-computes state the spec expects the PHY to derive
NPBCH cadenceonce per 640 msa PDU every SF#0 (10 ms)
DL payloadTX.request, correlated by pdu_indexpayload inline in the PDU; TX.request unsupported, pdu_index unused
UL grantsHI_DCI0.requestunsupported; grants go via NULSCH
NMM messages8 definednone used
NULSCH SFidx at 3.75 kHzrange scaled upscaled down — suspected bug
Wire formatSCF TLVMessagePack over WLS shared memory, produced by a reflection library
The wire-format gotcha: because serialisation is reflection-driven, a struct with no reflection entry simply cannot be sent — adding a field to a C struct is not enough. Init uses only the RF, scheduling, UL reference-signal and NB-IoT config groups; START/STOP carry no payload.

Adapter design for a standards-style MAC. If the MAC sends one PDU per grant, an adapter must expand it: an "expander / position calculator" generates the per-SF PDUs. Every PHY field gets one of five dispositions: DIRECT (copy), MAP (translate a value), JOIN (fuse the TX.request TB or the HI_DCI0 DCI into the PDU), COMPUTED (position counters, scrambler anchors) or CONSTANT. Trap found in design: a lone TTI indication without the matching subframe indication re-emits a stale SFN/SF, so the adapter's clock stalls.

An earlier code review of third-party NB-IoT PHY code found: a 1024-slot UL buffer limit that caps single-tone (16-slot RU) repetitions at 64 instead of 128 (16 × 64 = 1024); no NPRACH format 2; and test vectors with overlapping NPUSCH allocations for two RNTIs. Lesson: check limits against the spec maxima, not against what the test vectors happen to use.

Debug checklist when NB-IoT UEs fail only in deep coverage: count the PDUs per grant in the FAPI log (should be N_SF × N_Rep); confirm payload bytes are identical across the window; confirm the counters walk and the scrambler anchor steps only at block boundaries; confirm the NPBCH payload changes only every 640 ms.
Interview Q&A
Q: Your MAC sends an NPDSCH grant with N_Rep = 32 once. Cell-centre UEs work; deep-coverage UEs fail. Why?
A: This PHY does not loop repetitions. One PDU means one transmission, so the UE gets no combining gain. The MAC must send N_SF × N_Rep identical PDUs with walking repetitionIndex.
Q: Why are NPDCCH repetitions within a 4-subframe block byte-identical?
A: The scrambler c_init = (initialSubframe << 9) + cellID is anchored to the block start and only re-seeded every 4 NPDCCH subframes (TS 36.211 §10.2.5.2), and the coded bits depend only on payload and RNTI.
Q: How is NPBCH spread over 640 ms?
A: MIB-NB + CRC16 is TBCC-coded and rate-matched to 1600 bits = 800 QPSK symbols, cut into 8 slices of 100 symbols; each slice is sent in SF#0 of 8 consecutive frames, so 64 frames = 640 ms.
Q: Why must DL_CONFIG.request be sent in a subframe where nothing is scheduled?
A: SCF FAPI requires one per subframe: it carries SFN/SF and triggers subframe processing. An empty PDU list is allowed; an absent message is not.
Q: Name two places where this FAPI implementation deviates from SCF 082.
A: Per-subframe PDUs with code-only position fields instead of a single PDU; NPBCH every 10 ms; DL payload inline (no TX.request); HI_DCI0 unsupported; MessagePack instead of TLV on the wire.
vBBU internals — REX/NATS, user plane, logs, PM counters, CI triage, PKI
REXMSG01 REX / NATS JetStream IPC inside the vBBU - stream and consumer limits, live decoding, health criteria, three "published but the cell stayed DOWN" root causes and a pod-kill test recipe Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
REX is the message bus inside a containerised vBBU. Where the old system had every task send UDP datagrams straight to another task ("UniTask" IPC), REX puts a NATS server with JetStream (the rex pod) in the middle. Messages between pods are now stored in a stream and handed to a named, durable consumer that must acknowledge them; messages inside one pod use plain, fire-and-forget NATS subjects.

That buys persistence and observability, but it also adds new ways to fail: a consumer that exists but is bound to a dead connection, a stream purge that throws away the very message the cell was waiting for, consumers that leak until something runs out of memory. In every one of these the sender's logs say "published OK" while the receiver never sees the message.

REXDBG01 already gives the "is the cell really up?" triage tree, the 4G vs 5G cell-up evidence, consumer counting and the false positives - this card does not repeat it. This card is the transport's own toolbox: its building blocks and limits, how to decode live traffic, the health numbers a validation must show, three real root causes, and a reusable pod-kill resilience recipe. The RU activation chain (SET_FREQ → L1_DONE → RRH_READY) is in CM16 and RRHMGR01.
Figure - REX building blocks and where they can break (own drawing)
Core tasks reach the broker through the send hook; rrhmgr and the 5G stack-manager are native clients. Cross-pod traffic is stored in IPC_XPOD and handed to durable consumers; liveness traffic never depends on the broker.
Level 1 - building blocks, subjects, limits and consumer naming
REX is the whole transport, not just the pod. Five pieces: the BrokerBridge / ipcbridge static library compiled into every BBU binary (hooks UniTaskSend(), so legacy tasks need 1-2 lines of change or none); librex-messaging (C++ NATS adapter on the nats.c client library, topic transport, IPC codec); the rex pod (nats-server 2.10 with JetStream); a ReverseProxy that turns raw UDP sendto() from processes that bypass the hook (legacy standalone rrhmgr) into NATS publishes - only the destination prefix is redirected to loopback, zero code change; and the XpodConsumerWatchdog. Native clients (rrhmgr BrokerTransport, 5G stack-manager RexTransport, faultmgr SDK) call the library directly. The transport is chosen per pod at run time with IPC_TRANSPORT=broker (code default unitask), no rebuild.

TrafficSubjectDelivery
Inside one pod<pod>.<facility> e.g. bbu.configmgrcore NATS, fire-and-forget; the receiving bridge hands it to the task over local UDP
Between podsxpod.<facility> e.g. xpod.rrhmgr, xpod.oammgrJetStream stream IPC_XPOD, publish-with-ack, durable consumer per pod and facility; receivers keep only messages whose destination-IP header is local
Alarms<pod>.faultmgr / xpod.faultmgralarm publish, alarm-list request/response, API_UP handshake
Default cross-pod facilities (IPC_XPOD_FACILITIES): rrhmgr, platmgr, oammgr, configmgr, faultmgr. Routing headers travel as NATS headers (x_msg_id, x_src_task_id, x_dest_task_id, x_dest_ip, x_payload_len, x_pod_generation); the original UniTask header stays inside the payload. Typical message families: PLATMGR_* (platform-info request roughly every 2 s), SYSMGR_TASK_READY, RRHMGR_* (SET_FREQ_REQ, SET_TX_POWER_REQ, L1_DONE, RRH_READY). On 5G the DU stack-manager also sends RRHMGR_HEARTBEAT to rrhmgr every 5 s.

Stream and consumer limits - these changed between releases, so always read them from nats stream info IPC_XPOD rather than trusting a document:
ItemCurrent develop designEarlier design (first 2026 builds)
Stream IPC_XPODfile storage, 15,000 msgs, 256 MB, max age 24 h, 500 msgs per subject, 8 MB per message, discard-oldsame counts and sizes, but max age 5 min and no per-subject cap
Where the store liveshostPath on the server - survives a rex pod restart; Deployment strategy Recreate so two servers never share the storeemptyDir - survives a NATS process restart, lost when the pod is recreated
Stream lifecyclecreated by the first client that needs it; never deleted by a client; timed-out checks retried with backoff (30 s doubling to 240 s); only a real config mismatch is updated in placesame create-on-demand
ServerJetStream memory store 32 MB, file store 1 GiB, max payload 8 MB, 1,024 connections, write deadline 10 ssame
Consumer settingsAckExplicit, DeliverPolicy New, MaxDeliver 3, MaxAckPending 1,000, InactiveThreshold 300 sno InactiveThreshold (see RCA 3)
Consumer ownerDurable name patternWhy
ipcbridge owner process (Core tasks)<pod>_<gen8>_xpod_<facility> e.g. vnode01_2800bff3_xpod_oammgrgen8 = first 8 chars of the pod UID, so a recreated pod gets fresh consumers (generation fencing)
faultmgr daemon<pod>_<gen8>_xpod_faultmgrsame fencing
rrhmgr (native)<pod>_xpod_rrhmgr e.g. bbu_xpod_rrhmgrstable name
stack-manager (native)<pod>_xpod_oammgr (first builds used one constant name for every pod)per-pod name isolates DUs
Two properties that matter in every RCA: (1) DeliverNew means a consumer that is rebuilt does not replay what was published while it was missing - lost interest equals lost messages unless the sender retries. (2) Heartbeats never use the broker (UniTask heartbeat message ids 1-6 go straight over UDP), so a dead broker does not make tasks look dead - and a healthy heartbeat proves nothing about IPC.

The UDP fallback trap. When a publish fails the bridge falls back to legacy UDP with a syslog warning. Cross-pod, that UDP path may not even be routable. Worse, in a staged rollout where the sender is on the broker but the receiver is still on UniTask, the publish succeeds (the stream accepts it), nobody consumes it, and the fallback never fires: silent loss. Repeated "falling back" lines mean a broker problem is being masked, not handled.
Level 2 - decoding, monitoring, pass criteria and the pod-kill recipe
1. Decode what actually crossed the broker. The rex pod's nats container ships nats-ipc-decode, which turns raw IPC messages into names (task ids → facilities, msg ids → e.g. RRHMGR_SET_FREQ_REQ) and decodes the binary payload fields.
REX=$(kubectl -n pw get pods -o name | grep rex)
kubectl -n pw exec $REX -c nats -- nats-ipc-decode --stream IPC_XPOD --last 20      # last 20 stored cross-pod msgs
kubectl -n pw exec $REX -c nats -- nats-ipc-decode --stream IPC_XPOD --from N --to M -v
kubectl -n pw exec $REX -c nats -- nats-ipc-decode --subs     # connections + subscriptions
kubectl -n pw exec $REX -c nats -- nats-ipc-decode --stats    # server counters, slow consumers

# "what did this consumer last see?"  -> read Last Delivered stream seq, decode the 10-15 before it
kubectl -n pw exec $REX -c nats -- nats consumer info IPC_XPOD bbu_xpod_rrhmgr

# LIVE trace: subscribe in the pod, decode on the BBU host (copy the script out once)
kubectl -n pw exec $REX -c nats -- cat /usr/local/bin/nats-ipc-decode > /tmp/nats-ipc-decode
kubectl -n pw exec $REX -c nats -- nats sub ">" | python3 /tmp/nats-ipc-decode       # or "bbu.>", "*.rrhmgr.>", "xpod.>"
Do not run nats sub ">" | nats-ipc-decode entirely inside the rex pod: both processes then live in the pod's memory cgroup and a busy system can OOM-kill the broker you are debugging.

2. Monitoring is HTTP on 8222, not 4222. 4222 speaks the NATS client protocol and returns nothing useful to curl. The 8222 endpoints are reachable only from inside the cluster:
EndpointAnswers
/healthzis the server up (liveness)
/varzuptime, memory, in/out message counts, slow consumers
/connz?subs=1every client connection and its subscriptions - proves which process subscribed to which subject
/jsz?streams=true&consumers=truestream state plus every durable: delivered, ack floor, pending, redelivered
/subsz, /routezsubscription routing summary; cluster routes (none on a single node)
Need a raw protocol trace? Patch the rex ConfigMap's nats.conf to add trace: true, delete the rex pod so it restarts with it, read its logs - then remove it again; the volume is very high.

3. Per-client health lines. rrhmgr (native client, runs as a service inside the BBU pod, logs to the BBU syslog) prints every 10 s:
rrhmgr: INF BrokerTransport: health: connected=1 pub=101 pubFail=0 in=113 qHW=28 drop=0
#  connected  1 = NATS connection alive          pubFail  publishes rejected
#  qHW        inbound queue high-water (bounded at 1,024, oldest dropped)   drop  overflow drops
The 5G stack-manager proves its native transport at start-up with lines like RexTransport created (pod=...), JetStream subscribed to 'xpod.oammgr' (durable=...) and a connected line.
