It activates only when Helm sets IPC_POD_NAME plus the MSG_BROKER_* variables; if they are missing it logs one warning and stays inert. A STACKMGR_NATIVE_REX Helm value exists but the binary does not read it - never quote that flag as evidence the transport is on.

4. Pass criteria for a REX validation run (use these numbers in the test report, on top of the REXDBG01 evidence list):
CheckPass
every durable consumerpending 0, ack-pending 0, redelivered 0; ack floor tracking delivered seq
serverslow consumers 0; connection and subscription counts constant over the soak
consumer listone per pod x facility, no duplicates, no missing ones, count does not grow across restarts
streammessage count flat over 10+ minutes (no accumulation), bytes well below the limit
targeted deliveryconsumer seq advances by exactly the messages sent (e.g. +2 for L1_DONE + RRH_READY)
multi-DUzero "not for us" drops on the DUs that were not addressed
sender sideno "publish failed", "not connected" or "falling back" lines after cell-up
5. Metrics for dashboards. An optional prometheus-nats-exporter sidecar (port 7777, scraped about every 60 s, which makes rex 2/2 instead of 1/1) exposes connections, in/out messages, slow consumers, memory, JetStream streams/consumers/storage and per-connection pending bytes. A healthy BBU over a 20-minute soak: connections and subscriptions constant, 0 slow consumers, 1 stream, constant consumer count, JetStream storage flat. Where these metrics travel next is in VBBUSTAT01.

6. Resilience recipe (reusable for any broker-based IPC).
StepWhat to doPass
Pod restartsrestart each pod type 5 times: CU, each DU, BBU, rex = 25 cyclesper cycle: that pod's consumers are back; no purge on the oammgr subject; L1_DONE + RRH_READY delivered; rrhReadySent=1 per RRH; cell up
rex deletekubectl delete pod on rexstream restored, all consumers and connections back
rex unavailablescale rex to 0, wait about 95 s, scale backevery client reconnects, zero cascade restarts of other pods
rex OOMforce an OOM kill (exit 137)Running again in about 90 s, consumer count and stream count unchanged, zero redelivery
vnode recreate loopN delete/recreate cycles of a 4G vnode with BBU and rrhmgr left runningcell up after every cycle; consumer count stable
Log auditgrep warnings/errors of the transport after each cycleclean - a later campaign found a pod-name DNS bug only this way
Level 3 - three root causes, why skip-purge is safe, and known limits
RCA 1 - purge after a DU restart kept the cell DOWN (LTE4G-25889). After one DU pod restarted, its bridge discovered the late facility xpod.oammgr only on every 30th watchdog cycle, so for about 330 s there was no consumer. Meanwhile the RU side had already published L1_DONE and RRH_READY into the stream. When discovery finally ran it used the start-up path, which first purges the subject as generation fencing - deleting exactly those valid messages. OAM never saw them, the RRH was not recovered, the cell stayed DOWN. Fix: late discovery on every cycle (about 10 s) and skipPurge=true on late-discovery, broker-recovery and ownership-failover paths; only a true first boot still purges. After the fix the consumer appeared about 40 s after the restart and cells recovered 3/3, then 25/25 in the full campaign.
Why skipping the purge is safe: DeliverNew ignores messages that existed before the consumer; per-pod durable names isolate deployments; the first-boot purge still clears previous deployments; application ids (transaction id, RRH id, carrier id) correlate responses. The one dangerous stale class is a reboot request (RrhRebootReq) - rare, manual, and also skipped by DeliverNew.

RCA 2 - ownership failover with no subscriptions (LTE4G-24943). Exactly one process per pod owns the cross-pod consumers, decided by an exclusive flock on /var/run/pw/xpod-owner.<pod>.lock on a hostPath. When a 4G vnode pod was deleted while BBU and rrhmgr kept running, the old and new generations overlapped: the new processes started as NON_OWNER because the dying pod still held the lock. When the watchdog later won the lock, the promoted process had an empty local registry and the failover path simply skipped rebuilding. Result: consumers showing "No interest" (delivery bound to a dead connection), pending stuck at the 500-per-subject cap, platmgr retrying the inventory request 12,000+ times over 9.5 hours, cell DOWN forever while Kubernetes showed the pod Running. Hit rate 25-60 % depending on load. Workaround: restart rrhmgr. Fix: rebuild subscriptions immediately on failover with skipPurge=true, so the inventory responses queued during the gap are delivered (6 pending went to 0 within a second in one validation cycle). Validated with 20 delete/recreate cycles, 8 of which took the failover path, 0 cell-DOWN.

RCA 3 - orphan consumers ran the BBU out of memory (CLD-29332). The old nats.c 3.2.0 could not set InactiveThreshold, and generation-fenced names change on every restart, so each restart abandoned 12-17 consumers that never expired - 700+ on nightly setups. The metrics exporter OOMed iterating them, and BBU start-up OOMed because about 15 forked processes each enumerated full consumer configs (about 2.3 MB per call at 773 consumers) against a 1 GiB container limit. Fix: nats.c 3.9.2, InactiveThreshold 300 s on every new consumer, a one-time migration that lists names only (about 150 KB, 93 % less) and patches legacy _xpod_ consumers, one migrator per process. Proof: 774 consumers dropped to 14 within 5 minutes; active consumers untouched.

LessonWhat it means for a tester
publish success is not delivery successsender logs (and rrhmgr health lines) can look perfect while the receiver is dead; judge by nats consumer info: ack floor moving, pending 0, push-bound
"Running" is not "recovered"every RCA above left pods Running and Ready; the cell state and consumer state are the evidence
restart paths differpod delete, pod recreate with overlap, DU restart, broker outage and first boot take different code paths (purge vs no purge, owner vs non-owner) - test each
counts must be flatconsumer count and stream message count that grow across restarts are a leak even when nothing has failed yet
Known limits to keep in mind: single NATS node (no HA); no TLS inside the cluster; after a NATS outage a consumer can be missing for up to one watchdog period (10 s) plus retry backoff; the 5G stack-manager and the faultmgr daemon subscribe only once, so after a stream loss their consumers stay missing until the pod restarts; tasks do not yet re-publish state after a peer rebuilds a consumer. With DeliverNew, anything published in those gaps is gone unless the sender retries (OAM retries SET_FREQ_REQ about every 10 s, which is why 4G usually self-heals and why one-shot 5G messages are the ones to watch).
Interview Q&A
Q: Why can a sender log "publish OK" while the receiver never gets the message?
A: A JetStream publish is acknowledged when the stream stores it, not when a consumer receives it. If the durable consumer is missing, bound to a dead connection ("No interest"), or the receiver is still on legacy UDP, the message just sits in the stream (or is purged/aged out). Check the consumer: ack floor moving, pending 0, push-bound.
Q: What does DeliverPolicy New mean for recovery testing?
A: A consumer that is (re)created only gets messages published after it exists. Anything sent while it was missing is not replayed, so recovery depends on the sender retrying. That is why gaps of 10-330 s matter and why one-shot messages are the risky ones.
Q: Why did skipping the stream purge fix a cell-DOWN after a DU restart, and why is it safe?
A: The late-discovery path purged the oammgr subject and deleted valid L1_DONE/RRH_READY messages. Skipping the purge on recovery paths keeps them. It is safe because DeliverNew ignores pre-existing messages for new consumers, durable names are per pod, the first-boot purge still clears old deployments, and responses carry correlation ids.
Q: How do you watch live REX traffic without hurting the broker?
A: Subscribe inside the rex pod (nats sub with a subject filter) but pipe into nats-ipc-decode running on the BBU host, not inside the pod, so the decoder does not consume the rex pod memory cgroup. For history use the stream mode with --last or a sequence range ending at the consumer's last delivered seq.
Q: How would you prove a broker-based IPC survives pod failures?
A: Restart each pod type five times (25 cycles) and check per cycle that its consumers return, nothing was purged, the activation messages were delivered and the cell came up; then delete, scale to zero and OOM-kill the broker and require all connections and consumers back in about 90 s with zero redelivery and zero cascade restarts; finally confirm consumer and message counts stay flat.
NETCDBG01 Backhaul user-plane triage in the vBBU netcon pod (VPP) - direction-specific traces, GTP counters, the evidence bundle and four "DL is zero" root causes Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
On a vBBU every user-plane packet between the core network and the eNB/gNB stack passes through one pod: netcon. Inside it runs VPP (the vector packet processor) which terminates the backhaul interface with DPDK, does IPsec and, when S1-U aggregation is on, translates GTP-U between the single external address and each vNode's internal address (see IPAGG01 and IPSEC01). When a tester reports "DL throughput is zero" the fault can be in the core network, the transport, netcon itself or the stack behind it. This card is the triage kit: which VPP trace node to arm for which direction, which counters tell you "packets arrived but nobody read them", what to bundle with every bug, and four real "DL is zero" root causes that each pointed somewhere different.
Where to look in netcon for each direction
Arm the trace on the node where traffic enters VPP for the direction under test; counters on the far side tell you whether the receiver is draining.
Level 1 - Core facts: one pod, one graph, direction-specific trace nodes

netcon is the vBBU's backhaul gateway pod (container netcon-k8s). Its VPP graph has an input node per traffic source, and a VPP trace only records packets entering the node you armed - so arming the wrong node gives an empty trace that proves nothing.

DirectionTrace nodeCommandWhat it sees
Uplink (UE/stack → core)session-queuevppctl trace add session-queue 10000Packets the stack handed to VPP over its session layer
Downlink (core → UE/stack)dpdk-inputvppctl trace add dpdk-input 10000Packets arriving from the backhaul NIC
Both directionssession-queue + dpdk-input + af-packet-inputarm all three, then show trace max 30000af-packet-input covers host-interface paths
IPsec inner trafficloop10000 (pcap)pcap trace rx tx ... intfc loop10000 ... max-bytes-per-pkt 9000Decrypted side of the tunnel
  • Always vppctl clear trace before arming and after dumping; a node name typo (e.g. dpdk-input33) is rejected with "expected NODE COUNT".
  • pcap inside VPP: vppctl pcap trace rx tx max 99999 intfc any file /tmp/vppcapture.pcap, reproduce, vppctl pcap trace off, then copy into the pod share so it is visible from the host.
  • pcap in vNode/CU/DU pods: ordinary tcpdump -i any -s 0 -w /tmp/<pod>.pcap, then copy to /var/log/ - it appears on the host under /var/log/pw-share/pods/stack/<pod>.
  • Long captures: prefer switch port-mirroring. Long continuous captures inside netcon raise its CPU and memory use and can slow or crash it - you would be debugging your own capture.
Level 2 - How to test and measure: the triage sequence

Collect first, then reason. Start the traces and pcaps, reproduce once, then grab the snapshot set. Along with the bundle record the issue time, all SW versions (SMO and BBU) and a one-line symptom.

# enter netcon (namespace pw, container netcon-k8s)
kubectl -n pw exec -it pod/$(kubectl -n pw get pods | grep netcon | awk '{print $1}') -c netcon-k8s -- bash

# downlink problem
vppctl clear trace
vppctl trace add dpdk-input 10000
#   ... reproduce ...
vppctl show trace max 10000 > /opt/pw-share/pods/netcon/dltrace.txt
vppctl clear trace

# snapshot set for every case
for c in error runtime hardware-interfaces log; do
  vppctl show $c > /opt/pw-share/pods/netcon/show-$c.txt; done
cd /opt/pw-share/pods/netcon && tar czf netcon_bundle_$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S).tgz *.txt *.pcap
QuestionCommandHow to read it
IPsec tunnel up?swanctl --list-sas (newer builds, strongSwan under the netcon IPsec tree) or ipsec stroke statusall | grep ESTABLISHED (older)left = local endpoint, right = peer; IDs in brackets. Detailed tunnel debug is in IPSEC01.
Live ratevppctl monitor interface <if>rx/tx packets-per-second and bits-per-second, refreshed live - quick check that DL is entering at all
S1-U aggregation statevppctl s1u-aggregation statusDisabled means the old per-vNode path is in use (IPAGG01)
GTP per peervppctl show gtp counters [vnode|core], clear gtp counters [vnode|core]Per internal vNode address and per core peer: echo req/rsp TX/RX, error indications TX/RX, packets and bytes TX/RX
Drops by reasonvppctl show node counters / show errorLook for "Fifo full" on udp4-input
Socketsvppctl show session verboseLISTEN entries on GTP-U ports 2152/2153 per vNode and OPENED sessions to the core peer

Reading GTP counters: clear them, run traffic for a minute, read again. A vNode row whose PKTS RX grows while PKTS TX stays 0 means DL reaches the vNode but nothing comes back up (or the stack is not sending). A core row with ECHO REQ RX climbing but no ECHO RSP TX points to the path-management side. Error indications on either side usually mean a TEID mismatch after a bearer was torn down.

For netcon deployment failures (pod never comes up) the evidence lives on both sides: SMO logs for UCB, plugin, deployment manager, orchestrator, FOCOM manager, config manager and ACS; on the BBU helm -n pw history pw, helm -n pw get manifest pw and kubectl -n pw get cm -o yaml.

Level 3 - Expert detail: four "DL is zero" root causes

Four field cases that all started as "DL throughput is zero" - and each had a different owner:

CaseSymptomRoot causeLesson
1. Buffer fullDL low or zero; show node counters shows a large "Fifo full" count on udp4-inputnetcon received the packets and queued them to the stack socket, but the stack did not drain the FIFOBottleneck is in the stack (vNode/CU), not in VPP or transport
2. Wrong bind addressStack shows DL rate 0 (lteStats → showuerate) while traffic is visible in VPPeNB bound its GTP-U socket to the M-plane address instead of the S1-U aggregation address; the vNode configmap lacked the S1-U address (USER_LOCAL_ADDRESS) because the SMO still ran an older UCB (7.4) than the BBU (7.5)Check the bind line in the vNode messages file and the stack configmap; keep SMO and BBU releases aligned
3. Nothing arrivesNo packets even in a dpdk-input trace; an older known-good build behaves the sameDuplicate IP address in the core network - DL never reached the BBUIf dpdk-input is empty, look outside the BBU (core, routing, ARP)
4. Client crash → VPP panicDL drops, then netcon restarts after about 50 h of a weekend soak; cell availability dipsThe vNode's egtpPdcpRlcUl process crashed (SIG11), the vNode rebooted, its binary-API client stopped heart-beating ("REAPER: binary API client ... not responding"), FIFOs and VPP memory filled and VPP raised a deliberate panicFix was in the stack. Correlate netcon panic time with the REAPER line, the vNode reboot request and the zlog/core timestamps

Decision rule: dpdk-input empty → outside the BBU (case 3). dpdk-input full but stack sees nothing → check sockets/bind address and S1-U aggregation config (case 2). Stack sockets right but Fifo full → the receiver is slow or dead (cases 1 and 4). A netcon crash is often a victim, not the culprit: always check whether a client disappeared just before it.

Comparing builds: before calling a regression, run the previous known-good build on the same setup. In case 3 the "working" build also showed zero, which immediately moved the investigation to the network.

VPP memory growth: enable allocation tracking with memory-trace on main-heap, then show memory main-heap shows total/used/free plus allocation tracebacks. Unknown addresses in those tracebacks can be symbolised by starting VPP with the libvppmem_preload.so library in LD_PRELOAD. Useful when a soak shows netcon RSS creeping up.

Pitfalls: (1) arming dpdk-input for an uplink problem and concluding "no packets"; (2) leaving a pcap running for hours inside netcon; (3) forgetting clear gtp counters, so you read lifetime totals instead of the test window; (4) a show session verbose with LISTEN sockets but no OPENED session to the core peer - the stack never started sending.

Interview Q&A
Q: You see no DL at the UE. Which VPP trace do you arm first and why?
A: dpdk-input, because downlink enters netcon from the backhaul NIC. If that trace is empty the packets never reached the BBU, so the problem is in the core or transport; if it is full, move inward to GTP counters, sockets and stack.
Q: What does a large "Fifo full" count on udp4-input tell you?
A: VPP received and accepted the packets but could not enqueue them to the receiving application's session FIFO because the stack was not draining it. The bottleneck is on the stack side, not in VPP.
Q: netcon restarted with a panic after two days of soak. Is netcon the root cause?
A: Not necessarily. Check for a REAPER "client not responding" message before the panic, then the vNode messages for a reboot request and the GMDT logs for a core. In the real case a stack process crashed, its client stopped heart-beating, VPP memory filled and VPP panicked.
Q: Why should long captures be done with port mirroring rather than inside netcon?
A: A long VPP pcap or trace adds CPU and memory load to the data-plane pod, which can change timing, slow forwarding or even crash it. A switch mirror is passive.
Q: After an upgrade the eNB binds GTP-U to the M-plane address. Where do you look?
A: The vNode messages for the bind call, then the stack configmap for the S1-U local address. If it is missing, check whether the SMO's UCB matches the BBU release; an older UCB does not propagate the S1-U aggregation address.
VBBULOG01 vBBU Runtime Log Levels vs Kernel Trace — three "debug" paths, the UniLog bitmask, the ControlPath gateway and the ConfigMap + NATS fix Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
When a test fails on a vBBU the first instinct is "turn the logs up to DEBUG". On this platform that innocent request hides three different mechanisms that are easy to confuse:
  1. App log level via the management backchannel — the EMS stores the change and the BBU picks it up on its next heartbeat.
  2. App log level via direct HTTP to a small web server inside the BBU pod — immediate, but fire-and-forget.
  3. Linux kernel tracing (ftrace) from the GUI's "Debug" menu — which, despite the name, has nothing to do with application log levels.
Paths 1 and 2 end in the same place: every UniTask process in the pod flips a few bits in its own per-process log filter bitmask. Path 3 shares no code, pod, language or message with them. On top of that the level is lost on restart and only some pods are reachable. This card explains each path, how the bitmask works, why "I raised the level and still see nothing" happens, and the redesign (ConfigMap + NATS + rsyslog guard) chosen to fix it. The platform gateway that fronts the pods (ControlPath) is described here too. Related: REXDBG01 (NATS/REX IPC), CM29 (cloud-native platform), VBBUDMN01 (which daemon does what), VBBUSTAT01 (where logs end up).
Three "debug" controls on a vBBU - two meet at SysMgr, one never does
Own drawing, simplified. Port numbers as used inside the pod; no addresses shown.
Level 1 — the three paths and the bitmask
UniLog levels (8, most to least severe): FATAL, CRITICAL, ERROR, WARN, NOTICE, INFO, DEBUG, TRACE. Default is ERROR. About 54 named modules can be targeted (e.g. HEARTBEAT, IPC, PROCMGR, SYSMGR, CFGMGR, FAULTMGR, OAMMGR, RRC, S1AP, X2AP, PLATMGR, UNIFTPMGR, WEBSERVER, LTEMAC, LTERLC, LTEPDCP, PHYMGR); at most 100 module entries per request.
ItemWhat it isWhy it matters
moduleBitArrayPer-process, thread-local bit array: 9 level rows x 160 module bits (20 bytes per row)Setting module M to level L sets M's bit in every row ≤ L and clears it above L
UNILOG_ALLOW(handle, level, module)Tests a single bit before formatting a log lineCheap enough to sit in hot paths; a disabled DEBUG line costs one bit test
LogConfigMessageIPC message UNI_TASK_FRAMEWORK_MSG_ID_LOG_CONFIG sent to SysMgrSysMgr re-sends it to every child task, which calls setLogModuleFilter()
CWS web serverPort 80, bound to the in-pod debug bridge dbgbr0; handles sysLogLevelSet, alarm and GPS commandsThe only HTTP entry for log levels; answers 200 but nobody confirms the tasks applied it
CpMgr hook serverPort 60080 in every pod; hooks /ping, /version, /v1/rrh, /v1/spectrum/*What ControlPath forwards to; has no sysLogLevelSet hook (404/400)
Path 1 (backchannel): REST POST /pw/v1/deviceconfiguration/params/{deviceId} with systemInformation.Logging.LoggingModuleList = list of {ModuleName, LogLevel}. The SMO stores it in its database queue, the BBU pulls it on the next heartbeat, CfgMgr turns each entry into a LogConfigMessage, SysMgr broadcasts. Pending items: GET /pw/v1/devicebcupdatequeue/{deviceId}. Store-and-forward: survives the BBU being offline, but is never instant.
Finding: the GUI shows "Logging Module List" at the bottom of the vBBU Config Details SYSTEM tab, but renders it as a plain label with no editor — only the REST API can set it.
Path 2 (direct): GET http://<bbu-debug-bridge>:80/sysLogLevelSet?module=SYSMGR&level=DEBUG → SysReqHandler converts names to ids, sets one bit, sends IPC to SysMgr → broadcast. Reply {"result":"Setting Log level of SYSMGR to DEBUG"} — fire-and-forget.
Path 3 (kernel trace): the GUI "Debug" page is Kernel Trace Configuration, a single ON/OFF toggle. It goes through ControlPath to a Python FastAPI app in the MDT pod (POST /v1/kerneltrace, actions traceon / record / traceoff / status, plus events, plugin, process filter, max trace timeout/size), which runs trace-cmd or writes /sys/kernel/tracing (tracing_on, set_event, current_tracer, trace_pipe). Node-wide kernel events, not application logs; state lives in the Python process and is lost on pod restart.
Level 2 — using it in the lab and debugging the debug
Raising a level for a lab debug session (lab only; values are examples):
# Path 2 - immediate, from a host that can reach the pod's debug bridge
curl "http://<bbu-debug-bridge>:80/sysLogLevelSet?module=RRC&level=DEBUG"
#   -> {"result":"Setting Log level of RRC to DEBUG"}   (says nothing about whether tasks applied it)

# Path 1 - via the EMS REST API (body only; authentication omitted)
POST /pw/v1/deviceconfiguration/params/{deviceId}
{"systemInformation":{"Logging":{"LoggingModuleList":[{"ModuleName":"RRC","LogLevel":"DEBUG"}]}}}
GET  /pw/v1/devicebcupdatequeue/{deviceId}      # still pending? the heartbeat has not polled yet

# Proof it worked: a DEBUG line from the target module appears in the pod syslog
kubectl -n pw exec <bbu-pod> -c <container> -- tail -n 200 /var/log/messages | grep -i rrc
"I raised it and see nothing" — checklist
QuestionWhy it bites
Which path did you use?The GUI "Debug" toggle starts kernel tracing; it never changes app log levels
Has the heartbeat polled yet?Path 1 waits in the SMO queue until the next heartbeat; check the backchannel queue
Did the pod or process restart since?Runtime levels are held only in memory and reset to ERROR on restart
Does the module live in a reachable pod?Only the BBU GPP and BBP pods were reachable through CfgMgr; PHY, vNode, DU, CU, MDT were not
Is the module name exact?Names are mapped by uni_logmodule_gettype() and must match the module list exactly (SYSMGR, not SysMgr-1); the call itself never confirms the tasks applied it
Is the syslog side filtering?The rsyslog sidecar has its own severity filter; the app bitmask and rsyslog both have to pass the line
Before the redesign, engineers SSH'd into each pod, edited the syslog configuration and restarted — which itself wiped the level and often the failure they were chasing.
Level 3 — ControlPath, the security gap and the redesign
The ControlPath gateway. The platform exposes one management entry: an nginx pod ("ControlPath") with TLS on NodePort 30443 (basic auth, API key or optional client certificate) and mandatory-client-certificate mTLS on 31443 with optional CRL checking. A dynamic route /v1/route/<service>/<path> is proxied to <service>-cp on port 60080, i.e. to that pod's CpMgr hook server. So /v1/route/bbu/sysLogLevelSet lands on CpMgr, not on the CWS server, and returns 404/400. Security note: the external hop is protected, but inside the namespace pod-to-pod calls were plain HTTP with no NetworkPolicy, so any pod could call any other pod's 60080 hooks — the cluster relied on implicit trust.

The redesign ("Option E", ~270 lines, no new pods). Requirements: any module in any pod, survive restart, effect in < 2 s, usable with kubectl when the SMO is down, zero log loss and zero app restart.
PieceRole
ControlPath REST POST/GET /v1/logconfig/{pod}Validates and audits; RBAC limited to 17 named ConfigMaps
ConfigMap log-config-<pod>Durable state with a versioned annotation; read by SysMgr at start-up so the level survives a restart
NATS subject log.config.<pod>Immediate delivery (log.config.* for bulk; .ack and .status replies) to the pod's SysMgr, which applies and broadcasts
rsyslog sidecar entry scriptReads the severity from the ConfigMap, builds the filter, validates with rsyslogd -N1, reloads with SIGHUP, rolls back on failure; disk use at DEBUG bounded by a 60 MB cap
Why both ConfigMap and NATS? ConfigMap alone: kubelet syncs the mount only after roughly 60 s (violates < 2 s) and change notification is unreliable. NATS alone: a pod that was down, or a NATS restart, misses the message, and a pod restart falls back to ERROR. ConfigMap gives durability, NATS gives speed. An rsyslog-only option was rejected because the application bitmask drops DEBUG lines before rsyslog ever sees them.

Real case. A study traced every hop with a browser-automation run of the GUI and source reading. It found three paths instead of the assumed two, a GUI section with no editor, and that the "Debug" menu could not be reused at all (different pod, language, transport and target). Lesson: before trusting a missing log line as evidence, prove the level actually reached the process that should have printed it.
Interview Q&A
Q: A colleague toggled "Debug" in the GUI and expected DEBUG logs from RRC. Why did nothing change?
A: That page is Kernel Trace Configuration: it starts ftrace/trace-cmd in the MDT pod via ControlPath. App log levels are a per-process bitmask in the BBU UniTasks, set only via the backchannel config or the CWS sysLogLevelSet command.
Q: How does a UniTask decide whether to print a DEBUG line, and why is it cheap?
A: Each process keeps moduleBitArray[level][module]. Setting a module to level L sets its bit in every row up to L and clears the rest; UNILOG_ALLOW tests one bit before any formatting, so disabled lines cost almost nothing.
Q: Why might a level set through the EMS take minutes to appear?
A: It is store-and-forward: the SMO queues the change and the BBU only picks it up on its next heartbeat poll; then CfgMgr builds a LogConfigMessage and SysMgr broadcasts it. Check the backchannel queue endpoint for pending items.
Q: Why does /v1/route/bbu/sysLogLevelSet through the gateway fail?
A: ControlPath forwards /v1/route/<svc>/... to the pod's CpMgr hook server on 60080, which only has /ping, /version, /v1/rrh and /v1/spectrum hooks. sysLogLevelSet lives on the separate CWS web server on port 80.
Q: Design a runtime log-level control that survives restarts and acts in under 2 s.
A: Persist the desired level per pod in a ConfigMap (read at start-up), publish it on NATS for immediate application by the pod's SysMgr with ack/status replies, front it with the authenticated gateway, and let the rsyslog sidecar apply a validated, rollback-safe severity filter with a disk cap.
Addition - the log line itself: anatomy, build-time IDs, filter cost and where lines get lost
The control paths (GUI backchannel, direct level set, kernel trace) decide whether a line is allowed. This block is about the line itself - what it is made of and every place it can disappear on the way to Kibana.

Anatomy of one line. [logId lvl module task:inst file:line] message. It is formatted into a 2 KB buffer on the stack (no heap allocation on the hot path), so very long messages are silently truncated.

Every token is generated at build time. Each component has *Log.py definition files (entries of the form [name, format, level]) and a module registry that gives each module an ID range (e.g. SYSMGR 6000-6999). A code generator turns about 30 definition files into 113 *Log.h headers. Debug builds carry the full format text; release builds send parameters only, so decoding a release-build log needs the reverse-lookup table generated with that build.

Filtering is one bit test (~5 ns) on a per-thread handle (module x level). A forced log (opts=1) or anything from the SYSTEM module always passes. In cloud mode a per-level flood rate limiter sits behind the filter, so under a log storm lines can vanish even at the right level. Per task and per level there are counters - attempted, sent to the sink, filtered - which let you prove a line was filtered rather than lost.

Transport is fire-and-forget. libc syslog() is overridden at link time by liblogfaf, which turns each call into a UDP sendto() to port 514 - no error check, lock-free and crash-safe (a dying process still gets its last lines out). From there:
task -> uniLog filter -> liblogfaf (UDP :514) -> rsyslog -> /var/log/messages
     -> Filebeat -> Kafka -> Logstash -> Elasticsearch -> Kibana
Line missing in Kibana - possible causeHow to tell
never emitted (code path not taken)attempted counter did not move
filtered by module/levelattempted moved, filtered moved
flood-limited (cloud mode)other lines of the same level also thin out during a burst
lost UDP datagramsink counter moved but the line is absent from /var/log/messages
truncatedline present but cut at ~2 KB
pipeline lag or drop after rsyslogpresent in /var/log/messages, absent or late in Kibana
Level names are short codes in the direct call, e.g. sysLogLevelSet?module=CFGMGR&level=TRC (TRC = level 8); a change made in the GUI arrives with the next heartbeat (about 60 s cycle), so wait a minute before deciding it "did not work". The log-config message sent to the PHY processor is a packed struct with no version field - that is why log control on that processor cannot change without a firmware change on both ends.

Newer per-pod severity API. A ControlPath endpoint POST/GET /v1/logconfig/{pod} takes severities debug / info / warning / error for an allow-list of 17 pods (bbu, the DU and PHY nodes, ptpmgr, fronthaul server, netcon, icc, xdbsrvr and others). It patches a per-pod rsyslog-severity-<pod> ConfigMap; kubelet projects the change into the rsyslog sidecar in about 60 s and the sidecar applies it with a controlled restart. This filters at rsyslog, not in the task - the task-level counters still show the lines as sent. A GUI front end (through the SMO deployment manager, mTLS to the BBU OAM address) was designed but not yet built at the time of writing.
GMDTLOG01 vBBU log collection (GMDT) end to end - logtype bitmask, request classification, per-symptom log kit and the "log-collection storm" RCA Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
When something goes wrong on a vBBU, nobody wants to log into ten pods by hand. GMDT is the platform's log-collection service: a request (from the operator via ACS, from a crash, from an error handler) reaches the platform manager, gets classified, and the GMDT server fans it out to every relevant pod. Each pod tars its logs and copies the archive to the admin/SMO side. That automation is great - until something triggers it every five minutes. This card explains the request path and its bitmask, lists what to collect by hand for typical platform symptoms, and walks through a real "log-collection storm" that exhausted admin-server memory, including the investigation method that found the true trigger.
GMDT log-request path
One request can produce about ten archives; a trigger that repeats every few minutes multiplies that.
Level 1 - Core facts: logtype bits, classification and fan-out

Every log request is an upload-logs IPC to the platform manager carrying a logtype bitmask:

Bit nameValueContent
STACK1Stack logs
PHY2PHY logs
SYSTEM4System/platform logs
GMDT_CRASH8Crash with core files
CUSTOM16Custom log set
ALL7STACK | PHY | SYSTEM (note: not including crash or custom)

The platform manager then classifies the request, first match wins:

ConditionRequest type
Request came from ACSUSER
CRASH bit (8) setCRASH
CUSTOM bit (16) setCUSTOM
anything else, e.g. 0x5 or 0x7INTERNAL (default fall-through)

For a vNode request the GMDT server looks up its request matrix and fans out to about ten pod types. Known producers:

TriggerResult
5-minute core-check timerCRASH only if core files exist; otherwise a harmless "not uploading zlogs here" log line every 5 min
Cell crash / OAM crashlogtype 0xD (STACK|SYSTEM|CRASH) → CRASH
RF error, 5G stack errorALL → INTERNAL
PHY manager on SIGUSR2INTERNAL directly
Real-time watchdog sees the platform manager dieSYSTEM only
Operator request through ACSUSER
Cloud unreachable → reachablerestarts the core-check timer and auto-uploads pending cores (CRASH); does not create INTERNAL
4G MIB subscription on the "zlogs upload" attributeSTACK|SYSTEM (0x5) → INTERNAL - the storm trigger below
Level 2 - What to collect per symptom, and how to test collection

Automatic GMDT bundles are a start, but platform engineers usually ask for a specific kit per symptom. Collect it at the time of the fault, before a reboot rotates logs.

SymptomCollect
Any platform issuehost pod log tree, BBU XDB config XML, describe pods + describe nodes
Out of sync (PTP)the above + PTP manager config from host and from inside the ptp pod (see DUOPS01 for timing bring-up)
Hung / rebooting nodethe above + per-boot kernel "killed process" lines + dmesg
Something the SMO did (config push, reboot request)ACS logs from Kibana: Discover, index pattern logs-filebeat-smo*, filter auditInfo.appname.keyword: "ACS"
# any platform issue
tar czf platform_logs.tgz /var/log/pw-share/pods/ /opt/pw-config/xdbsrv/BBU-Val.xml
kubectl -n pw describe pods  > describe_pods.txt
kubectl -n pw describe nodes > describe_nodes.txt

# OUT_OF_SYNC (timing): add PTP manager config from host and from inside the ptp pod
cat /opt/pw-share/pods/ptpmanager/config
kubectl -n pw exec -it <ptp-pod> -- cat /opt/ptp_manager/ptp_manager.cfg

# HUNG node: OOM-killer evidence from every boot, then dmesg
journalctl --list-boots | awk '{print $1}' | xargs -I{} \
  journalctl --utc --no-pager -b {} -kqg 'killed process' -o verbose --output-fields=MESSAGE
dmesg > dmesg.txt

When testing log collection itself: trigger one request of each type (ACS, crash, SIGUSR2 to PHY manager) and verify the archive count and content per pod; during a long soak, count archives per hour on the admin server - it should be near zero on a healthy system. See K8SVBBU01 for the pod log layout.

Level 3 - Real case: the log-collection storm and how the trigger was found

The storm. On one 4G server the admin side kept receiving ..._vnode01_internal archives. Investigation went in three passes:

  1. Rule out the obvious timer. A 5-minute core-check timer existed, so it was the first suspect. Reading the code showed it only ever creates CRASH requests (when cores exist) or logs a harmless line. Cloud reachable/unreachable transitions were also ruled out.
  2. Map every producer. A table of every call site → logtype → trigger (Level 1) showed only one path producing INTERNAL with 0x5: the OAM manager's subscription to a "zlogs upload" MIB attribute (ID 39) - whenever the stack wrote a value > 0, it requested STACK|SYSTEM logs, with no rate limit.
  3. Trace backwards to the writer. Only one writer of that attribute existed: a hard-coded "continuous zlogs upload" timer inside the third-party LTE stack binary, firing about every 330 s with an incrementing millisecond timestamp, enabled at start-up, with no config knob, and firing even with cells not transmitting.
Measured impactValue
Total collections in ~15 days530
Of those STACK|SYSTEM via the MIB subscription500 (94 %)
From PHY SIGUSR23
OAM-manager reboot requests in the persistent log137
vNode pod restarts7 or more
Effectadmin-server memory exhaustion

With ~10 pods each producing tens to hundreds of MB, one cycle meant roughly 0.5-2 GB, and a new cycle started every 5.5 min whether the previous one had finished or not.

Regression or not? The first write-up called it a stack regression. Bisecting the git history of the handler showed it had never changed and never had rate limiting; the timer had been present for several releases. The behaviour was always there, masked by shorter uptimes and smaller logs. Lesson: check uptime and data volume before declaring a regression.

Fix options, ranked: (1) a cooldown (e.g. 30 min) in the subscription handler - about ten lines, low risk, primary fix; (2) rate-limit INTERNAL requests per source pod at the GMDT server or the platform manager - defence in depth; (3) do not throttle the shared send function, because crash uploads use it too; (4) the real fix: ask the stack vendor for a config switch or a much longer interval.

Interview Q&A
Q: A log request arrives with logtype 0x5. What type does the platform manager assign and why?
A: 0x5 is STACK|SYSTEM. It is not from ACS, has neither the CRASH (8) nor the CUSTOM (16) bit, so it falls through to INTERNAL.
Q: Why can a frequent log trigger take down the admin server rather than the BBU?
A: Each request fans out to about ten pods, each sending an archive to the admin side. At one request every 5.5 min that is hundreds of archives a day and gigabytes per hour landing on one server, filling its memory and disk.
Q: There is an obvious 5-minute timer in the code. Why not blame it?
A: Because the archive type did not match: that timer only creates CRASH requests when cores exist. Building a table of every producer and the type it creates is what pointed to the real trigger.
Q: Where would you add rate limiting and where not?
A: At the specific trigger (the subscription handler), with a cooldown, and optionally at the GMDT server per source pod. Not in the common send function, because that would also delay crash-dump uploads you really need.
Q: A node is hung. Which evidence do you collect?
A: The pod log tree, describe pods/nodes, and for every boot in journalctl the kernel lines containing "killed process" (OOM killer), plus dmesg.
PMSHM01 How eNB/gNB PM Counters Are Really Produced — shared-memory slots, SUM+COUNT means, interval deltas and the TS 32.435 file Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
Every KPI you read off an eNB or gNB — RRC setup success, PRB utilisation, DRB throughput, SDU delay — starts life as a number that some stack thread bumps while it handles traffic. Somebody then has to collect those numbers once per reporting interval (typically 15 minutes), turn them into a value and write the standard TS 32.435 XML file that the EMS/SMO picks up. This card explains how that chain works in a design where stack threads write counters into shared memory (SHM) and one RAT-blind PM daemon reads them and emits the file for 4G, 5G and NB-IoT alike.

The key insight is simple but easy to miss when you are validating KPIs: a percentage is not additive, but the raw sums behind it are. "47 % PRB usage" cannot be added or averaged across intervals; "sum of PRBs used" and "number of TTIs" can. So the counters that matter are stored as SUM + COUNT pairs and divided only at report time. Once you understand this, many "the KPI looks wrong" bugs become easy to explain: the SUM and the COUNT came from different intervals, or two readers both reset the same counter.

For the meaning of the counter families themselves see LF28 (eNB counters) and NS34 (5G counters, TS 28.552); for the formulas built on top of them see NT6; for O-RU-side PM files see CM21.
Figure — counter path from stack thread to PM file (own drawing)
Writers only add; the daemon only reads. The descriptor is the single source of truth for what each slot means. Northbound transport (TR-069 upload, O1) is unchanged.
Level 1 — counter kinds, descriptor and addressing
  • Two kinds of counter. In 4G roughly 30 % are event counts (RRC attempts, handovers, bearer setups: +1 when the event happens). About 70 % are performance measurements (throughput, PRB usage, delay, drop rate) that the layers used to compute over a window when polled.
  • The unlock: MAC already accumulates sum_PRB and a TTI count every TTI; RLC/PDCP accumulate byte_sum, delay_sum and a packet count on every PDU. Storing those additive pieces lets a generic daemon produce the ratio itself.
  • Floats never go into atomics. The producer pre-multiplies by a fixed-point scale (1000, 1e6 ...) and stores an integer; the daemon divides at emit time.
  • Generated descriptor (.pmd). Produced from the stack's counter XML by the existing code generator, committed and shipped in the image, never hand-edited. It lists segments, the slot base/span of each KPI, its scope, logical type, scale, export target and the 3GPP measType name.
  • Layout hash. A 64-bit digest of the ordered layout is written into each segment header; a producer refuses to attach if its hash differs — a stale-build guard that stops a new stack writing into an old layout.
logical typeslotswriter doesdaemon reportsexample
count1+1 per eventinterval countRRC connection attempts
sum1+v per eventinterval sumbytes (paired with a time slot)
mean2 (SUM + CNT)+v on SUM, +1 on CNTSUM / CNTRRU.PrbUsedDl.Avg, DRB.RlcSduDelayDl
rate2 (SUM + TIME)+bytes, +ticksSUM / TIMEDRB.ThpVolDl (throughput)
gauge1store current valueload, never resetinstantaneous level
scopeidentityslot indexspan (4G)
ENBthe eNB itselfslot = base1
CELLcell id 1..4slot = base + cellIdx (static)4 cells
PLMNMCC-MNCslot = base + cellIdx×6 + plmnIdx (assigned on first sight)6 per cell = 24 per eNB
A mean/rate counter reserves a SUM block and a COUNT/TIME block: SUM at base+i, COUNT at base+(cnt/2)+i. Per-QCI counters are separate KPI ids (..._QCI1 etc.), not a runtime dimension. PLMN scope needs the identity table so the file shows the real PLMN=<mcc-mnc>; the same table is the list of active objects the daemon iterates.
Level 2 — interval models, the output file and how to validate it
Interval models — how "this 15 minutes" is cut out of a running counter. Three options were compared; the one you are testing changes which bugs are possible.
modelhow it worksstrengthweakness
Reset-on-readdaemon does an atomic exchange of each slot with 0lossless, no delta maths, no wraparounddaemon needs write access to SHM; only ONE reader may exist
Two banks in SHM, reader flipswriters use the active bank; reader flips and zeroes the old onereader controls the boundarya writer that latched the old bank can land +1 between read and zero → event lost; 2× memory
One cumulative bank, sampled (recommended)writers only add; daemon copies slots into private cur, reports cur − prev, swapsdaemon never writes SHM, no event loss (a late add shows up next interval), any number of readerslayers need a never-reset accumulator (MAC has one; RLC/PDCP need a small shadow)
WRITER (stack thread)      fetch_add(slot[i], v)            # never resets
DAEMON (each interval)     s1 = load(commit_seq)
                           cur[i] = relaxed_load(slot[i])   # for all i
                           s2 = load(commit_seq); if s1 != s2: retry
                           report cur - prev   (gauges: report cur)
                           swap(prev, cur)
What the emitted file looks like. One measCollecFile per interval: a file header (format version 32.435 V9.0, vendor, element type), a collection begin time, then one measInfo block per scope. Inside it the measType entries carry the 3GPP names (e.g. RRU.PrbUsedDl.Avg), each measValue has an LDN such as ...,ENBCell=1 or ...,ENBCell=1,PLMN=<mcc-mnc>, and the r values are positional. A CSV twin is produced from the same data (4G previously had none). The legacy 4G dialect is reproduced byte for byte so downstream parsers do not notice.

How to test it — parity diff. The migration runs in modes, and each mode is a test opportunity:
modebehaviourwhat you check
offlegacy PM onlybaseline files
shadowboth run, legacy file ships, new file is diffedparity diff of the two files for the same interval: same measTypes, same LDNs, same values
activenew file ships, legacy kept as safety netEMS ingestion, CSV present, instant rollback by flag
exclusivelegacy removedmemory drop, no missing counters
  • Diff per measType and per object, not whole-file text: ordering of measInfo blocks can legitimately differ.
  • Drive a known load (fixed iperf rate, fixed number of attaches) for exactly one interval and check counts and means against what you injected.
  • Add/remove a PLMN mid-run and confirm the new PLMN gets its own LDN with the real MCC-MNC rather than an index.
  • Check /dev/shm on the target has at least ~4 MB free before enabling the feature.
Level 3 — sizes, crash behaviour and the pitfalls behind "wrong" KPIs
Sizes worth remembering.
item4G5G
counters14,299 KPIs after expansion (CELL 71 %, PLMN 26 %, ENB 3 %)~380 (CU 284, DU 96)
segmentsone per writer family: MAC, RLC, PDCP, signallingtwo: CU process (RRC/PDCP/GTP threads) and DU process (MAC/RLC/PHY threads)
SHM~106,000 slots ≈ 0.85 MB (ceiling < 3 MB)small
daemon~6 MB RSS steady, ~16 MB peaksame daemon
counter stylelayer accumulatorsmix of 256 cumulative and 18 reset-style counters
Crash and upgrade behaviour. The daemon creates and owns each segment; producers only attach. A POSIX SHM object survives either process crashing. After a daemon restart it adopts the segment (checks magic + layout hash) and never zeroes it; the interval spanning the gap is flagged suspect. If the stack dies, the daemon notices via the producer pid / heartbeat timestamp / attach generation in the header and marks those counters suspect instead of reporting frozen values. On an upgrade that changes the layout the hash mismatches, the producer refuses to attach and the segment is recreated at the new size. A reboot clears tmpfs, so whether the EMS expects back-filled intervals is a real open question to test.

Pitfalls when a KPI looks wrong:
  • SUM and COUNT from different intervals. If a mean is computed from a SUM sampled before the boundary and a COUNT sampled after, you get impossible values (PRB usage > 100 %, negative delay). The sequence counter (commit_seq) exists to stop this.
  • Two consumers resetting the same counter. With reset-on-read, a second reader (a debug CLI, the legacy collector) silently steals counts; both outputs come out low and their sum is right.
  • Ratio computed per object then averaged. Averaging four cells' PRB % is not the eNB PRB %; sum the SUMs and COUNTs first.
  • Stale build. A producer built against an older counter XML must be rejected by the layout hash; if counters appear shifted by one KPI, suspect a guard that was bypassed.
  • Frozen values after a stack crash. A cumulative counter that stops growing produces zeros, not errors — look for the suspect flag or the gap in attach generation.
Real lesson from the design review: the first design assumed every counter was a simple tally and only fitted ~30 % of 4G counters; it worked once the team stopped tapping the computed ratio that layers hand out on poll and tapped the raw accumulators behind it. The staged rollout (first let the existing KPI manager write its assembled interval set into SHM, then move writes down into the datapath one family at a time behind a flag and a performance gate) is a good template for any risky change on the real-time path.
Interview Q&A
Q: Why can a PM daemon not just store "PRB usage = 47 %" each TTI and average it?
A: A percentage is not additive: averaging per-TTI or per-cell ratios weights them wrongly. Store the additive pieces (sum of PRBs, number of TTIs) and divide once at report time.
Q: What is the difference between a mean and a rate counter?
A: Both use two slots. A mean is SUM / COUNT (e.g. average SDU delay = delay_sum / packets). A rate is SUM / TIME (bytes over active time ticks = throughput).
Q: Why sample a cumulative counter instead of resetting it on read?
A: The reader never writes shared memory, so it cannot corrupt counters; there is no read-then-zero race, so no event is lost (a late increment lands in the next delta); and several readers can sample at once.
Q: A layout hash mismatch is reported at stack start. What does it mean?
A: The producer was built against a different counter descriptor than the one the daemon uses. Attaching would put counters in the wrong slots, so it refuses; rebuild or align the image.
Q: How would you prove a new PM pipeline is correct before it replaces the old one?
A: Run both in shadow mode and diff the two files for the same interval per measType and per object, with a controlled known load, then check PLMN/cell LDNs and the CSV twin.
RANCIDBG01 From a Red CI Run to a Root Cause — where each vBBU log lives and the broken-vs-healthy signature for platform, RRH/fronthaul, call, throughput and test-rig faults (2G/4G/5G) Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
A red CI run on a vBBU (2G, 4G and 5G stacks in Kubernetes pods driving real RRHs and real phones) is only a symptom. The test harness tells you which step failed; it rarely tells you why. This card is a map: for each kind of failure, which log file to open, what to grep for, and what the broken value looks like next to the healthy one. Every case below comes from real investigations on a multi-RAT CI farm. The big lesson: a large share of red results are not product bugs at all (bad cable, broken GPS antenna, a NUC that is powered off, a harness that picked the wrong IP), and some green results are fake.
Five layers to triage a red run
First decide the layer, then open that layer's log. The right-hand column lists where the evidence lives.
Level 1 — where every log lives
Memorise this map; most time in triage is lost opening the wrong file.
SourcePathWhat it holds

Level 3 — test-rig faults, red herrings and habits
Rig faults that look like product faults
SymptomReal cause
attenuator clients = None, Errno 113UE-controller NUC powered off (check boot list)
attenuator clients = None, timeoutusb0 took the default route on the NUC (journal: "as default for IPv4")
UE "attach failed" in ~100 msUSB port re-enumerating every ~30 s with error -71 = bad cable/port; if lsusb shows only root hubs the whole xHCI controller died → unbind/bind (now automated)
2G UE never attachesBTS RACH log: zero RACH at that UE's usual level while a peer UE RACHes normally and SI is unchanged → phone fault
pcap "N/3 messages missing", call passedtwo setups sniff the same shared pod and kill each other's tcpdump (same-second kill -s 15)
config check live 7 != configured 9two EMS POSTs a few hundred ms apart both return 200 but only the first is applied; 10 s apart both work → send all params in one POST (CLD-29114)
Red herrings already ruled out
  • 2G "XML parser" error with centre frequency 0 — triggered by a read-only ACS poll (GetParameterValues only).
  • DL PA failure and DL FIFO alarms during a GPS or link outage — symptoms, they clear themselves.
  • A RACH threshold value that is identical in passing and failing builds.
  • "All IPsec tunnels active" — proves IKE only; check VPP SA/SPD counters for ESP return traffic.
  • iperf "did not receive ack of last datagram" — also present in passing 2G UDP tests.
Habits that make the evidence trustworthy
  • Compare against a known-good peer: sibling RRH, another vnode, rotated logs from when it worked.
  • Align time zones before building a timeline — Jenkins mail, NUC journal and pod logs can all differ by hours, and the BTS log lags 4–10 s.
  • Count, don't eyeball: grep -c on good and bad runs turns "I think" into "211 vs 0".
  • Don't stop at the first match; bootstrap noise appears in healthy runs too.
  • Check the test itself: a PASSED step can be vacuous (see the "stale green" ideas in NBCI01).
# quick first pass on a red 2G/4G run (inside the right pod)
ls /var/run/pw/ | grep pw-startup_done
grep -c STARTED /opt/pw/pw-startup2.log; grep -c FINISHED /opt/pw/pw-startup2.log
grep -nE "EAL:|hugepage" /var/log/pw-share/pods/<pod>/<node>/messages
grep -c "<serial>.*RRH_DEVICE_STATUS" /var/log/messages
ethtool -S <fh-if> | grep -E "remote_faults|crc"; sleep 20; ethtool -S <fh-if> | grep remote_faults
ss -naup | egrep ":4801|:4802"   # run twice; Recv-Q must stay 0
Interview Q&A
Q: kubectl logs for a crashing PHY pod only shows "Aborted (core dumped)". Where do you look next?
A: The pod's own messages file on the host under /var/log/pw-share/pods/<pod>/<node>/. In one case it showed "EAL: FATAL: Cannot get hugepage information" — the ICC and VPP pods had used all the boot-time hugepages.
Q: A 4G cell is not transmitting and the RRH looks "not ready". How do you tell a GPS problem from an RRH problem?
A: Read the GPS block in the per-RRH log: non-zero GPS status, signal 0 and a 1980 date, plus "No GPS signal, S-Plane out-of-sync" in fhsrv and a climbing tp_delta. An RRH-ready fault has no RRH_DEVICE_STATUS lines at all.
Q: How do you prove a flapping fronthaul link is the fibre, not the BBU port?
A: Watch mac_remote_faults and CRC counters and the optical Rx power (-11 dBm vs ~-2 dBm healthy), then move the SFP and fibre to another port. The fault moved with them, so it was the link.
Q: An LTE iperf test reports 0.19 Mbps. What non-RAN cause did you find?
A: The harness picked the UE's IMS PDN address (QCI 5, AMBR 200/96 kbps) because it read the first line of ip route. It was an automation defect.
Q: Name two ways a CI run can be green but meaningless.
A: Throughput "Average 0.0, Expected 0.0 → PASS"; and an upgrade step reporting PASSED while a pod is in ImagePullBackOff. Always check counters or pod state, not just the verdict.
PKIBH01 Certificates for backhaul IPsec — factory vs operational certificate, priming tunnel, SCEP vs CMPv2 enrolment, renewal threshold and OCSP/CRL revocation that tears tunnels down Debug click to expand ▾
In plain English
Backhaul IPsec (see IPSEC01) is only as good as the certificates behind it. In an operator network the radio node and the gateway authenticate each other with X.509 certificates issued by the operator's CA, and those certificates must be enrolled, renewed before they expire and checked for revocation – automatically, for thousands of nodes. That is what the PKI function does.

The classic trap is a chicken-and-egg problem. A brand-new small cell (an access node, “CWS” on the HNG) needs an operator certificate, but it is only allowed to talk to the operator network through an IPsec tunnel – which needs a certificate. The answer is a factory certificate installed in the depot, signed by the vendor CA. With it the node brings up a restricted priming tunnel to a matching “factory endpoint” on the gateway, enrols for its operational certificate through that tunnel, then switches to the full operational tunnel.

This card walks through that lifecycle on the HNG gateway and the access node, the profile knobs (SCEP vs CMPv2, key size, renewal threshold, OCSP/CRL), what happens on revocation, and how to debug a node stuck in factory mode. M-plane TLS/SSH certificates on the RU are a different topic (RUSEC01); installing a certificate bundle on the vBBU is CM39; HNG basics are in HNG01.
Certificate bootstrap and lifecycle (own drawing)
Steps 1–3 happen once per node; step 4 runs every responder interval for every connected node.
Level 1 — core facts
ItemWhat to know
Factory certificateIssued by the vendor CA in the depot; lets the node build the priming tunnel. The gateway needs its own vendor-CA certificate to run the factory endpoint, otherwise the two sides are not in the same trust domain.
Operational certificateIssued by the operator CA; used for the full-service tunnel with all control- and user-plane traffic selectors.
DomainsThe gateway runs one IPsec service per domain (factory and operational). Which one a peer reaches is decided by subjectAltName DNS entries in the certificates and the certificate-profile matching them.
Enrolment protocolsSCEP and CMPv2 (both supported on the gateway and the node, historically SCEP for the gateway and CMPv2 for nodes), plus a manual “terminal” mode: generate a PKCS#10 request from the CLI, have the CA sign it, import CA and EE certs by paste.
Certificate profileProtocol + server URL + authentication (out-of-band reference/secret, or in-band RSA with a vendor certificate), DN, RSA key size, SAN, optional validity, CA-certificate fingerprint, renewal-threshold (% of lifetime, 50–80 typical).
ResponderOCSP, RTCS or CRL, poll interval in seconds, CRL fallback when OCSP fails, strict-CRL policy.
RenewalAt the threshold the node requests a new certificate: CMPv2 cr (certification request, signed with the current key) or kur (key update request, disabled by default; answered by kup).
Key sizesThe node accepts 512/1024/2048/4096, but the CMP client library sometimes failed to generate 4096-bit keys, so CMP profiles are limited to 2048. SCEP can use 4096.
Golden rule: enable NTP on both the gateway and the nodes before anything else. Certificate validity windows, OCSP responses and renewal timers all assume correct time; a node that booted with a wrong clock fails “mysteriously” at enrolment or at IKE.
Level 2 — configure, inspect and test
Order of configuration on the gateway. (1) a PKI profile under profiles pki <name> (provider, CA URL and authentication, DN, key, SAN, responder); (2) certificates use-pki true (PKI is off by default) and a pki ipsec instance naming the factory domain, the operational domain, the profile and a common name; (3) for each access node an accessnodes cws <node> certificates pki ipsec entry pointing at a node profile. If the gateway sends CA traffic from a dedicated interface, configure the virtual network first, then a source-based policy route towards the CA, then the PKI instance with that floating address as bind address – these cannot be changed afterwards. CA and OCSP service must sit on the same address.

Where the files live (gateway and node):
PathContent
/etc/cert/ipsec/factory/factory CA, certificate, key, PKCS#12 bundle
/etc/cert/ipsec/cert0/ (cert1/)operational certificate(s) – delete cert0 and the node falls back to factory mode on the next reboot
/etc/cert/ipsec/vendor/certificate used for in-band (RSA-signed) CMP authentication; its DN must match the profile DN in the same attribute order
/etc/cert/CA certificates; strongSwan reloads CAs on every certificate event
# what certificates does the gateway hold / monitor?
show certificates pki ipsec operational ca|ee
show certificates pki ipsec factory ca|ee
show certificates pki ipsec monitored list        # serial, CN, validity of every peer cert
show certificates pki statistics                   # provider + responder success/failure counters

# on the node / any Linux box: read the files directly
openssl x509 -in /etc/cert/ipsec/cert0/cert.crt -noout -text
openssl x509 -in /etc/cert/ipsec/cert0/cert.crt -noout -dates ; date
openssl x509 -in ca.crt -noout -fingerprint -md5   # must equal the profile fingerprint
iptables -t nat -nvL                               # NAT rules towards the CA on the gateway
Statistics to read. Provider counters: working (initial) success/failure and replace (renewal) success/failure with timestamps. Responder counters: status-check success/failure and CRL download success/failure. On the node the same provider statistics come from the certificate manager's debug client (FSM state should end in CERT_DONE).

Alarms/events to expect in a test: CERT_FETCH_FAILURE (CA down, service not running, wrong authentication) · CERT_WORKING_REVAL_ENTER (renewal window reached) · CERT_WORKING_PUBLISHED (new cert handed to the IPsec manager) · CERT_WORKING_EXPIRED (renewals kept failing) · CERT_WORKING_FETCH_REQUEST (manual enrolment pending) · CERT_STATUS_FAILURE per monitored certificate · on the node CERT_FACTORY_TUNNEL_REQUEST at boot with factory cert only.

Test matrix worth running: first boot with factory cert only; reboot with a valid operational cert (must go straight to the operational tunnel); renewal at threshold (shorten validity on the CA to minutes); expired certificate; revoked certificate; OCSP unreachable with and without CRL fallback; wrong CA fingerprint; CMP with each key size; clock skew.
Level 3 — mechanics, pitfalls and recovery
NAT for enrolment. During the priming phase the node can only reach the gateway, so the gateway NATs everything addressed to the CA. Because the DPDK fast path had no NAT module, both SNAT and DNAT rules (plus a packet mark and a policy-routing rule) are installed in the Linux path. If initial enrolment fails with zero requests sent, check TCP reachability to the CA port first, then these NAT rules.

Revocation monitoring. The gateway's certificate manager learns each peer certificate from strongSwan over the VICI interface and re-checks every certificate once per interval seconds using the OCSP/CRL location in the certificate or in the responder config. The CA product used in the lab supported OCSP and CRL but not RTCS. Status REVOKED → the IPsec manager tears the tunnel down. The node retries IKE, strongSwan re-checks OCSP, gets REVOKED again – the node is stuck. The only recovery is to delete the revoked operational certificate (cert0) on the node so it re-enrols through the factory path. Note the node itself did not monitor the gateway's certificate.

Common configuration traps.
  • The CA-certificate URL in the CLI must be double-quoted, otherwise characters such as ? are truncated and the URL is invalid.
  • The CA fingerprint type defaults to MD5; it must match the CA certificate actually installed.
  • In-band RSA authentication fails if the DN attributes are in a different order from the vendor certificate (“CN first” vs “CN last” are different DNs to the CA).
  • A factory-certificate import via the CLI broke after an OS upgrade removed bzip2 – the workaround was copying the extracted files into the factory folder by hand.
  • Strongswan caches certificates, CRLs and OCSP responses: after changing CA material, purge the caches (hidden engineering CLI) or results look stale.
Escape hatches in a lab (never in the field): to force a node back to factory mode remove the operational directories and the operational CA link, then reboot; to bypass PKI, point the node's top-level certificate links at the factory files and disable PKI in its config. If PKI stays configured on the gateway it will still accept the tunnel but stops OCSP monitoring of that node.

Debug order for “tunnel never reaches operational”: time sync → factory tunnel up? → provider statistics (fetch failures) → CA reachable through the gateway NAT → authentication (reference/secret or vendor cert + DN order) → certificate written to cert0 → operational IKE with the operational-domain SAN → responder status not REVOKED/failed.
Interview Q&A
Q: Why does a new small cell need a factory certificate at all?
A: All its traffic, including enrolment, must go through IPsec to the gateway, and IPsec needs a certificate. The vendor-signed factory certificate builds a restricted priming tunnel, through which the node enrols for its operator certificate; then it switches to the full operational tunnel.
Q: What is the difference between CMPv2 cr and kur?
A: Both renew a certificate before expiry. cr is a certification request for a new certificate, typically signed with the existing key; kur is a key update request, asking the CA to certify a new key pair to replace the old one, answered with kup. kur was disabled by default.
Q: A node's certificate is revoked. What happens and how do you recover it?
A: The gateway's OCSP/CRL check returns REVOKED and the IPsec manager tears the tunnel down. Every IKE retry fails the same check, so the node is stuck until its revoked operational certificate is deleted and it re-enrols through the factory path.
Q: What does the renewal threshold mean?
A: The percentage of certificate lifetime after which renewal starts, e.g. 80 means renew once 80 % of the validity has elapsed, leaving the remaining 20 % for retries before expiry. Entering this window raises an event; repeated failures end in an expired-certificate alarm.
Q: What do you check first when PKI enrolment fails in the lab?
A: Clock sync on both ends, then the provider failure counter and whether any request was sent at all (TCP reachability to the CA, NAT rules on the gateway), then authentication: secret/reference, or for in-band RSA the vendor certificate and DN order, and the CA fingerprint in the profile.
